# Owlshift — design & architecture

Status: draft, 2026-09-27. Companion documents: [scenarios](scenarios.md), [runtime & operations](runtime-and-operations.md), [roadmap](roadmap.md), [build plan](build-plan.md), [visual identity](visual-identity.md).

Owlshift is an open-source runner that works a team's existing backlog continuously with coding agents, and brings a human in only where a decision is theirs, on the ticket, in the tracker they already use.

## 1. Purpose & positioning

The bottleneck of agentic development is no longer writing code, it is human attention: questions buried in terminals, work that waits because nobody noticed a blocker cleared, parallel agents colliding on the same files, sessions a person has to babysit.

**What it is.** A runner that:

1. pulls ready tickets in priority order, respecting dependencies and conflicts with all work in flight, human work included;
2. takes each ticket through a multi-stage pipeline (intake, design, review, build, verify, deliver, watch), each stage on the harness and model that fits it;
3. stops and asks on the ticket whenever a decision belongs to a human, checks that the answer actually answers, and resumes, as many rounds as needed;
4. delivers a verified pull request. Humans merge, unless the project opted into merge on green, where the runner merges a pull request it follows once it is green (D14, section 5, "Merge on green").

**What it is not.** Not an IDE, a chat or a new board: the team's tracker stays the interface. Not an auto-merger by default: merge on green is off until a project turns it on, and no agent ever merges. Not a hosted service: it runs on a developer's machine or a team's server.

**Landscape, as of 2026-09-27.** Ticket-to-agent orchestration became a category in 2026. The two closest projects:

| Project | Shape | Human decisions mid-run | Blockers and conflicts | License |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| [Symphony](https://github.com/openai/symphony) (OpenAI) | A [spec](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/openai/symphony/main/SPEC.md) plus an Elixir reference implementation, engineering preview; drives the Codex app-server; in-memory state | A run "MUST NOT stall" on user input: each implementation auto-fails, surfaces to an operator or auto-approves; the run ends in a handoff state such as Human Review | `blocked_by` is passed to the prompt, not enforced by the scheduler; no conflict avoidance | Apache-2.0 |
| [Sortie](https://github.com/sortie-ai/sortie) | Go single binary with SQLite; GitHub, GitLab, Gitea, Linear, Jira; Claude Code, Codex, Copilot, OpenCode, Kiro, Gemini; a `WORKFLOW.md` per project ([docs](https://docs.sortie-ai.com/)) | A handoff state at the end of the run; no documented question loop during it | Not documented beyond isolated workspaces | Apache-2.0 |

Parallel-agent workspaces such as [Vibe Kanban](https://vibekanban.com/) manage agents from their own board, supervised by a person: a different lane.

**Where this product differs: three bets.**

1. **The human decision loop is the product.** Questions are posted on the ticket at any stage (before code, during it, after the PR), answers are verified before anything resumes, and there is no limit on rounds.
2. **Scheduling is enforced, not advisory.** Blockers gate dispatch. Declared resources (code zones, migration chains, the one browser) keep two in-flight tickets from colliding, whether an agent or a person holds them.
3. **A ticket is a pipeline, not a session.** Plan before code, independent review by fresh runs on another model (of another family when one is configured), each stage routed to the harness and model that fits it, cost measured per ticket.

Owlshift is built from scratch rather than on top of Sortie (decision D1, section 12).

## 2. Design principles

Nine rules; every later choice in this document derives from one of them.

1. **The tracker is the human interface, git is the machine's memory.** No new board to adopt. Everything a person needs to see or answer lives on the ticket; everything the runner needs to resume lives in the repository.
2. **No LLM sits idle, and none orchestrates.** Scheduling, state and policy are deterministic code. A model is called only to run a role, for as long as that role takes. At rest the runner costs zero tokens.
3. **An agent that needs a human stops.** It leaves a checkpoint, a question and its session's transcript, and once its questions are decided or answered the stage resumes that session, or a fresh run resumes from the checkpoint when the session cannot be (section 4, "Resuming the asking run's session", OWL-394). No process waits for the answer: the transcript waits on disk, and a resume a few minutes later reads it from the prompt cache.
4. **Ask as early as possible.** The dominant latency is the human's, so questions surface at intake, before a ticket can block anything.
5. **Silence is never consent; in doubt, ask again.** An unanswered or ambiguous reply never starts work.
6. **Nothing enters the work queue without a human gesture.** The runner only pulls tickets a person admitted; follow-ups it proposes wait in an inbox.
7. **Guardrails are enforced by the runner, not requested of the model.** Agents have no tracker write access and cannot merge, deploy or change state, by construction.
8. **Neutral by contract.** Tracker, forge, harness, model and stack are adapters behind capability contracts. A contract is frozen only after three implementations exist, one of them possibly on paper.
9. **Subscription first.** Owlshift drives the Claude and Codex CLIs on the user's own subscription: Codex through the login the user configured, Claude Code agent runs through a token of that subscription made with `claude setup-token` and stored by `owlshift init`, never the user's own Claude Code login. It never asks for, stores or passes a model API key, and the user's own Claude Code configuration does not reach an agent run: a Claude Code agent run is billed to the subscription through that token, and a Codex run on an API-key login is the one API-billed case expected ([runtime and operations](runtime-and-operations.md)).

## 3. Core model

Ten concepts, owned by the product and independent of any tracker, harness or stack.

| Concept | Definition | Lives in |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Ticket | A unit of work read from the tracker, admitted to the queue by a human gesture | Tracker |
| Pipeline | A declarative sequence of stages a ticket goes through; a project ships variants (`trivial`, `standard`, `risky`) | Project config |
| Stage | One step of a pipeline: a role to run, its entry condition, its exit contract, its gates | Project config |
| Role | What a stage executes: a prompt, required capabilities (browser, network), a permission level (read-only, write in worktree) | Product; a project adds rules to it or overrides it |
| Run | One execution of a role on one harness, in one worktree; returns a validated `result.json` and a measured cost | Runner, logged to git |
| Gate | A point where a human decision may be needed: clarification, arbitration or approval | Ticket (questions and answers) |
| Artifact | What a stage leaves for the next: plan, step ledger, review findings, delivery report | Git (dedicated refs) |
| Resource | Something two in-flight tickets cannot hold at once: a code zone, a migration chain, the browser, a port range | Declared at intake, held by claims |
| Policy | A non-configurable floor plus per-project rules: what may be decided without a human, budgets, caps | Product floor + project config |
| Adapter | The boundary to an outside system, declaring its capabilities: tracker, forge, harness, notifier, stack profile | Product (built-in) or plugin |

A project adopts the product with one committed file (the project config) plus one uncommitted file per developer (identity, installed harnesses, local caps).

## 4. Default pipeline & gates

A ticket moves through seven stages; four loop back, and five can stop at a gate where a human decides.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    intake[Intake] --> design[Design] --> review[Design review] --> build[Build] --> verify[Verify] --> deliver[Deliver] --> watch[Watch]
    review -- revise --> design
    build -- "replan (P5)" --> design
    verify -- fix --> build
    watch -- "review comments (P5)" --> build
    watch -- "red check: fix run · conflict: rebase run" --> watch
    gate{{"Gate: needs input<br/>questions on the ticket, answer verified,<br/>the asking run's session resumes,<br/>or a fresh run from the checkpoint"}}
    intake <-.-> gate
    design <-.-> gate
    review <-.-> gate
    build <-.-> gate
    watch <-.-> gate
```

Dashed links mark the stages that stop for a human in the default pipeline: Intake (clarification, a false premise), Design (an open point, a false premise), Design review (plan approval), Build (an open point, the plan invalidated) and Watch (a prerequisite, a reversed decision). A human merges after Watch, or, on a project that opted in, the runner does (section 5, "Merge on green", D14). Since OWL-377 a review finding is never a question by itself: it goes to the role that owns the work, so Design review stops only for the plan's approval and Verify never stops for a human (section 5, "A finding goes to its author"). Section 5, "The quality pipeline", sets the rules of Design, Design review and Verify (OWL-296).

| Stage | Does | Leaves behind |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Intake | Understands the ticket, checks its external premises live, declares resources, picks the variant and the routing | Routing record, resources, questions |
| Design | Writes the plan: approach, files, test strategy, risks, success criteria | Plan |
| Design review | Independent reviewers, on another model than the author's when the configuration names one; their number follows the variant (section 5, "The quality pipeline") | Findings, approval |
| Build | Implements step by step, one commit and one ledger entry per step; runs the checks that cover its change, while the runner runs the project's full gate (lint, formatter, tests) once on the commit it delivers (OWL-355) | Commits, ledger |
| Verify | Reviews the diff against the plan and criteria, on another model than the author's when the configuration names one, before the PR opens; the complete CI result is Watch's (OWL-296); browser QA when declared, a later step than P6 (roadmap, P6's readings) | Findings; a bounded fix loop |
| Deliver | The runner opens the PR and posts the delivery report on the ticket | PR, report |
| Watch | Reacts to CI regressions and merge conflicts with fix and rebase runs of its own, which keep the ticket at Watch (section 5, "Watch after delivery"); human review comments on the PR loop back to Build from P5; on a project that opted in, merges the pull request once it is green (section 5, "Merge on green") | Fix runs, rebases, gates, the merge |

**Variants.** `trivial` skips Design and Design review and uses one reviewer. `standard` is the diagram. `risky` adds reviewers and makes human plan approval mandatory. Intake picks the variant; a label on the ticket forces one. The roster of each is in section 5, "The quality pipeline" (OWL-296).

**Gate policy.**

- **Resolver first.** Every question a role raises goes through a resolver run, except those the runner sends straight to the decider (below). What is discoverable (the ticket, the repository, the docs, a verifiable fact) is decided and logged on the ticket as a reversible decision; the rest goes to a human. Decided on 2026-10-03 (OWL-138): the runner routes each question by its category, so an always-human one never reaches the resolver; the resolver is a read-only run, since OWL-344 with Bash inside the read-only confinement, whose network stays open, on the standard tier below delegation level `all` and the deep tier at `all` (section 9), so a verifiable fact is one it can check in the repository and its history or, since OWL-415, with its web tools on the web, cited with its source and date (section 8); each decision is a DECISION comment, kept in the ticket ref, from which alone a later run reads it, and the decider's word overrides it. When every question of a run is decided, the stage runs again without a stop, at most `policy.resolver_passes` runs in a row in one command (5 by default since OWL-328, below; 3 before) before the questions go to the decider, or, at `all` since OWL-375, are taken on the asking run's recommendation ("Resolver passes", below). No harness takes over from the resolver, P6's fallback being the review roles' alone (section 5, "Parallel", "Usage limits"), so a resolver that cannot give its result sends every question to the decider ([build plan](build-plan.md), "The resolver"). Decided on 2026-10-04 (OWL-144): a question the asking run files under the wrong category still reaches the resolver, so the resolver labels each question it decides with a category of its own, read from the question's text and context without seeing the asking run's, and the runner logs the decision only when neither label is always-human and the resolver's is a well-formed token; otherwise the question goes to the decider. The runner draws that consequence; recognising the topic stays a model's judgment, and what remains is plain: a floor-topic question both runs label as something else is still decided, reversible like any decision and listed in the delivery report; a project's own always-human categories are told to both runs since OWL-151 (decided 2026-10-04), in the brief's `always_human`, so each labels with them, but recognising a topic stays a model's judgment and a question on one both runs label otherwise is still decided; decisions kept before this check stay as they were. Decided on 2026-10-07 (OWL-176): the Build run takes no decision of its own. A choice of what the work delivers (its content, behaviour, interface or wording) that nothing above plainly settles, and every point the ticket leaves open, is a question, so the resolver decides what is discoverable and the floor applies; the runner refuses a Build result that lists `decisions` ([build plan](build-plan.md), "Build takes no decision of its own"). Decided on 2026-10-07 (OWL-186): the Build run told of that refusal may not end `done`, so it asks the refused choices or stops ([build plan](build-plan.md), "A retry after refused decisions asks"). Decided on 2026-10-08 (OWL-192): that hold and the refusal's reason are kept in the ticket ref until a Build run asks, so a park, a usage limit or a later command never drops them ([build plan](build-plan.md), "A Build refusal kept across commands"). Decided on 2026-10-11 (OWL-222): a question the asking run names as reopening a choice the runner refused (`refused_choices_asked`, OWL-193) goes to the decider and never to the resolver, whatever its category, so a choice refused for being taken without a human is never settled without one; the routing stays per question, so the run's other questions go through the resolver as before ([build plan](build-plan.md), "A retry asks every refused choice"). The cost is a question for the decider when the thread, a rule or the repository settles the choice since, which the run's `context` names. Rejected: the resolver deciding such a question, reversibly, as before, which is the settling the refusal stopped, by another model; and the resolver running on it with its decision refused, a run whose only outcome is the decider's question. The runner sees only what a run reports: a choice a run makes without recording or asking it stays unseen. Decided on 2026-10-11 (OWL-146): the decider reverses a decision by replying to its DECISION comment. Before delivery, the next run follows the reply over the decision, and a reply to the resolver's plan approval returns the ticket to Design (OWL-345, below); after it, watch starts a correction run on the pull request (section 5, "A reversed decision"). Decided on 2026-10-15 (OWL-296): three more questions go to the decider without the resolver, a finding two review passes left at Design review or Verify, the plan-approval question, and a `replan` question on an approved plan (section 5, "The quality pipeline"); the question of a false premise already does, since only the decider can re-scope or close a ticket. Superseded on 2026-10-18 (OWL-342, "Every question through the resolver" below): those questions, the one reopening a refused choice (OWL-222) and every other a role raises reach the resolver, which decides itself what it answers and what it passes on. Decided on 2026-10-19 (OWL-351): a resolver run's decisions are one DECISION comment, one short line each, numbered D1..Dn, their reasoning in the ticket's "Decisions" document where the tracker holds documents and folded in the comment otherwise; a reply naming `D2` reverses that decision alone, a reply naming none every decision of the comment ([build plan](build-plan.md), "One DECISION comment per run"). Rejected: one comment per decision, which buried the thread (43 on 9 tickets in one drain), and a section of the plan's document, rewritten only with a PLAN.
- **Always human.** Security, data loss, money, legal wording, irreversible external actions and scope changes, by default. Security and irreversible external actions are part of the floor: they reach a human at every delegation level. The other four go to a human unless the project's delegation level hands them to the resolver (below). A project may add categories (`policy.always_human`), which stay human at every level.
- **Delegation level.** Decided with the maintainer on 2026-10-17 (OWL-328), after LOC-1407's first question, filed `scope`, reached the decider though the ticket's text settled it. `policy.resolver_delegation` sets what the resolver may decide, from nothing to everything but the floor:

  | Level | The resolver decides | Always to the decider |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | `none` | nothing: no resolver run | every question |
  | `facts` | what it observes in the repository and its history: a file, an existing pattern, a documented behaviour; since OWL-415, a public fact it checked on the web, cited with its URL and date | the rest |
  | `discoverable` (default) | facts, and what the ticket, the decider's comments, earlier decisions and the project's rules settle | data loss, money, legal, scope, the floor |
  | `ticket` | the same, and a `scope` question the ticket's own text answers, quoted | data loss, money, legal, the floor |
  | `all` | everything but the floor, on its own judgment where nothing settles a question (OWL-342) | security, irreversible |

  The project's added categories go to the decider at every level; since OWL-342 a question reopening a refused choice and a blank category reach the resolver (below). The runner enforces the level, never the resolver's prompt alone: each decision names the kind of its basis (`fact`, `settled`, `ticket` or, at `all` only, `judgment`; at `all`, the runner's own `recommendation` at a chain's bound, which no resolver may give, OWL-375), a basis above the level is refused, and a `ticket` basis must quote text the runner finds in the ticket's title or description or a comment of its decider. Both labels of a decided question (OWL-144) are held to the level's always-human set. The level shows wherever a decision does: `owlshift doctor` names it, and each DECISION comment and delivery report says which level allowed it.
- **Resolver passes.** `policy.resolver_passes` bounds the runs in a row a resolver unblocks in one command: a whole number from 1 to 50, 5 by default, or `"unlimited"`. An unlimited chain is bounded by money instead: `"unlimited"` requires `policy.resolver_budget_usd`, a dollar ceiling on what the resolver runs of one chain cost (the `cost_usd` they reported since the command's last stop), past which the next run's questions go to the decider. A resolver run whose cost is not reported ends the chain, since the ceiling could not be checked. The ceiling may also be set with a number of passes; whichever is reached first ends the chain. Decided on 2026-10-19 (OWL-376): the ceiling counted the asking runs too (Design, the design reviewers, Build, Verify), and on the 2026-10-18 drain one Build cost $9.70, a design review pass $2 to $3 and a resolver run $0.11 to $0.70, so a $5 ceiling was spent by the work itself in one or two loops, and its questions went to the decider for `budget` in at least 13 of the 30 rounds of 2026-10-17 to 19. The key now bounds the delegation, as its name says. The runs a chain unblocks are bounded each by its harness's `budget_usd` ([runtime & operations](runtime-and-operations.md#configuration)), and in number by the pass limit only. What the asking runs whose questions reached the resolver cost is measured, on each `gate` event of the resolver, and bounds nothing. What a whole ticket costs, every run counted and across its commands, which a ceiling per chain would not give, since a chain ends at every stop, is bounded since OWL-378 by `policy.ticket_budget_usd` ("Ticket budget", below), which leaves this ceiling as it is. Rejected: no bound but the failed-run limit, which a chain of successful runs never reaches; a high fixed ceiling of runs, which says nothing of what a run costs; the asking runs in the ceiling, which made a key read as a bound on the resolver cap the whole pipeline.
  - *The bound at `all`.* Decided on 2026-10-20 (OWL-375). From 2026-10-17 to 19, 30 rounds reached the maintainer on 9 tickets run at `all`, at least 13 of them `pass_limit` for `budget`, and 28 of the 29 replies took every recommendation: a bound meant to cap what the resolver costs had become "ask the human everything". At `all`, a bound reached (the pass limit, the ceiling, or a resolver run's cost unreported) still starts no resolver run, and the runner takes the asking run's recommendation on each question it would have given the resolver, a decision of the kind `recommendation`: one DECISION comment for the run, whose lead line names the bound, kept in the ticket ref and reversible as any decision (OWL-146). The stage then runs again; a plan approval under `on-fork` is approved on its recommendation, option A, kept as the resolver's approval is. Still the decider's: a question the asking run filed under the level's always-human set or `policy.always_human`, as always; one whose category is blank or not a token, which only the resolver's label would route; one reopening a refused choice (OWL-222); the questions of a `premise_false` result, whose closure is the decider's; one without a recommendation, which only a question the runner wrote could be. The loop that reached the bound stops: an asking role takes recommendations once per chain, so a role that took them and asks again in the same chain sends its questions to the decider, as before OWL-375; the next stage of the chain (Design review after Design, Build after it) takes its own once, a step forward and not the loop. The DELIVERY report lists such decisions first, under a heading of their own that says how to reverse one. Like any kept decision, one taken on a recommendation is an earlier decision that later runs follow until the decider reverses it. Below `all` nothing changes, `ticket` included: taking a recommendation follows the asking run's own weighing of a choice, which only `all` delegates (the `judgment` kind is `all`'s alone) and a maintainer at `ticket` kept. Accepted residual: no resolver labels the question (OWL-144), so the floor rests on the asking run's category alone. A question touching the floor that the run filed under another word is taken on its recommendation, and the approval is taken whatever the plan's forks touch, since forks carry no category: a departure from the ticket's "unless a fork is in the floor". Each such decision is reversible, shows as a DECISION on the ticket and under its own heading in the DELIVERY report, and a person merges the pull request; `plan_approval = "always"`, which `risky` forces, keeps the approval the decider's. Rejected: a resolver run at a cheaper tier past the bound, which spends what the bound caps; a label-only run to keep the second label, the same cost and a role mode of its own; a scan of the questions' words for the floor's, noisy on a project whose tickets name security everywhere; higher default bounds, which move the problem.
- **Every question through the resolver.** Decided with the maintainer on 2026-10-18 (OWL-342). With `all` and unlimited passes on Locary and Owlshift, the decider still answered seven rounds on LOC-1406, three on OWL-330 and two on OWL-339 overnight: half the rounds never reached the resolver (the bypasses of OWL-222, OWL-139, OWL-296, OWL-306 and the fix and rebase runs'), and the resolver passed on every question it was given, five runs deciding nothing, since even at `all` it needed a written basis and a trade-off has none; read-only, it could not run Bash either, so not even `grep`. The rule now: at every level but `none`, every question every role raises reaches a resolver run, and the resolver decides, question by question, what it answers and what it passes on. That includes Intake's and Design's, a finding two review passes left at Design review or Verify, the plan-approval question, a `replan` on an approved plan, a `premise_false` result's question, a question reopening a refused choice, the run's after late comments, fix, rebase and correction runs' (section 5, "Watch after delivery"), and a question with a blank category, which the resolver's own label then routes (OWL-144). The level still bounds what it may decide (above), and the runner enforces it: Superseded in part on 2026-10-19 (OWL-377, section 5, "A finding goes to its author"): no review finding becomes a question by itself, so a finding two review passes left is no longer among these questions; one a role turns into a question of its own is that role's, routed as any.
  - *Still the decider's.* A question the asking run files under the floor (`security`, `irreversible`) or a category of `policy.always_human` goes to the decider without a run, since no decision on it could be logged; a resolver label on either refuses the decision, as since OWL-144. Under `pipeline.plan_approval = "always"`, which `risky` forces, the plan-approval question is the decider's: the setting names a human. A `premise_false` question's closure is the decider's act in the tracker, so the resolver decides only the re-scope, a `scope` decision under its level's rule, or passes on. The runner's own question after a second late comment (section 4, "No premature resume") stays the decider's: it is the runner stopping, not a role asking.
  - *The `judgment` basis.* At `all`, a fourth kind: a decision resting on the resolver's own weighing when nothing settles the question, a trade-off, a priority, an intent the ticket does not state. It names the option chosen, the reasons, the cost of the choice, and what in the thread, the earlier answers and the rules makes it the one the decider would likely take; the asking run's recommendation may be followed, never as the reason on its own. Below `all` it is refused, as any kind above the level. The resolver still passes on what it cannot weigh: an option that goes against an earlier answer of the decider, a prerequisite only a person can supply (a secret, an account, infrastructure), a check its web tools cannot make (one behind an account or a credential, or any web check when the operator turned its web tools off, OWL-415). A judgment decision is reversible like any (OWL-146); its DECISION comment says it rests on judgment on its visible line, and the delivery report lists such decisions first, so the decider sees which ones to read. The runner's `recommendation` kind ("Resolver passes", "The bound at `all`") is not this: no resolver weighs it, the runner takes the asking run's recommendation as written once the chain's bound stops resolver runs, which is why it is a kind of its own.
  - *Plan approval.* At `all`, under `on-fork`, the resolver approves the plan or picks one of a fork's other options. An approval keeps its DECISION comment (shared, since OWL-351, with the run's other decisions) and the plan's hash in `plan.json`, where the decider's keeps their comment, and the core's hold on the plan's hash is unchanged (OWL-32); a change returns to Design, as the decider's would, and so does the decider's reply reversing it (OWL-345: the kept approval is dropped before Design runs; section 5, "A reversed decision").
  - *A review finding.* A `blocker` or `major` finding still confirmed after the second pass reaches the resolver with the arbiter's ruling and the plan or diff it is about. The resolver may have its fix applied, take another option or drop it, on any basis its level allows; the next Design or Build run follows the decision. Superseded on 2026-10-19 (OWL-377): such a finding goes to its author for one last run and is listed, never asked (section 5, "A finding goes to its author"). Since 2026-10-20 (OWL-379) a ref written before that is no longer read for such a decision or question (section 5, "Records written before").
  - *What the resolver is given.* At `all`, the deep tier and the ticket's effort, since judgment is the hardest call a run makes; below, the standard tier (section 9). A 30-minute deadline. Bash, inside the read-only confinement, whose network is left open as every run's is (section 8), the decider accepting it on OWL-344, so that it reads the code and its history as Build does (`git log`, `git blame`, `grep`), checked live on macOS and Linux on 2026-10-20 ([build plan](build-plan.md), "The resolver"); its result is its last message. Since OWL-415 (decided by the maintainer on 2026-10-21) its web tools too, WebSearch and WebFetch, unless the operator turns them off, so that a CLI flag, an API's behaviour or a library's version is a fact it checks and cites with its URL and date, rather than a question for the decider (section 8, "Untrusted input"). Beside its questions, the asking run's own output: its summary, its draft plan, the findings and the arbiter's rulings, and the branch's diff from its base.
  - *Cost.* A resolver run on each round that had none, on the deep tier at `all`; `policy.resolver_passes` and `policy.resolver_budget_usd` bound the chain as before, the ceiling counting the resolver runs alone (OWL-376), past which, at `all`, the runner takes the asking runs' recommendations (OWL-375), and a round reaching the decider says how many questions were decided. The ticket's budget, checked first, bounds every run of the ticket, the resolver's included ("Ticket budget", below, OWL-378).
  - Rejected: a separate level for judgment, since `all` is the level a maintainer sets to delegate everything they can; keeping the bypasses as an option, which leaves the knob the maintainer turned without effect; the resolver deciding the floor or a project's added categories, which the maintainer kept human; the resolver closing a ticket on a false premise, an act in the tracker.
- **Ticket budget.** Decided with the maintainer on 2026-10-20 (OWL-378), the question OWL-376 left open. `policy.ticket_budget_usd`, a dollar amount, bounds what one ticket's runs cost. Every run the runner starts for the ticket is counted once as it ends, at the `cost_usd` its harness reports: Intake, Design, each reviewer, the arbiter, Build, Verify, the resolver, the answer check, and Watch's fix, rebase and correction runs. The spend is kept in the ticket ref (`cost.json`) right after each run, so the count goes on across every command and stop, whatever stops it (OWL-397, *Kept after every run* below). Before a run starts, the runner compares what the ticket's runs cost since its last restart with the budget; at or past it, no run starts and the ticket parks where it stands, waiting for input if it was, its PARKED comment giving the spend and the budget (park reason `TicketBudget`). A person's `owlshift continue` restarts it, the budget then counting from the restart, as a restart resets the breakers' counts; the ticket's whole spend stays for the record. `owlshift do` on a parked ticket counts on, and so does a ticket `owlshift forget` started over, which keeps `cost.json`. At a resolver routing the budget is checked first: reached, no resolver run starts, as at a chain's bounds. At `all`, the asking run's recommendations are then taken ("The bound at `all`") and the next run parks the ticket; below, the questions go to the decider in a round that names the budget and says that once answered the ticket stops until a person's `owlshift continue`, so the decider answers and a person restarts it once. Its `gate` event is `pass_limit` with `reason` `ticket_budget`.
  - *With `resolver_budget_usd`.* Both apply, whichever is reached first, each with its own consequence: the chain's ceiling counts the resolver runs of one chain and ends the chain, the ticket's budget counts every run of the ticket and parks it. `"unlimited"` passes still require `resolver_budget_usd`: each key keeps one meaning, and no project file breaks.
  - *The value.* No budget when absent, the behaviour before OWL-378. This repository sets 100 USD, above every ticket's spend the maintainer's event log showed on 2026-10-20 for the tickets run since 2026-10-14: OWL-330 79 USD (still open), OWL-339 57, OWL-336 45, OWL-160 42, OWL-344 24, OWL-340 19, OWL-345 15.
  - *The figures.* Each `usage` event gives `ticket_spent_usd`, the ticket's whole spend after its run. Each resolver `gate` event gives `ticket_spent_usd` and `ticket_unreported_runs`, and, with a budget, `ticket_budget_usd` and `budget_spent_usd`, the spend since the last restart, which the budget counts. `owlshift config show` and `owlshift doctor` (`budget`) show the key.
  - *Accepted residuals.* A run that reports no cost adds nothing and is counted apart, which the PARKED comment and the round say, where a resolver run without a cost ends a chain: a run cut off at its deadline reports none, and the failed-run breaker bounds those. A runner killed during a run never reads its report, so that run is not counted at all. Until OWL-397 a third residual stood, a run whose command wrote nothing after it, or of a ticket with no ref yet, not keeping its cost (*Kept after every run*, below).
  - *Kept after every run.* Decided with the maintainer on 2026-10-20 (OWL-397). Until then the spend rode on the next write of the ref that kept something else, since watch read any move of the ref as progress, holding a ticket whose continue left it as it was (section 5, "A comment whose state is not kept"); so a run whose command wrote nothing after it (one cut by a usage limit, a crash, a refused stop, a failed delivery) and the runs of a ticket with no ref yet lost their cost, in the very failures that run again. Now the runner writes the ticket ref right after each run's report, before anything else the command does: the record as the ref keeps it, only `cost.json` new. Watch reads a ticket ref's progress without `cost.json` (section 5, "A move that is only the spend"), so that write lifts no hold. A write that fails (the ref locked, or moved by a run that broke isolation) is a `warning` event (`what` `cost_not_kept`), never a stop: the spend stays in the command and rides on its next write that keeps something else, as before. A ticket without a ref gets one at its first run's end, holding what `owlshift forget` leaves of a ticket at round 0 (Ready, nothing asked) and `cost.json`, which every reader takes for a ticket that keeps nothing: `do` starts from Ready, `continue` refuses it as it refuses a forgotten ticket, the drain takes it in, watch leaves it alone. The ticket has a ref from then on, so the rest of the command keeps its state at each step as for any ticket with a ref, which changes three things for a ticket a person's `owlshift do` started:
    - *Its stops keep their state.* A park is kept with the stage's counters, and its PARKED comment names `owlshift continue`, which restarts the stage it parked at, where it named `owlshift do`, which started again from the first stage with fresh counters; `continue` after a usage limit or a refusal resumes the stage, where it answered that Owlshift keeps nothing on the ticket.
    - *A running watch takes it.* Its ref at Design, Design review, Build or Verify is resumed by `owlshift watch` while the tracker shows it working, as section 5's "The next step" says of a `do` that a stop left there, which until now reached only a `do` whose ticket a round, a decision, a refusal or `forget` had given a ref.
    - *It holds the slot.* That ref holds the project's one slot (section 5, "One at a time"), so `owlshift watch --drain` dispatches no other ticket until it is delivered or parked; `owlshift doctor` names the holder.

    "Makes no ticket ref until a round opens" ([build plan](build-plan.md), OWL-122) is superseded, and with it the exceptions listed against it. Each run adds one commit to the ticket ref. Rejected: the spend in a ref of its own, which watch never reads, at the cost of a second ref per ticket and of `cost.json`, kept in the ticket ref since OWL-378, moved; the spend written only when a command stops, which a crash after a run misses and every stop path would have to carry; the loss accepted as bounded, though a usage limit cuts a run at each of its windows on a ticket watch resumes; the ref made under the stage the run ran in, as a refused Build result makes it, which the command's next write of its state does anyway, where the record `forget` leaves keeps a write of the spend changing nothing else for every reader; and tickets without a ref left out, a residual of one run per command for a `do` typed by hand, but of every cut first intake under the drain.
  - Rejected: summing the local event log, per machine and not the ticket's state; the spend in `state.json`, which every rebuild of the state would have to carry; a bound at the resolver's routing alone, which leaves the loops that pass counts bound spending past it; parking without posting the asking run's questions, whose restart runs that run again to ask them; the spend written after every run, whose moves of the ref would lift watch's holds (since OWL-397 it is, watch reading the ref's progress without it); a park inside the round, a second comment and a second stop for one event, and none for a re-ask.
- **Resuming the asking run's session.** Decided with the maintainer on 2026-10-20 (OWL-394), on live check C18 of that day ([build plan](build-plan.md#results)). On the P5 gate's drains, 93 Design and Build runs, 532 minutes and 85 USD, did nothing but restart a stage from zero after a question round, 40 of them after a resolver decision that came within minutes. Now the stage resumes the Claude Code session of the run that asked, with `claude -p --resume`, instead of starting a fresh run.
  - *Which stops.* A Design run at Design and a Build run at Build whose questions are all settled. That happens when the resolver decides every question of the run, the stage running again in the same command, or when the answer check settles the round the run opened, whatever command or watch step then runs the stage. It applies past the cache's hour as within it: C18 measured a resume 90 seconds after the asking run at 0.018 USD against 0.211 USD for a fresh run of the same task, and one 69 minutes after it at 0.207 USD, with no file read again in either. Intake, the reviewers, the arbiter, the resolver, the answer check and Watch's fix, rebase and correction runs stay fresh runs.
  - *What the resumed run is given.* Its brief, written anew as for any run: its thread holds the DECISION comments, the answers and the RESUME comment. A short prompt tells it to read the brief again and carry on, and to end as its role says, with a new `result.json`. The role's prompt is already in the session and is not sent again. The brief and the result keep their formats.
  - *When a fresh run starts instead,* as before OWL-394, the reason in the run's `run_started` event (`fresh`):
    - its questions are not settled, or the session is another role's or stage's;
    - the harness or its model changed, since the cache is per model;
    - the ticket's branch head moved since the asking run ended. The run reads its branch, not the base, so this is how "the base moved" is read;
    - the session's total cost at the asking run's end is unknown (*Its cost*, below);
    - the transcript is missing, unreadable or larger than 64 MiB, the most the runner copies. Transcripts are kept on the machine that ran the asking run, so a command on another machine starts fresh;
    - a cold resume of too large a context. A resume an hour or more after the asking run ended, past the prompt cache's hour (C18 saw only `ephemeral_1h` writes), writes the whole context to the cache again, about 3.7 USD per million tokens on Sonnet 5.5 in C18. So a cold resume needs a context of at most 200,000 tokens, as the asking run's last request reported it: about 0.75 USD, under half of a restarted Build's 1.6 USD average on the P5 gate. Past it, the rewrite alone nears what a restart costs. A context of unknown size is not resumed cold either. A warm resume reads the context at a tenth of that price, so the cap does not apply to it.
    - The asking run was quarantined or its result refused: it leaves no session to resume.
  - *What holds across a resume.* The resumed run is a new run in everything but its conversation: its own run directory, temporary folder, empty configuration folder, isolation snapshot and check, and `result.json` judged as any. The guardrail flags come from its own command line, and C18 saw them hold on a resume: no MCP server, skill or slash command, the permission mode asked. Only the transcript crosses runs. The executor copies the session's `.jsonl` out of the asking run's configuration folder once its processes are stopped, through no link, into that run's directory, which the sandbox hides from runs. It copies it into the resumed run's empty configuration folder at the same place, and nothing else of the folder outlives a run, as OWL-94 has it. The transcript holds what the run's stdout log already holds. A copy that fails starts the run fresh, with a `warning` (`session_not_resumed`), and the run's `run_ended` says `resumed=false`. A resume the CLI itself refuses is a failed run, and the next run is fresh, the session being spent.
  - *Its cost.* C18 found that a resumed session reports `total_cost_usd` for the whole session, the earlier runs included. So the runner keeps the session's total at the end of each run that asked and counts a resumed run's own cost as the reported total minus that one, in the ticket's budget, `cost.json` and the `usage` event. A total below the kept one is counted whole, with a warning. `--max-budget-usd` counts the current run only (C18).
  - *Where it is kept.* The session's id is one Owlshift chooses for a fresh run, derived from its run, and a resume keeps it. The ticket ref holds the latest asking run's session in `session.json`: its run, session id, role, stage, harness and model, the branch head and time at its end, the session's total cost, its context, the round its questions opened, and whether they are settled. Every write of the ref keeps it, watch does not read it as progress, `owlshift forget` drops it, and the next Design or Build run spends it, resumed or not. `owlshift logs` shows a resumed run's `run_started` with `resumes=<run>` and `session=<id>`, and its `run_ended` with `resumed=true`.
  - Rejected: a configuration folder kept for the whole ticket, where everything the CLI writes would outlive the run; `--fork-session` on each resume, a new id per run for nothing; a process left waiting for the answer; resuming only within the cache's hour, which C18 showed costs no more past it than a fresh run, while keeping the run's own understanding of its work.
- **Plan approval** is set per project: `always`, `on-fork` (only when the design has a real fork) or `never`; the `risky` variant forces `always`.
- **Who decides.** One person, the ticket's decider: only their answers unblock the ticket; other people's comments are context. Decided on 2026-10-02 (OWL-33), reading "the assignee by default" literally: the decider is the ticket's assignee whenever it has one. A ticket without an assignee falls back on the owners the project names for its code zones (`[zones]` in the project file, [runtime & operations](runtime-and-operations.md#configuration)). Each zone the ticket declared belongs to the owner of the deepest owned zone that contains it; a declared zone no owned zone contains has no owner, even when owned zones lie inside it, so a ticket declaring `backend` while `backend/billing` has an owner touches no owned zone, and declaring the narrower zones is the fix. Decided on 2026-10-20 (OWL-412): a ticket declares the zones of its `zone:` labels and, once its intake ended, those of its intake record (section 5, "Declared at intake"), each counted for its own owner, so a label inside an intake zone keeps its owner, where the held set keeps only the outer zone; a ticket without an intake record declares its labels' alone, and an intake that names no zone, which holds the whole repository, adds none, since the whole repository has no owner. A record kept for an earlier text of the ticket still counts until an intake replaces it, as it still gives what the ticket holds: the decider of a ticket in flight does not change with an edit of its text, and the zones it counts were declared by an intake, so an edit cannot choose an owner. Intake's zones never count for an intake run, a first one or one again: intake runs only on a ticket that its assignee or its labels give a decider, so labels, or an assignee, stay required to get a ticket through intake. Intake's zones can then add an owner to its labels', which leaves the ticket without a decider, never replace theirs, and choose the owner alone only for a ticket whose assignee was removed after its intake; they name no one the project file does not, and whoever edits the ticket's text, labels or assignee stays who chooses its zones. Declined with the maintainer on 2026-10-20 (OWL-413): letting a ticket without an assignee or an owning label through intake so that its zones alone choose the decider. An intake run's output, read from the ticket's text, would then choose who instructs every such ticket with no person's gesture, where an assignee or a label is one; the saving would be that one gesture, which the admitter can make on the ticket they are admitting and which the drain's waiting line asks for; and this repository's `owlshift.toml` names no zone owner. Rejected with it: the admitter answering intake's questions until its zones name the owner, a third decider rule in `questions.json` and two deciders in turn on one ticket; and parking a ticket whose intake must ask a person, which stops the intake at every question that reaches a person and needs a brief without a decider. Zones without an owner are left out: when the owned zones the ticket touches all belong to one person, that person decides; when they belong to several, or the ticket touches no owned zone, the ticket has no decider, and the runner says why instead of picking one. The decider is resolved when the command that asks starts, from the assignee and the zones declared then, and stays the decider of that ask; owners are read from the project file at the base commit, never from a run's worktree, so a run cannot choose who instructs it. Rejected: the zone owner before the assignee, which would change a ticket's decider once intake declares its zones and keep the person who delegated it from unblocking it; and several deciders on one ticket, which would leave the answer check without a single last word.
- **Answer check.** Questions are numbered, their options lettered, and each carries a recommendation, so the decider may answer `Q1: B`, a bare letter when one question is asked, or `ok` for every recommendation (decided on 2026-10-16, OWL-324; [build plan](build-plan.md), "The QUESTIONS layout"). Before any resume, a small run classifies each one, save a thumbs-up (below): answered, partial, unanswered, or a counter-question. The runner then resumes, re-asks only what is missing, or replies in the thread. Each check has exactly one of these outcomes, by precedence (decided on 2026-10-01, OWL-114): a counter-question on any question gets the reply and nothing else, so nothing is re-asked and the round does not count toward the re-ask limit, and the next check, after the decider's next reply, classifies every question of the same ask again; otherwise any partial or unanswered question is re-asked, or the ticket parks once the re-ask limit is reached; only when every question is answered does the ticket resume. The answer check writes the reply (decided on 2026-10-02, OWL-115): the run that recognises the counter-question already holds what answers it, the question's context, options and recommendation, the decider's comments and the ticket, so each `counter_question` verdict carries its own reply, and the runner posts it as written, flattened to one line like every text a model writes. Rejected: the resolver, which would add a run per counter-question and a repository-wide context to explain a question already asked, and whose job is to decide, not to talk with the decider; and a reply the runner composes itself, which cannot say what an option means. The reply rests on the brief alone, so it cannot carry repository content the brief does not; when the brief does not hold the answer, the reply says so. Decided on 2026-10-19 (OWL-371): a thumbs-up answers a question asked alone. When the latest ask holds one question, a `+1` reaction its decider puts on that ask's own comment (the round's QUESTIONS comment, or the RE-ASK) is the answer `ok`. It waits through the quiet window as a typed `ok` does, never ending with `go`; one removed before it counts answers nothing; once it counts, the runner resumes the ticket on the question's recommendation itself, the one resume no answer check classifies, since a thumbs-up has no other reading. Nobody else's reaction counts, an app user's included, nor any other emoji, nor a thumbs-up on another comment or on an ask of several questions, where a thumbs-up meaning "seen" would take every recommendation at once. Words win: when the decider also commented since the ask, the check reads their words ([build plan](build-plan.md), "A thumbs-up answers a one-question round"). Rejected: the answer check run on a thumbs-up, which needs a brief format and a role change to classify what can only mean `ok`; Linear's agent-session choices (OWL-354), which need a webhook and show outside the issue's thread.
- **No premature resume.** The runner waits for a quiet window after the decider's last edit (10 minutes by default, `policy.quiet_window_minutes` in `owlshift.toml` sets it per project, from 1 to 1440) unless the reply ends with `go`, and re-reads the thread before delivery for late comments. Decided on 2026-10-03 (OWL-127): the window holds whoever notices the answer, a person typing `owlshift continue TICKET` as much as `watch`, since the person typing it is often not the decider and an answer checked before it is finished can spend a re-ask. The reply is the decider's comment with the newest last edit, and it ends with `go` when its last word is `go` in any case, punctuation trimmed. Rejected: a typed command skipping the window, and a flag to override it, which would let the operator overrule a decider still writing. Decided on 2026-10-04 (OWL-139): for a ticket whose ticket ref keeps an ask, the runner re-reads the comments once a Build run is done and its gate passed, before the push; a late comment is the decider's (the decider of the latest ask) whose last edit is newer than the newest one that run's brief held, so a comment posted between the answer check and the Build start is not late, since the Build read it as an instruction. A late comment gets one more Build run, its thread holding the comment, and that run judges it against the work in front of it: `done` integrates it and delivers, recorded as a decision in the delivery report; questions send the ticket back to needs-input, straight to the decider without the resolver, in a round that names the late comments. A comment the run can follow is integrated, even one that reverses an earlier answer, since the decider's latest word is the instruction; "contradicts the work" means the run cannot integrate it without the decider. One such run per delivery: a further late comment after it opens a round of one always-human question naming them, and nothing is pushed. Rejected: the answer check, whose classes say whether a question of the latest ask is settled and which reads the brief alone, never the work; and a dedicated classification role, which needs a role, a result shape and a contract format of its own, judges the work without it, and still needs the Build run to integrate. The build role is told so (`roles/build.md`, "Resuming"): a decider comment newer than the runner's latest RESUME comment is integrated, steps added or revised even when every step is done, or asked about when the run cannot follow it without the decider; the decision in the delivery report still shows the reviewer that late comments were given to a run. A ticket without a question round is delivered without a re-read.
- **Pull request reviews are an input channel.** A human review comment sends the ticket back to Build or opens a gate, like a ticket comment.
- **A parked ticket shows it.** Decided on 2026-10-04 (OWL-148): when the runner parks a ticket, the PARKED comment is followed by a move of its visible stage to the state the project maps under `[tracker].states.parked`, an optional key; a project that names none shows a parked ticket as needs input, and the PARKED comment says why. So the stage alone does not say that questions are open: the comments and the ticket ref do, and nothing reads the stage back. A restart moves the stage to what the ticket then waits for: needs input when it was parked while questions waited, working when Build runs again. Rejected: a required key, which would break every existing project file and ask every tracker for a workflow state of its own; and needs input alone, which leaves a parked ticket indistinguishable from one waiting for an answer.

## 5. Architecture overview

One deterministic runner per machine serves every adopted project; models only ever run inside a harness, one role at a time. Agents never touch the tracker: the runner is its only writer.

```mermaid
flowchart TB
    tf["Tracker and forge<br/>tickets, comments, states · PRs, checks, reviews"]
    notifier["Notifier<br/>push, chat"]
    subgraph runner["Runner, one process per machine"]
        scanner["Scanner<br/>polls, reconciles"] --> scheduler["Scheduler<br/>ready set, claims"] --> executor["Executor<br/>runs one role"] --> writer["Writer<br/>sole tracker writer"]
        store["Local store<br/>run history, costs"] -.- scanner
        policy["Policy<br/>floor + project rules"] -.- writer
    end
    git["Git remote<br/>claims, checkpoints, artifacts on dedicated refs"]
    harness["Harness CLIs<br/>Claude Code, Codex, … one worktree per run"]
    tf -- read --> scanner
    writer -- write --> tf
    writer -- notify --> notifier
    scheduler <-- claim --> git
    executor <-- artifacts --> git
    executor <-- "spawn · result" --> harness
```

One event, end to end:

1. **Scanner** polls the tracker and the forge (webhooks are an optional speed-up, never required) and turns changes into events: ticket admitted, answer posted, PR merged, check failed.
2. **Scheduler** computes the ready set (admission, blockers, resources, caps, budget) and claims a ticket with an atomic push of a git ref, so two machines never take the same ticket.
3. **Executor** creates the worktree, writes the brief, launches the harness CLI for one role, then validates `result.json` against its schema and checks isolation (main checkout untouched, diff inside the worktree, right branch). An exit code is never taken as proof.
4. **Writer** applies the result: comments, state transitions, follow-up proposals, the PR. It is the only component with write access to the tracker and forge, and it asks **Policy** before each write.
5. Artifacts and the checkpoint go to the git remote; cost and run history go to the **Local store** (SQLite); the **Notifier** fires only when a human has become the blocker.

**The brief's thread** (OWL-357, decided 2026-10-19). A brief carries the ticket ref's record once: the plan in `plan`, each ask as its round's entry, each decision of the resolver as a decision entry. Owlshift's own comments that only repeat it read in the thread as their header line, their time and their comment id: PLAN, RESUME and DELIVERY. What the record does not carry, a PARKED or INTAKE comment and an ask or a decision it no longer keeps, is cut to 4 KiB, and a REPLY stays whole, since the decider's next answer is read against it. A person's comment is always whole. A comment counts as Owlshift's when it is marked from its header to its footer and was never edited, Owlshift editing none; a person who posts a whole copy of one has it read short as well ([build plan](build-plan.md), "The brief's thread"). What is settled reads short too (OWL-359, decided 2026-10-19): a round the decider answered, every question's latest verdict `answered`, is one `answered` entry giving each question's text and what the answer check understood of the answer, its options being on the ticket; a decision's question is its id and text, the decision saying what was chosen, unless the decider replied to it. The ask an answer check judges is always whole.

**Deployment.** One service process per machine, `owlshift daemon`, serves several projects, the machine-wide caps (concurrent runs, each subscription's usage) shared through the files P6 keeps. Since P7 it supervises one `owlshift watch --drain` per served project rather than running their passes itself (section 5, "The background service"); comparing two projects' priorities, the single browser slot and port ranges wait for a later step. A local API serving the CLI, the local web UI (P8) and the desktop app (P11) comes with P8. The same binary runs on a team server; since shared state lives in the tracker and git, moving from a laptop to a server needs no migration.

**Folded when superseded** (OWL-353, decided 2026-10-19). A ticket that goes through several rounds kept every PLAN, QUESTIONS and RESUME comment expanded: on the 2026-10-18 drain, OWL-160 had 13 comments and OWL-336 had 14, most of them out of date. On a tracker that can resolve a comment (section 6), when the runner posts a comment that supersedes one of its own, it resolves the older one, which the tracker then shows folded: a PLAN is folded by a later PLAN, whole or the pointer to the plan's document, or by a DELIVERY; a QUESTIONS or RE-ASK comment of a round by the RESUME of that round or the QUESTIONS of another round; a RESUME by the QUESTIONS or the RESUME of another round. So only the latest round and the live PLAN stay expanded. A DECISION is never folded, since a threaded reply to it reverses it; nor a REPLY, an INTAKE, a PARKED or a DELIVERY comment, nor a person's comment: a comment counts as Owlshift's by the rule of "The brief's thread" above, for this ticket, and only a top-level one, since Linear resolves no reply. The comments to fold are read from the thread, as the ticket ref keeps no id of a PLAN or a RESUME and its format does not change. A comment that a comment posted in between already folds is left, so each is resolved once, and a person who unfolds one keeps it open; one whose fold failed stays expanded. A fold that fails is a warning, never a stop, and a folded comment is still read as before: every run, check and report reads it as it did. A tracker without the capability, the Markdown tracker among them, folds nothing.

**Watch mode, before the runner process.** Decided on 2026-10-04 (OWL-152): `owlshift watch`, roadmap P3, is a loop in the foreground of a terminal that does what a person typing `owlshift continue` does once the decider has answered, for one project, the repository of the current directory, whose configuration and secrets it reads once at start.

- **Which tickets.** Those whose ticket ref, in the project's dedicated checkout, keeps a state waiting for the decider's answers and not parked; and, since OWL-208, those at Watch whose delivered pull request Owlshift still follows ("Watch after delivery", below). The ticket refs are the one list of the tickets Owlshift asked questions on or delivered, and a ticket without one has nothing to answer or follow. A parked ticket stays parked until a person types `continue`, or since OWL-420 `continue --to-watch`, which leaves the driving to watch ("A restart handed to watch", below), even once its decider has answered: the restart is the person's gesture ([build plan](build-plan.md), OWL-122), a deliberate reading of the roadmap's "without typing `continue`". Since OWL-243, a park at Watch also ends when its pull request is merged, closed or pushed to by someone else, which restarts nothing ("A park the pull request outlives", below): only a person's `continue` restarts a run. A ticket left at Build, by a usage limit, a failed delivery or a refused push, before any question round (round 0) also waits for `continue`: resuming it once the harness's limit resets is P6's pause until reset. Since OWL-273, a ticket an answered intake round left at Intake, its second intake cut off, is an exception: watch resumes it while it shows needs input ("Intake", below). Since OWL-340, so is a ticket left at Design, Design review, Build or Verify with an answered round (round > 0), as the next paragraph says; since OWL-367, whatever its round, a Build left at round 0 and a `do` typed by hand that a stop left there included, since watch works each ticket one stage step a pass and leaves every ticket it works at such a stage between two passes ("One step a pass", below). A ticket at Intake keeps OWL-273's rule, an answered round only. These restarts stay the person's: P3's exit gate, reworded on 2026-10-10 (OWL-208, [roadmap](roadmap.md) P3), records each with its reason instead of failing on it. Rejected: reading the event log, which is append-only and does not say whether a ticket still waits, and naming the tickets on the command line, which would miss a round a `do` opens meanwhile in another terminal.
  - *A stage left after an answered round.* Decided on 2026-10-18 (OWL-340). Seen live on 2026-10-17 on pitchopp/locary, ticket LOC-1406: the answer check passed, `continue`'s path posted the RESUME, kept the ticket at Design in round 3 and showed it In Progress, and then the dispatch's first forge read (the open pull request of the branch) was answered with a transient HTTP 401, so no run started. Watch listed no such ref (it takes waiting refs only), the answer check had no comment newer than `checked_through` to read, and the hold was lifted because the ref had moved; a refusal notifies no one ([runtime and operations](runtime-and-operations.md), "Notifications"), so the ticket stayed In Progress with nothing running and nothing retrying until a typed `continue`. Anything else `dispatch` or the run refuses before it starts leaves the ticket the same way. Watch now resumes a ticket whose ref is at Design, Design review, Build or Verify with an answered round (round > 0) while the tracker, read again under the project's lock, still shows it working, through `continue`'s own path (`OnDemand::continue_shown`), held as any continue is: ten minutes after a resume that left the ref as it was, until the reset of a usage limit, until a person acts on a state not kept. The rule is OWL-273's, widened: the ref's stage and round tell the case, with no marker. From such a ref `continue` runs no answer check and posts no RESUME, since it checks answers only from a waiting ref, so a forge that keeps refusing costs neither at any pass, only the retry every ten minutes. The stage is read without the lock first, and a ticket shown in another stage (moved by a person, or in the ready column) is left alone and its stage not read again for ten minutes unless its ref moves. While another command holds the project's lock (a run in flight, whose ticket shows working too), a pass ends at its snapshot with the busy line before it reads any ticket: no stage read, no resume, no hold and no comment, and the first pass after the lock is released resumes the ticket, with no ten-minute wait. A ticket a review loop (OWL-307) left at Build or Verify, a followed delivery whose ticket is at those stages, is resumed the same way once its pull request, read for an end, has not ended the loop; `continue` reads it again first under the lock, and an end it finds moves the ref, so no hold is set. The bound is the stage's: a resumed run that returns `failed` moves the ref and is retried at the next pass until two failed runs park the ticket, which only a person's `continue` restarts, while a refusal that leaves the ref as it was and a usage limit count nothing. This also resumes, ahead of P6's pause until reset (OWL-160), a Build with an answered round that a usage limit, a failed delivery or a refused push left, once the limit's reset has passed. A ticket at those stages at round 0 is not taken, a review loop's Build left before its question round included: it still needs `owlshift continue`, since taking it would take the drain's tickets (scenario S6 holds the slot at round 0 until a person types `continue`); a Build left at round 0 is not resumed by this rule. Reversed on 2026-10-19 (OWL-367, "One step a pass", below): the drain's own tickets now wait at those stages between two steps, so watch resumes a ticket there whatever its round, and S6's Build cut by a usage limit is resumed once the limit has reset. Accepted residuals: a restarted watch forgets a usage limit's reset, so its first pass resumes a limited Build once, which meets the limit again and pauses; and a ticket whose move to working failed after the RESUME (a `stage_not_moved` warning) shows needs input with the ref at Design, which the rule leaves alone; since OWL-367 a watch that ran that answer check moves it to working at its next pass and resumes it, one restarted since still leaving it alone. Rejected: `continue` making the dispatch's reads (the forge read, `configuring_base`, `rules_at`) before the answer check, which cannot cover a refusal only `drive` raises and reorders `dispatch` and `check_answers` for a case the resume already covers, and both together, for the same reason (either can follow in its own ticket); a marker in the ticket ref saying a refusal left the ticket, the format change OWL-273 rejected for what the visible stage and the round already say; a retry of a 401 in the GitHub adapter before it reports `Unauthorized`, which would have hidden this instance but not the gap and changes how many calls a revoked token makes, so a ticket of its own; and counting each resume as a failed run, which would park a ticket on a usage limit.
  - *A restart handed to watch.* Decided on 2026-10-21 with the maintainer (OWL-420), found at P6's exit gate on Locary (OWL-408), where three tickets parked by a base-side gate failure each needed `owlshift continue` once the cause was fixed: `continue` restarts a park and then drives the ticket in the foreground to its next stop, an hour of Build, gate and Verify in a terminal tab held open beside the operator's `owlshift watch --drain`, where Ctrl-C quarantines the project ("How it stops"). Since watch already drives every ticket it resumes, the person's part is only the restart gesture (OWL-122). `owlshift continue --to-watch TICKET` does what `continue` does up to where it would start driving: the same reads first (a review loop's pull request read for an end, the empty gate), the restart under the ticket's lock and the project's sections, the budget counted afresh (OWL-378), the restart's `decision` event with `to_watch` and what watch does with the ticket (`takes`: `resume`, `answers` or `follow`), the visible stage, and `carry.json` dropped as for any person's `continue`; then it returns, with exit status 0 and no notification, and starts no run and makes no admission check: watch's next pass takes the ticket under its usual rules (the slot's turn, `max_in_flight`, collisions, machine slots, usage limits). It hands a ticket only to what watch already takes: a park at Design, Design review, Build or Verify, restarted at that stage and shown working, which watch resumes ("The next step"); a park at Watch whose pull request is still followed, shown in review, which watch follows; a park while a round waited, back to needs input, whose answers watch checks once the decider's reply counts. Refused, naming plain `continue`, with nothing written: a park at Intake that restarts to Intake itself, which goes on showing parked while intake runs (OWL-248), so watch, which resumes an Intake ticket only while it shows needs input, would never take it; a Verify whose Build is no longer the one to push (`verify_resumable`), a review loop's included, which plain `continue` builds again; and any other state watch does not take. On a ticket that is not parked the flag restarts nothing and writes nothing: it succeeds, saying what watch does with the ticket, when watch takes it as it stands (a round waiting, a stage shown as watch's resume requires, a followed delivery), and is refused otherwise; it says what watch is eligible to do, a hold watch keeps on the ticket ("Errors", "A comment whose state is not kept") standing until its own end. The ticket's acceptance line "An answered round waiting for the decider behaves as today with or without the flag" is read that way: such a round is handled as today, by watch, the flag running no answer check. Without a watch on the project the command still succeeds and says that nothing drives the ticket until `owlshift watch` starts: watch, plain or `--drain`, holds a shared lock on `watch.lock` in the project's data directory while it runs, which the command tries once to take exclusively; the kernel drops a dead watch's lock. Two orders keep the handoff safe. The visible stage moves before the ticket ref is written, unlike plain `continue`: a pass that read the restarted ref while the tracker still showed parked would leave the ticket alone for ten minutes as one shown elsewhere, while a pass between the two writes reads a parked ref, which it leaves alone with no hold; a stage that cannot be moved refuses the restart with nothing written, and a ref write that fails moves the stage back to parked when the ref, read again, is still the one the command started from. And the command holds the ticket's lock until it returns, so a step of watch's that starts meanwhile finds the ticket busy, which holds nothing, and runs at a later pass. `owlshift why` names a ticket handed for a resume as restarted for watch, until watch's own wait or the ticket's run replaces it. Accepted residuals: a watch whose shared lock is tried at the very moment of the command's probe runs without it after a few tries, warned of, and the flag then says no watch runs; a ref write that fails after the ref moved (its run file not saved) leaves the stage shown working, which the error names. Rejected: a move of the ticket from parked to working on the board as the restart gesture (option B), which a stray drag would trigger; a request file that watch reads, a channel and a format for what the ticket ref already says; and plain `continue`'s order, ref then stage, for the ten-minute gap above.
- **The drain.** Since OWL-244, `owlshift watch --drain` also reads the ready column at each pass and takes admitted tickets in, intake first and one Build at a time ("The drain", below).
- **Poll interval.** 60 seconds after the end of a pass in which no ticket took a stage step; at once after a pass in which one did, its ticket ref moved, or in which the ticket whose turn the slot was left it (OWL-367, "One step a pass", below). A pass works the tickets one at a time, each for one stage step at most, so the others wait for the run in progress, not for a whole pipeline: an answer that counts is picked up once that run ends, or within a minute when nothing runs, at about one comments request per waiting ticket per pass, and since OWL-265 one stage request per ticket at Watch whose delivery is followed ("The stage while a repair runs", below), against Linear's 2,500 requests an hour (check C4). Since OWL-367 each step also reads its ticket and its stage, a few requests per step. Until OWL-367, while one ticket's answer check and its whole pipeline ran in a pass, the others waited, for hours once a Build and Verify loop ran long. Rejected for now: an interval of the project's or the operator's choosing, and waking at the very time a reply counts, which saves less than a minute.
- **The same resume.** Whether the decider's reply counts is decided by the function `continue` uses: an answer newer than the latest ask, left unedited for the project's quiet window by this machine's clock or ending with `go` (section 4). When it counts, a `decision` event records that watch continues the ticket, and the ticket goes through `continue`'s own path: answer check, RESUME, RE-ASK or REPLY, Build, delivery, park. Its outcome is printed, and notifies the operator, as `continue`'s is. Since OWL-367 that path stops once the RESUME is kept, and what it resumes runs at the next pass, one stage step a pass ("One step a pass", below).
- **One step a pass.** Decided on 2026-10-19 (OWL-367). On 2026-10-18 the maintainer answered five tickets within two minutes; watch took OWL-160's answer and, in the same pass, ran its answer check, Design, a Build of 85 minutes, Verify, a Build of 23 minutes, Verify, the resolver and a Build, until a park 3 h 27 later, reading no other ticket's thread meanwhile. On 2026-10-19, OWL-339's Build and Verify loop held the answers of OWL-336 and OWL-344 unread for more than an hour. "A pass works the tickets one at a time" cost little while a Build took minutes.
  - *A step.* Watch works each ticket for one stage step a pass, then goes on to the next ticket. A continue stops once its answer check's RESUME is kept (a round back at Ready excepted, which runs nothing); a resume, the drain's dispatch and a review loop stop once the run of their ticket's stage is kept, its state in the ticket ref (`Stop::Stepped`). A step ends at each change of the core state's stage: after a Design run, a Design review pass (to Design, to Build, or a round the resolver decided), the pass of Design's last revision, which runs no reviewer (OWL-377), a Build with the Verify pass of its commit, a Verify pass that sends the ticket back to Build (a fix or the last fix). This is the reading of "at most one agent run per ticket per pass": a step is one stage's run, with the runs that settle it in the same step, a review pass's reviewers and its arbiter, a failed run's one more run with the failure in its brief, the resolver's run and the runs again after it decided all of a run's questions, the one Build run that integrates late comments, and the delivery. A Build and its Verify pass stay one step: a decider comment between them would make `continue` at Verify refuse to run the pass (OWL-304), leaving a ticket that holds the slot with nothing to resume it. `owlshift do` and a person's `continue` run to the end, as before.
  - *The next step* runs at a later pass, which comes at once after a step ("Poll interval"). Watch resumes the ticket as "A stage left after an answered round" says, whatever its round: every ticket at Design, Design review, Build or Verify that the tracker shows working, held as any resume is (ten minutes after a refusal that leaves the ref as it was, until a usage limit's reset, until a person acts on a state not kept). So a `do` typed by hand that a stop left at round 0 is picked up by a running watch too. The step into Build is the dispatch to Build, outside a review loop: the base fetched again and a branch with no commits of its own fast-forwarded to it (OWL-274), as when one command runs from Design review to Build; a person's `continue` keeps OWL-331's rule.
  - *What a step carries.* What a command keeps in memory to bound its runs crosses the steps: the resolver's chain (`policy.resolver_passes` and its ceiling, section 4), which alone bounds the rounds of review passes the resolver decides in a row, each buying one more pass, with, since OWL-375, the asking roles that took recommendations at its bound, and whether a Build run of the delivery already integrated late comments (OWL-139, once). Watch keeps them for the ticket's next step and drops them at any other outcome. Since OWL-411 it keeps them with the ticket ref's progress (OWL-397) after the step, and the next step uses them only while the ref still shows that progress: a command that keeps anything in the ref between two steps, a person's `do` or `continue`, moves its progress and the next step starts afresh as a typed `continue` would, while a move of the spend alone does not; a ref that cannot be read after a step drops them too. Since OWL-399 they are kept on disk, in `carry.json` ("Parallel", "What it carries", below), tied to the ticket-ref commit the step left as its lock read it, rather than to that progress: between two steps of a ticket that holds the slot only a person's command writes its ref, and `do` and a person's `continue` remove the file, so the next step starts afresh as before; a step that ran nothing (a busy project, a stale envelope, the pull request cap, a gesture that does not admit) leaves it as it was.
  - *The slot's turn.* Between two passes several ticket refs may hold the slot ("One at a time", below): a ticket whose answer counted while another's steps ran is at its stage, working, from its RESUME. Watch's steps that need the slot, at Design, Design review, Build and Verify, run in the turn of the first `policy.max_in_flight` holders by dispatch time (OWL-401, "Three counts" below), one whose ref keeps no dispatch time first, then in identifier order; none while a ticket ref cannot be read (`schedule::slot_turn`, in `owlshift-core`). Until OWL-401 the turn's previous holder kept it while its ref held the slot; the dispatch-time order replaced that rule, so at a capacity of one a ticket dispatched earlier, whose answered round brings it back, takes the turn at the next step boundary of a ticket dispatched later: finishing comes before starting. A ticket whose ref comes to hold the slot during a pass takes the turn while fewer than the capacity hold it. The others wait: one `decision` event (`watch` = `waits`, `reason` = `slot`, the holders in `tickets`) and one line, once per holder. The drain, its dispatch's admission and `owlshift doctor` read every such ref as holding, as before, so nothing is dispatched from Ready while the capacity is held. A review loop's start is a repair at Watch, which needs no capacity (OWL-401); its next steps wait for their turn. A person's `do` or `continue` makes the drain's check since OWL-401 (section 5, "Parallel", "Admission, one transaction").
  - *Every answer at every pass.* Each pass still reads every waiting ticket's thread first.
