- **Who merges, on GitHub.** The App's user token acts as the user who signed in, so the merge shows that person as its author (`merged_by`, C19). The token needs Contents (read and write) for it, which the App asks since D14; an installation that has not accepted that permission refuses the merge, a final refusal, and `owlshift doctor` names the missing permission when `[forge] merge` is not `never`.
- **Known limits.** Branch protection binds an administrator only where the repository enforces it for administrators: Owlshift's own conditions, not the protection, are the guarantee, the review among them since OWL-446. The last read narrows the base and the closed pull request to a short window, it does not pin them: a sibling merged, a person's close or a retarget between the last read and GitHub's merge still gets this pull request merged, since neither GitHub merge call (REST, or GraphQL's `mergePullRequest`) refuses a closed pull request or takes a base to expect (C19). A 401 on the call stretches that window by the token's refresh before its one retry. The branch is not deleted: the repository's "automatically delete head branches" setting does that. OWL-427's rebase of held branches does not touch a followed pull request, so it cannot race the merge.
- **As built** (OWL-445). The merge is a step of watch's own process at the end of a followed pull request's pass (`watch/merge.rs`), after the owed report, the correction's and the reviews' reads; a failed read of either, a reply within its quiet window or a run held back waits, as a run that started does. The switch is read in the dedicated checkout at the base branch's head, which one light query (`GitHubForge::branch_head`, the expression the base checks query reads) gives once a pass; the checkout is fetched only when it does not hold that commit, and a commit's switch is kept in watch's memory. A project at `never` reads nothing more. The pass's read of the pull request does not say whether it is a draft (its query's text is pinned by fixtures recorded live, OWL-217), so the merge reads it with the last read's query once every condition before it holds. A base that moved gets the refresh rebase as a repair step (`StepKind::Repair` with `merge_refresh`), whose repair rebases onto the base it fetched when its own read is green. Events: `pr_merged` (`watch` `merged`, the pull request, head and method, and the merge's commit when GitHub's answer gave it), a `decision` with `watch` `merge_refused` (`final`, `status`, `reason`), and the warnings `merge_facts_unread` (`fact`: `base`, `switch`, `label`, `stage` or `questions`, and `pull_request` or `merge` for a read or a call the forge failed otherwise than by a spent budget, which pauses the forge reads as a look's does; once until every fact reads), `merge_unanswered` (no verdict), `merged_unposted` (the MERGED comment was not posted, so the end waits for the next read) and `merge_unkept` (a ref write failed). The MERGED comment names the pull request and head, by which a later post finds it. `merging` stays across an open read of its head, since a merge with no verdict may still land; a read or push of another head clears it, and so does an end. Known limits: a `merging` left set (a crash before the call, or no verdict) followed by a person's merge of that same head reads as the runner's merge; a crash between the end's write and the `pr_merged` event loses the event, as any event recorded once its write is done; a draft is never sent to GitHub, so it is no refusal and notifies no one. Scenario S18: `owlshift-testkit`'s `merge_scenarios.rs`.

**The drain.** Decided on 2026-10-13 (OWL-244), roadmap P4, scenarios S3, S4, S6, S12 and S13: Owlshift picks its own work, one ticket at a time. `owlshift watch --drain` does what watch does, then, in the same pass, takes in the admitted tickets of the ready column: intake first, then a dispatch to Build when a ticket is ready and nothing else of the project is being built. From P5 the dispatch takes the ticket to the first stage of its variant after Ready, Design for `standard` and `risky`, and the one slot is held from that dispatch until delivery (section 5, "The quality pipeline"). Since OWL-367 the dispatch runs that first stage's step only, and watch takes the next ones, one a pass ("One step a pass", above). Without `--drain`, watch is P3's and picks nothing. The Linear and GitHub facts below were checked live that day, read-only ([build plan](build-plan.md), "P4 · The drain"), except the writes, which are pending checks, each the first step of the work that writes.

- **Admission.**
  - *The gesture* is the project's `[tracker].admit`, as before: a label (`{ label = "agent" }` on Owlshift's own backlog) or a state (`"ready"`, or `{ state = … }`). A state gesture is the move to the ready column itself, so `"ready"` names it without repeating it, and `{ state = … }` must name `[tracker].states.ready`, the configuration being refused otherwise. Decided on 2026-10-16 by the maintainer (OWL-322), while setting up Locary's file: the two values can only be equal, so the unit form is read as the state gesture on `states.ready` when the file is parsed, and nothing downstream tells the two forms apart; a file using it requires an Owlshift that reads it; doctor's tracker states line checks it with the other states (OWL-155). `"delegation"` is not built: decision D8's app takes no delegation scope, so nothing can delegate to it, and `--drain` refuses to start on it, which doctor warns of.
  - *By whom.* `[tracker].admitters` lists the tracker accounts whose gesture admits, by identifier written exactly, as zone owners are (section 4, "Who decides"), and is read from `owlshift.toml` at the base commit, as they are. Without it `--drain` refuses to start; `owlshift do` needs no admission, as before, since the person typing it makes the gesture. The account of a label gesture is the actor of the latest history entry that added the label, or the ticket's creator when no entry did, since Linear records no history entry for a label set at creation. An app user (Owlshift's own, D8) never admits. Like OWL-74's ticket author, an issue created through a person's API key is theirs: a tool holding the operator's key admits as the operator.
  - *A state gesture* is read past the runner's own moves. Decided on 2026-10-14 by the maintainer (OWL-263): the runner moves a ticket back to the ready column through the app ("Back to the ready column", below), so once `owlshift forget` drops the intake record, the latest move to ready would be the app's, and the ticket would never be admitted again until a person moved it by hand. So the moves of state Owlshift's own app user made are skipped, and the latest move left decides: to the ready state, its actor is the gesture's account, whoever it is, so a later move by someone not listed replaces an earlier admitter's, and another app's never admits; anywhere else, by a person, a bot or another app, the ticket was taken out of the column and the gesture is nobody's until a person moves it back. With no move left, the creator made the gesture when the ticket was created in the ready state, which the oldest move's origin tells, or no move at all; otherwise nobody did. Skipping Owlshift's move can only reach a move to ready that nothing but Owlshift followed, so a withdrawn admission never counts. Only Owlshift's own app user is skipped, decided on 2026-10-14 by the maintainer (OWL-270), overturning OWL-263's first rule, which skipped every app user: another app with write access, such as a coding agent's app that takes a ticket to In Progress and later puts it back, would leave a person's earlier admission standing for a ticket no person put back. The app user's id comes from the check Owlshift already makes through the app when it opens the tracker (`viewer`), so no request is added; without an app, Owlshift writes as the API key's account and no move is skipped. Checked live on 2026-10-14, read-only: the app's moves (OWL-256, OWL-257, OWL-258) carry an actor with `app` true and the states moved from and to; the GitHub integration's carry a bot actor. Checked live on 2026-10-14 at 11:02 UTC (OWL-270), read-only, through a token of the app revoked right after: `viewer` answers `app` true and an id, and each of the ten moves of state by an app actor on those three issues has that id as its actor's. Residuals: a bot's move out of the column, such as the GitHub integration's at a pull request, fails closed, which the runner's moves back to ready, all made before a dispatch, do not meet; once the app is replaced, the former app's moves are another app's, so a ticket whose gesture is read again afterwards (its text edited, or its intake record forgotten) and whose latest move is one of them waits, failing closed, until a person moves it back to ready. The Markdown tracker is not concerned: its gesture is the ticket's author, and it keeps no history. Rejected: documenting the gap and leaving the move to the person, which silently drops from the drain every ticket of a state gesture that went through a round and was forgotten; skipping every app user, which reads another app's move out of the column and back as no move at all.
  - *Exclusion* (S12). `[tracker].exclude`, an optional label: a ticket carrying it is never taken in, whatever else it carries. A ticket already dispatched is stopped by its stage, never by a label ("A ticket a person moved", above).
  - *Recorded once.* The gesture's account is read just before the ticket's first intake and kept in its intake record (below). Later passes ask only that the gesture is still on the ticket. Accepted residual: a label taken off and put back by someone not listed is not read again, labels being trusted as ticket metadata as zone labels are.
  - *Not admitted.* A gesture from an account not listed is a `warning` event (`not_admitted`), once per ticket and account, and a line; nothing is posted on the ticket, since a stranger's ticket gets no answer from Owlshift.
  - *Rejected.* Admission open to any account of the tracker, which section 8 forbids and a public tracker would turn into anyone's queue; the key required in every project file, which breaks the existing ones (OWL-148's reason); the assignee as admitter, since assigning a ticket to a person is not handing it to an agent.
- **Picking.**
  - *The ready column* is the state `[tracker].states.ready` names (`Todo` on Owlshift's backlog). Each pass reads it once, after watch's own work: the team's tickets in that state carrying the admission label (all of them for a state gesture), 50 a page to the last, each with its priority, creation time, assignee, labels, title, description, and the tickets that block it with the type of their state. That query cost 171 of Linear's complexity points on 2026-10-13, so a pass a minute spends about 10,000 of the 3,000,000 an hour, and one request of the 2,500. A ticket whose intake round waits is not in the column; watch continues it as any round ("Back to the ready column", below).
  - *Order.* Priority first: urgent, high, medium, low, then none (Linear's 1, 2, 3, 4, then 0). Ties go to the ticket created first, then the lower identifier. The runner sorts, never the tracker: Linear's priority sort put roughly the newest first among equals, and ascending meant low first, both observed that day; the Markdown tracker has no sort. Rejected: Linear's manual order, which the Markdown tracker lacks, and the newest first.
  - *Blockers* come from two sources, whose union counts: the tracker's relations (on Linear a `blocks` relation whose `relatedIssue` is the ticket; the Markdown tracker's `blocked_by`) and the text convention, read by a pure function in `owlshift-core`. The convention is a line of the description that starts, after a list marker, heading marks or emphasis, with `Blocked by` in any case; every identifier on that line is a blocker (on Linear and the Markdown tracker `KEY-n`; `#n` for GitHub Issues in P9). Twelve Owlshift tickets used the phrase on 2026-10-13. Nine name their blockers on that line ("Blocked by OWL-203.", "Blocked by OWL-33 and OWL-122."), and those whose relations were read (OWL-118, OWL-124, OWL-223) have exactly those. "Blocked by the remaining P2 work", a heading with the identifier on the next line, and "follow-ups blocked by this issue" give nothing, which is intake's work (below). A blocker releases the ticket once the tracker shows it completed (on Linear, a state of type `completed`; on the Markdown tracker, the stage `Done`), or once its ticket ref records its pull request merged. A canceled or duplicate blocker does not: the ticket waits, its wait naming the blocker, until a person removes the relation or the line. Rejected: releasing on cancellation, which starts work whose prerequisite was dropped without a person saying it still makes sense. An identifier that names no ticket is decided on 2026-10-14 (OWL-264). The convention reads any `KEY-n` token, so "Blocked by OWL-1 (not UTF-8 related)" also names `UTF-8`, and a ticket can name itself. The ticket's own identifier is ignored, by relation or by line, since a ticket is never its own prerequisite. An identifier the tracker answers it does not know (on Linear, `Entity not found: Issue`; on the Markdown tracker, no ticket folder) holds the ticket, its wait naming it, until a person corrects the line, as a canceled blocker does. Only that answer makes it unknown: a read that fails otherwise counts the blocker open for the pass, so it never releases the ticket. Rejected: dropping an unknown identifier with a `warning` event, which dispatches before a prerequisite named in another tool or mistyped, the warning reaching no one before the work starts; reading only the team's key, which drops a real blocker of another team of the workspace without a word and leaves a missing `OWL-n` and the ticket itself as they were. Intake drops an unknown dependency and the ticket itself ("Dependencies to relations", below) because its dependencies are a model's reading, shown in the INTAKE comment; a "Blocked by" line is a person's statement, which the drain does not overrule.
  - *Unblocked by a merge* (S4). The pass reads the followed pull requests before it picks, so a merge watch reads ends the following and releases the ticket it blocked in the same pass. Linear's GitHub integration also moves the merged ticket to Done (OWL-242 moved at its merge on 2026-10-13, by the GitHub integration actor). The branch starts from the base fetched at the dispatch, so from the merge.
    - *How the branch reaches that base* (OWL-274, decided 2026-10-14). The ticket's intake ran in an earlier pass, through the executor on the ticket's branch, so the worktree and `owlshift/<id>` already exist at the base of that pass, and the executor reuses a kept worktree as it is. So at every dispatch to Build from Ready, the drain's and `do`'s alike (from P5 at the dispatch from Ready, whichever stage follows, and again at the dispatch to Build; section 5, "The quality pipeline"), before the `dispatch` event, `Executor::start_from_base` brings a kept branch with no commits of its own to the base fetched then. A branch whose head is an ancestor of that base is fast-forwarded: in its worktree, once the checks the executor makes of a kept worktree pass, by `git merge --ff-only --no-squash --no-autostash --no-overwrite-ignore`, or by `git branch -f` when it has no worktree. Moving a branch from an ancestor to a descendant loses no commit, since every commit of the old head is reachable from the new one, and the merge carries uncommitted work along, Build's plan and ledger included. Any other branch is kept as it is: one with commits of its own, a person's or a delivery's, even on an older base, and one ahead of the base. Local changes the fast-forward would overwrite, tracked, untracked or ignored, refuse the dispatch with git's message, nothing moved; the drain holds the ticket for ten minutes as after any refusal, and a person clears the way. Checked live on 2026-10-14 with git 2.54.0 in a linked worktree: that merge moves the branch and keeps an untracked file and an edit the base does not touch; it stops with exit 1, nothing moved, on an edit to a file the base changes, on an untracked file where the base adds one, and on an ignored file where the base adds one, which a merge without `--no-overwrite-ignore` replaced; `merge.autostash` and `branch.<name>.mergeOptions=--squash` do not take over; `git branch -f` refuses a branch a worktree has checked out. The executor's tests play it on the three platforms. Rejected: intake not keeping its branch, which leaves every other kept branch, such as a Build a usage limit cut off before its first commit, on its old base; making the worktree anew at the base as before a rebase run, which deletes uncommitted work and the Build's checkpoint; dispatching on the old branch with a warning when the fast-forward is refused, a Build without its blocker's code.
    - *Which commit configures a run* (OWL-331, decided 2026-10-17 by the maintainer). A branch with commits of its own stays on the base it was made from, so the code a resumed Build works on and the default branch can differ. Found on Locary's LOC-1407 during OWL-309: `continue` ran a gate that the new base added, `sh ci/owlshift/gate.sh`, on a branch made from an older base without that script, so the Build stopped `blocked` (exit 127) until the branch was rebased by hand. A run's configuration therefore follows its code. At each dispatch, the runner takes the project file (`owlshift.toml`) and the rule files at the commit the ticket's branch was made from: `git merge-base` of the branch and the base just fetched, in the dedicated checkout. That covers `stack.gate`, `stack.setup` (OWL-330), `stack.rules` and `[roles.<role>].rules`, and the executor's own gate run takes the same gate and setup. A branch that does not exist yet takes the fetched base, as it starts there; a rebase run takes the base it rebases onto. A fix, a correction, a review loop and a resumed intake take their branch's merge-base. That commit is always one the default branch held, never one of the branch's own, which agents write. The commit's project file wins over the person's own, on a first Build too, so a gate edited in the person's checkout applies once merged. The person's file is used only when that commit has none, as in a project adopting Owlshift. Zone owners, admitters and `[forge].reviewers` stay read at the fetched base, since they say who decides, not what the code runs; the policy, the pipeline, the models, `stack.gate_env` and `stack.caches` (OWL-330) stay the person's file's. The `dispatch` event names the commit (`configured_at`) and the file's source (`project_file`: `commit` or `local`). A branch that shares no history with the base refuses the dispatch. Accepted residual: the agent environment is made once per command from the person's `stack.gate_env`, so a commit declaring other names is a `warning` event (`gate_env_differs`) and the run goes on with the person's names. Likewise the sandbox opens the caches of the person's `stack.caches`, checked against `allow_caches` when the configuration is loaded, so a commit declaring other caches is a `warning` event (`caches_differ`), which `owlshift gate` prints, and the run goes on with the person's caches. Changes merged during the ticket reach it once it is delivered: CI runs the new gate on the pull request, and Watch's rebase run ("Conflicts and the rebase run") repairs a conflict or a check that fails. A branch with no commits of its own that a person's `continue` resumes is not fast-forwarded, and keeps the configuration of its older base too; watch's step into Build fast-forwards it, as the dispatch to Build does (OWL-367, "One step a pass"). Rejected: rebasing the branch onto the new base before the Build resumes, which costs an agent run on nearly every resume, sends a conflict to the decider in the middle of a Build, and rewrites the commits the Build's ledger names, so that finished steps would be redone. A Build held on a broken commit is the one exception (OWL-427): it is rebased once a newer head passes, since it cannot go on otherwise ("Parallel", "A broken base", "Leaving a broken commit"). Also rejected: reading the gate on the ticket's branch, where a run could choose the gate that judges it, and keeping the person's checkout as the source, which a long `watch` reads once and which can be at any commit.
  - *Ready, without a model* (S3). A ticket is dispatched when, in this order: it is admitted and not excluded; it has a decider (section 4); its intake record holds its current text; it neither waits nor is parked, and its ticket ref is at Ready, or at Watch with its following ended, which `do` would restart from Ready too; every blocker released it; the open pull requests are under their cap; and the slot is free. The core's ready set computes it from plain data, with one reason per waiting ticket.
  - *One at a time.* From P6 the one slot is a count, `policy.max_in_flight`, 1 when absent, so this rule is a project's that does not set it (section 5, "Parallel"). The slot is taken by any ticket of the project whose ref is at a stage after Ready and before Watch, whatever command dispatched it. A ticket left at Build by a usage limit, a failed delivery or a refused push keeps it until a person types `continue`, or until watch resumes it, which since OWL-367 it does whatever its round (OWL-340 resumed one with an answered round only; "Which tickets", above). A ticket waiting for an answer, parked or at Watch does not hold it, so one question never stalls the drain. Since OWL-367 watch works a ticket one stage step a pass, so several refs may hold the slot between two passes, a ticket whose answer counted while another's steps ran among them: they all hold it here, so no ticket is dispatched from Ready meanwhile, and watch runs their steps in one ticket's turn ("One step a pass", above). A ticket that becomes ready while another is built waits for the first pass after the slot frees; nothing is preempted, so a more urgent one is simply first in line. A ref that cannot be read, or whose state does not read, holds it too, since nothing tells it does not. Decided on 2026-10-15 (OWL-291), after four refs left at Verify since P2's gate held it for 13 minutes of the P4 gate with nothing naming them: the wait names the tickets that hold the slot ("Why a ticket waits", below), and `owlshift doctor`'s `drain slot` line warns of every ticket ref that holds it, read from the dedicated checkout without the lock, naming each with its stage and the command that clears it: `owlshift continue` for one left at Build, `owlshift do` to run one at another stage again, `owlshift forget` to start any of them over, the only one for a ref that does not read. One a command is working on now is named too, so the warning says so. Since OWL-401 it warns only once the refs that hold the slot reach `policy.max_in_flight`, when a `do` of another ticket waits too. Open: whether the slot should ignore a ref whose ticket the tracker shows completed or canceled.
  - *The dispatch.* At most one a pass until OWL-401, a list since, up to the project's capacity, each through `do`'s own path, its first stage step only since OWL-367 ("One step a pass", above), with its admission (section 5, "Parallel", "Admission, one transaction"), and only while the ticket ref is still the one the snapshot read and the ticket, read again, still shows the ready state and the gesture. Its `dispatch` event names `command` = `drain`.
  - *Why a ticket waits.* Each change of a ticket's reason is one `decision` event (`drain` = `waits`, with the reason) and one line, not one per pass, as `stage_moved` is (OWL-219); `owlshift why` (P6, section 5, "Parallel") reads them, a collision among their reasons from P6. The tickets a reason names are in the event's `tickets`, in identifier order, and on the line: the blockers that hold it, or the tickets that hold the slot, the one dispatched in the same pass included (OWL-291), so a change of holder is told again.
  - *Told on the ticket* (OWL-419, decided 2026-10-21). A ticket in flight whose next stage step watch holds back shows In Progress, as one whose run works, so the wait is also said on the ticket: one short WAITING comment per change of reason, in plain words (`owlshift why`'s phrase), naming what it waits on, and saying that it goes on by itself. Found on Locary's LOC-1425 during OWL-408, held back by a collision with LOC-1011 over `backend/leases` with no sign on the tracker. Three reasons post one, each a wait that can last a run or more: a collision, with the holder and the resource; the project's capacity, with the tickets that hold it, a change of holders being a change of reason as for the `decision` events; a harness at its usage limit, with the reset time known when it was first told, the harness alone being the reason, so a probe's new deadline every 30 minutes posts nothing. Since OWL-418 a fourth does: a broken base, the commit alone being the reason, so a re-check that still fails posts nothing ("A broken base", below). The rest post none: the work outside Owlshift that could not be read, a read failure the operator sees, which leaves the comment shown as it is; a machine slot (OWL-409), seconds; a busy lock; the holds after a refusal. The reason's kind gates it, not a time threshold. A ticket that waits at Ready posts nothing: it shows ready, and a blocker, which holds a ticket only at Ready (the dispatch checks it, not the stage steps), is a relation the tracker shows; only the quarantine tells a ready ticket, and folds that comment once cleared (section 8, OWL-423). The WAITING comment shown is kept in the ticket's folder (`waiting.json`: the reason and the comment's id), written once it is posted and removed once it is folded, so a watch started again posts nothing for a reason already shown and folds one whose wait ended meanwhile. A new reason posts its comment and folds the one shown; the end of the wait, when the step goes on or the ticket leaves the in-flight set, folds it and posts nothing (on Linear, resolved as superseded comments are, "Folded when superseded"; a tracker that cannot fold leaves it expanded). Each comment is resolved once at most. A comment that could not be posted is a `warning` event and is not tried again until the reason changes. The visible stage stays In Progress. Rejected: editing one comment in place, which needs a tracker write the runner does not make and makes the comment one the runner no longer takes as its own; recognising the comment again from its text after a restart, which cannot tell a folded comment from a shown one; a time threshold, which needs a clock per ticket and still posts a wait that ends a pass later.
- **Intake** (S3).
  - *When.* For an admitted ticket with a decider, in the ready column, not excluded, whose ref is absent, at Ready, or at Watch with its following ended and holds no intake record or one for another text: a SHA-256 of the title, a NUL byte and the description; or whose ref is at Intake, whatever its record. The state is put back at Intake keeping its round count, as `do` puts it back at Ready, so the core's rule that a ticket at Intake has asked no round becomes a rule on the rounds asked since; a ref already at Intake keeps its state, its failed runs counting against the stage's bound as under `continue`. Decided on 2026-10-14 (OWL-275): a ref at Intake is an intake that has not ended, since one that ends `done` leaves the ref at Ready and one that asks leaves it waiting, so a record holding the text there is a round's, not an ended intake's. In the column, it is the drain's own intake cut off, or a ticket an answered round left at Intake that a person moved back to the ready column, the person's gesture that restarts it ("Which tickets", OWL-122), whatever the round. The core's ready set never dispatches it (its ref is not at Ready); the drain takes it in first. One intake a pass, before the dispatch, highest priority first, whatever its blockers and the caps, so its questions reach the decider before the ticket can block anything (principle 4). Once dispatched, a ticket's text is read by every Build brief, and intake does not run again.
  - *The run.* The `intake` role (`roles/intake.md`), read-only and without network, on the standard tier (section 9), in a worktree at the base commit. Its brief holds the ticket, its thread, its decider, the project's rules and, since brief format 26 (OWL-416), the ticket's relations on the tracker: the tickets that block it, those it blocks and those related to it, each with its title and its state's name and type. Only intake's brief holds them. They are read for each run through `Tracker::links`, a page of each direction on Linear, and a read that fails gives the run none, with a `warning` event (`relations_not_read`), since they inform the role and decide nothing. It ends `done` or `questions`, both carrying its record: the variant, the routing verdict (harness, tier, effort) and the dependencies it found, each a ticket identifier with the words that name it. A dependency named in prose without an identifier is a question, never a guess, unless a ticket that blocks it by relation covers it by its title: the relation settles it. Decided on 2026-10-21 (OWL-416): on Locary, five intakes that day asked the decider "which ticket provides X" for prerequisites Linear already held as `blocks` relations, each costing a round and a ticket in Needs Input. A ticket it blocks or one related to it is never a dependency because of that link. `blocked` and failed runs go as any run's, and the second failed run parks the ticket at Intake, which `continue` restarts by running intake again. A false premise (S8) ends `premise_false`, with no record, and is a round or a park as section 5's "A false premise" says.
  - *Its questions* go through the resolver first, as Build's do, then to the decider in a round whose return stage is Intake. The record is kept with the round, its dependencies related, and once the round is answered intake runs again, the answers in its thread, before the ticket goes back to the ready column: that run's record replaces the round's, its dependencies checked and related as any kept verdict's ("Dependencies to relations", below). The Build reads the answers in its thread. Decided on 2026-10-14 by the maintainer (OWL-269): a prerequisite the decider names only in an answer ("it is OWL-12, it must land first"), the very answer intake asks for when the text names one without its identifier, was recorded nowhere, so no relation held the ticket and the drain could dispatch it before its blocker; the text convention reads the description's "Blocked by" lines, never a comment. It costs one more intake run, on the standard tier, per answered round, whose answers may refine the variant and the routing too. While it runs the ref is at Intake, never dispatched, and the ticket keeps the needs-input stage, as a park at Intake keeps its stage, until the record is kept and the ticket is moved back. A usage limit or a refusal before then leaves it there, out of the column the drain reads; `do` would skip intake. Decided on 2026-10-14 (OWL-273): watch resumes it, with or without `--drain`, with no `continue` typed. A ticket whose ref is at Intake after a question round, and which the tracker, read again under the project's lock, still shows as needs input, has its intake run again through `continue`'s path, held as any continue is: ten minutes after a resume that left the ref as it was, until the reset of a usage limit, until a person acts on a state not kept. A ticket at Intake shown otherwise is left alone: in the ready column it is the drain's, taken in again whatever its record ("When", above, OWL-275), and plain watch leaves it as it leaves the column; moved elsewhere by a person, it is theirs; parked, a person restarts it. Its stage is read without the lock first, and once it shows another stage, not again for ten minutes or until its ref moves. The bound stays the stage's, as below: a failed run of the resume counts, a usage limit does not, a refusal moves nothing. A crash still leaves the project's marker, which a person clears first. Unlike a Build left at round 0 until OWL-367, which only `owlshift continue` resumed (OWL-340 widened this rule to the later stages with an answered round (round > 0), OWL-367 to every round, "Which tickets", above), this ticket shows needs input while nothing is asked of anyone, so nothing told a person to act. Accepted residuals: on a project with no `parked` state, parked shows as needs input, so watch also resumes an intake that a person's `continue` of a park at Intake started and a usage limit cut off; a usage limit with no reset time keeps the state, which moves the ref, so the resume runs again at the next pass rather than ten minutes later, as the drain's own intake does. Rejected: the drain's pass resuming it, which plain watch, having run the answer check that started the intake, could not finish, and which the drain cannot see out of its column; watch telling the case from the ref alone, its record holding the current text, which loses a ticket whose text was edited during the round and cannot tell the drain's own cut-off intake on a ticket with earlier rounds (the drain, OWL-275, need not tell them apart: in its column both are due); a marker in the ticket ref, a format change for what the visible stage already says; counting each resume as a failed run, which parks a ticket on a usage limit. Rounds are unlimited, as at any stage (S2): the role asks again only what the answers leave open. A failed run counts against the stage's bound across the round, as at Build: a failed intake run or answer check before the answer and one failed run after it park the ticket at Intake. A relation written for the round's record stays when the next record no longer names the dependency, since the runner never removes one: a person does. A ref whose round was asked at Intake before OWL-269 returns to Ready, as it did, without the run. Rejected: the answer check listing the prerequisites the answers name, which gives the check a second job, a result field of its own, and the rule of what a prerequisite is in two prompts; intake run again with the ref left at Ready, which a cut-off leaves dispatchable; leaving it to the Build (P5), which is dispatched before the blocker, the failure itself; asking the decider to write a "Blocked by" line in the description, which an edit already turns into a new intake that the drain reads each pass, but which loses without a word an answer given in a comment.
  - *The record* is `intake.json` in the ticket ref: the text's hash, the gesture and its account, the routing verdict and the dependencies with what became of each. An `[owlshift] INTAKE` comment shows the verdict and the dependencies on the ticket, as section 9 asks of the verdict. In P4 the verdict is recorded and shown, and the Build runs as `do`'s does; per-ticket routing follows it from P5, labels overriding it then.
  - *Dependencies to relations.* For each dependency the runner reads the ticket named. It writes a `blocks` relation from it to the ticket through the Writer, unless one exists, the identifier is unknown or the ticket itself, the dependency is already done, or it is already blocked, directly or through its own blockers up to ten deep, by the ticket, a cycle. Each case but the first is named in the INTAKE comment, and no relation is written. Agents never write one: they hold no tracker credential. A dependency has two sources since OWL-416, decided on 2026-10-21 by the maintainer: the verdict, each one quoted from the text, and the tickets that already block the ticket by relation, which the runner adds itself after the verdict's, compared by the tracker's identifiers, with the outcome "relation already there" and the quote "a blocks relation on the tracker", read with the ticket's own blockers after the run. The record holds at most 64; blockers past that are left out of it with a `dependencies_cut` warning, the tracker still holding them. The result format and the refusal of a dependency the text does not name stay as they were: a relation needs no quote, and the role does not copy what the runner knows. Rejected: the role recording them with a `relation` source and no quote, which needs a result format, a wider refusal, and a model copying the runner's read. The check is made on the verdict intake keeps, at its end or its round, never on one the resolver's decisions send back to a new run, so the record never holds a dependency `pending`. A read or a write that fails otherwise than with the tracker's "not found" is a failed intake run, with a `warning` event (`relations_not_written`) giving the reason, decided on 2026-10-14 (OWL-249): one more run, then the ticket parks at Intake, as any failure inside a pass. The second run is a model run spent on a tracker failure, the price of a bound. Rejected: refusing the pass and running intake again at the next, which, intake going first by priority each pass, starves every other ticket's intake while the failure lasts; recording the dependency `pending` for the drain to hold and retry, which changes the ready set's input.
  - *`do`* skips intake, as before: a person's explicit run is no scheduler's dispatch.
  - *Rejected.* Intake at the ticket's turn only, which holds its questions until then; no INTAKE comment, which leaves the verdict off the ticket; following the verdict in P4, which is P5's per-ticket routing.
- **Back to the ready column.** An intake that ends at Ready, a `done` run, the one after an answered round included (OWL-269), or the RESUME of a round asked before it, moves the ticket's visible stage back to `[tracker].states.ready`, so the drain reads it again. These are the only moves to ready the runner makes, the exception to the rule "Ready is a person's gesture, never the runner's" (`VisibleStage`): they restore the stage the person chose, which a round or a park took away, and a state gesture is read past them ("A state gesture", above). So the move is made only while the tracker still shows the state the runner set, needs input or parked, as watch's move to review is (OWL-242): a ticket already in the column gets no write, and a stage a person chose meanwhile, Canceled or Done, is kept. Decided on 2026-10-13 by the maintainer (OWL-248), widening the RESUME alone, after which a ticket parked at Intake and restarted by `continue` stayed out of the column. Rejected: showing it as working, which says In Progress of a ticket that waits for its turn, and takes it out of the column the drain reads; leaving the move after a park to the person, which leaves a ticket that passed its intake out of the drain with nothing saying why.
- **Back-pressure** (S6).
  - *The cap.* `policy.max_open_prs` in `owlshift.toml`, a whole number from 1 to 20, 3 when absent. The count is the project's ticket refs whose delivery is still followed, an open pull request Owlshift delivered, whatever its ticket's state: waiting and parked tickets count. No forge call: watch ends a following at the merge or the close. It counts on every such end being read, a parked ticket's included, which OWL-243 settles. "Awaiting review" means open: GitHub's `reviewDecision` was empty on this repository's open pull request on 2026-10-13, since no review is required.
  - *At the cap* no ticket is dispatched; intake goes on. That reads S6's "nothing new starts" as no new Build: intake opens no pull request, and its questions are better asked while the decider reviews. One `decision` event (`drain` = `pr_cap`, with the count) and one line when the cap is reached, none per pass. No notification: S6's list has no cap, and the daily digest is P7's.
  - *Rejected.* Counting the forge's open pull requests, which counts a person's own; a notification at the cap.
- **Follow-ups** (S13).
  - *Which.* The `followups` of the delivering Build, and of fix, rebase and correction runs whose push landed.
  - *Owed, then filed.* They are kept in the ticket ref (`proposals.json`) as owed, in the very write that keeps the delivery or the repair, as its report is ("The owed report"). After the push, before the DELIVERY report, the runner files each and keeps it as filed, so the report names what became of them; a filing the tracker refuses stays owed, with one warning until its reason changes, and a later pass files it, posting no second report. Filing comes after the push, so it never delays the ticket. Exactly once: before filing, the runner looks for the proposal's fingerprint, and one already on the tracker whose footer names the same parent counts as filed, a crash having cut the write that keeps it. As built (OWL-253, 2026-10-14): every owed proposal is searched before any is filed, so one a crash left unkept takes its place among the three, and a search that fails holds its place, nothing being filed into it that pass. A push a watch pass adopts owes its follow-ups from its owed report at that pass's filing, before the report is posted, since `follow::changed` keeps no proposals; `do` and `continue` owe them in the write that keeps the push, with those of the unposted report it replaces. A tracker without a proposals inbox owes nothing: its report lists the follow-ups as not filed, as before; the Markdown tracker has one since OWL-267 ("Where", below). A ticket ref keeps at most 64 proposals in 512 KiB; a follow-up past either is not owed, and its report says it is not filed. The pass that reads the end of the following still files what is owed; a filing refused then stays owed, since no pass reads the ticket again. Residuals: a report adopted and replaced before any pass owed its follow-ups, which takes two pushes cut short in a row, loses them; a proposal filed, not kept, and completed before the next pass is filed again, since the search leaves completed tickets out.
  - *Where.* The tracker's proposals inbox: on Linear, the team's state of type `triage` (team `OWL` has Triage on); without one, the team's first backlog state with an `agent:proposed` label (section 6). Issue creation straight into Triage through the API key was checked for C4 (OWL-141). The Writer refuses, as part of the floor, a proposal in the ready state or carrying the admission label, so a proposal never enters the queue without a person (principle 6), and no intake runs before one promotes it. As built (OWL-253): the floor refuses every proposal when the admission label is `agent:proposed` in any case, the one label a proposal may carry, since the Writer cannot know beforehand whether the inbox is the labelled backlog; the ready state is refused by the adapter, which knows the target state, before any write. On the Markdown tracker (OWL-267, decided 2026-10-14), the inbox is the stage `Triage`, a name fixed as `Done` is, since its stages have no types: a proposal is a new ticket folder after its parent's key's highest number, authored `owlshift`, its `related` front-matter key naming the parent and its `blocked_by` the parent when it needs its code ([build plan](build-plan.md), "The test tracker"). The folder lands in the tree the tracker reads, the checkout for `do` and `watch`, for the person to commit, as comments do. A ready state named `Triage` is refused by the adapter, and doctor warns of it. The Markdown gesture being the ticket's author, the person who accepts a proposal also makes it theirs by setting `author`; until then it is never admitted. Rejected: a `[tracker].states.triage` key, a contract change for a test tracker; the footer alone as the relation to the parent, which a scenario cannot read as one.
  - *What.* The title, why, evidence (`file:line`), source (agent, reviewer, CI) and definition of done, the parent ticket and its pull request, a line `**Blocked by** <parent>, whose code it needs.` when the proposal needs its code (OWL-268, below), and a footer `[owlshift] PROPOSAL · <parent> · fingerprint <12 hex digits>`; one relation to the parent, a `blocks` relation from it when the proposal needs its code, else a `related` one; the parent's project. No label, priority or estimate: the person who accepts it sets them. One relation, since Linear keeps one between two issues: checked on 2026-10-15 (OWL-292, [build plan](build-plan.md), part I), a `related` and a `blocks` write on the same pair each answered success, in either order, and only the later one was left, which is how the P4 exit gate's proposals lost their `related` relation. The Markdown tracker's `related` and `blocked_by` keys are independent, so it writes both ("Where", above). Rejected: writing both, `related` first, which leaves `blocks` alone while the runner believes it wrote two; `blocks` first, whose `related` write replaces the relation the drain reads.
  - *A relation lost after filing* (OWL-268, decided 2026-10-14). On Linear the relation is a write of its own once the issue exists, `IssueCreateInput` having no relation field (checked 2026-10-13, OWL-252), so it can fail after the proposal is filed; the Markdown tracker writes `related` and `blocked_by` with the ticket folder. A relation not written is a `proposal_incomplete` warning, and the proposal counts as filed, since the search would find it at the next pass. The runner never writes a lost relation again. A lost `blocks` relation is the one that matters, and the description does its work: the `**Blocked by**` line names the parent, which the drain reads as a blocker ("Blockers") whatever became of the relation. A proposal in the inbox is not in the ready column, so nothing needs to hold it there; once a person promotes it, the drain holds it until its parent is completed or its pull request merged, as the relation would. The person triaging reads the dependency on the proposal itself, where the warning, in the event log of an unattended drain, reaches no one. Its intake reads the line as a prerequisite (`roles/intake.md`), and the runner writes the missing relation as it relates any dependency ("Dependencies to relations", OWL-249), unless the parent is done by then, when the line releases the ticket anyway; were intake not to name it, the line alone still holds the ticket. A lost `related` relation, that of a proposal not needing its parent's code, loses nothing the description does not say: its "Proposed by" line and its footer name the parent. The cost: a person who drops a proposal's dependency on a canceled parent removes the line as well as the relation, as on any ticket. Rejected: writing a lost relation again on a later pass, which needs a new format of `proposals.json` to keep what is missing, a `related` write the tracker contract lacks and a retry with its warnings, still loses the relation once the parent's following ends, since no pass reads that ticket again, and leaves open the time between the failure and the repair, in which a person may promote the proposal; naming the lost relation in the parent's DELIVERY report, which the person triaging does not read beside the proposal and which needs the same new format.
  - *Not twice.* The fingerprint is a SHA-256 of the title, lowercased, with every run of characters other than ASCII letters and digits made one space and the spaces at either end dropped (OWL-253), its first 12 hexadecimal digits. A proposal is a duplicate when a ticket of the team not completed has the same title in any case, or carries the same fingerprint in its description: open, in Triage, or declined, Linear's decline being a canceled or duplicate state. A completed one does not stop it, since the issue came back after it was done. A duplicate is named in the parent's report ("already proposed as OWL-n"), never commented on: Owlshift only touches the tickets it took. At most three are filed per parent, over all its runs; the rest are listed in the report. These are exact matches: a theme worded otherwise passes, and the person declines it in one gesture as a duplicate.
  - *The residue of a red check* past P3's bound stays a park (above): a check that must pass is the ticket's own acceptance, and S13 forbids deferring that. The build role keeps the rule that its own acceptance criteria are questions, never follow-ups; the runner cannot see a breach of it.
  - *The free plan.* Linear's free plan holds 250 active issues; Owlshift's workspace held 234 on 2026-10-13, 217 of them closed, which the team archives a month after they close. A proposal the plan refuses stays owed, as any refusal.
  - *Rejected.* A model run per proposal to find duplicates worded otherwise, which spends a run on what the person triaging settles in one gesture (P5 may add one to its review roles); a comment on the existing ticket; filing before the push.

**The quality pipeline.** Decided on 2026-10-15 (OWL-296), roadmap P5, scenarios S8, S9 and S17, with the maintainer for the four forks marked below. From P5 a ticket goes through the stages of its variant (section 4) instead of the Build alone, so that its pull request is at least as good as those of the `/parallel` workflow that built Owlshift's own backlog until P4: a plan before code, the plan and the diff reviewed by fresh runs on another model, and each finding settled before delivery. What depends on Codex or GitHub below is a pending live check, the first step of the part that depends on it ([build plan](build-plan.md), "P5 · Quality pipeline").

- **The roster.**

  | Variant | Design | Design review | Verify |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | `trivial` | none | none | one reviewer: the critic |
  | `standard` | one run | two: the critic and conformance | one: the critic |
  | `risky` | one run | three: the critic, conformance and debt | two: the critic and conformance |

  Every reviewer of the table runs on Claude Code (OWL-347, below); a project may add one Codex reviewer to each pass (OWL-348, below).

  - *The mandates.* The critic looks for what breaks the work: a premise that does not hold, a case left out, a simpler way. Conformance checks the plan or the diff against the ticket's criteria, the decider's answers and the project's rules. Debt looks at what the change does to the code around it: a primitive duplicated, a pattern bypassed, a test that tests nothing. They are the three mandates of the `/parallel` workflow's review panel, which runs two of them on a standard ticket and all three on a risky one. A critic alone in its pass holds conformance too.
  - *The variant* is intake's verdict; a `variant:<name>` label on the ticket overrides it, read at dispatch.
  - *Another model.* Every reviewer, the critic included, runs on Claude Code, as Design and Build do (decision D4), on another model than the author's when the configuration names one: the `deep` tier's, or the `standard` tier's when the author ran on `deep`. When both name the author's model or none, as with a configuration that names no model, the reviewer is a fresh run of the same model, and the report says so.
  - *Codex.* Decided by the maintainer on 2026-10-18 (OWL-347): Owlshift must not require subscriptions with several providers, so no review needs Codex. From OWL-303 to OWL-347 the critic ran on Codex, a reviewer of another model family than the author's, and stood in on Claude Code where Codex was not usable; on 2026-10-18 a Codex quota ran out and four tickets waited at their design review for more than two hours while Claude Code had capacity. Runs no longer look for Codex: a missing, logged-out or rate-limited Codex changes no review, and no report names a stand-in. The Codex harness ("The Codex harness", below) and its guards stay in the code, unwired from the roster, so that a reviewer of another family can come back as an opt-in complement (OWL-348) under S17's rule, whenever one is available and configured.
  - *The opt-in Codex reviewer.* Decided on 2026-10-20 (OWL-348): `pipeline.codex_reviewer = true` in `owlshift.toml`, off when absent, adds one Codex reviewer to every Design review and Verify pass, after the table's Claude Code seats, which it never replaces. It holds the critic's mandate, S17's reason for a reviewer of another family, so it may raise what the Claude Code critic raises too; its findings are numbered after theirs, at most what `MAX_PASS_FINDINGS` leaves, and confirmed or answered with them. It runs with the stage's own prompt on Codex's standard tier at the ticket's effort ("Per-ticket routing", below). Whether Codex can serve is asked at each command's start: `codex` on the `PATH` when the process started, its login file, the machine's requirements allowing `danger-full-access` ("The Codex harness", below) and the harness's own check. A Codex that cannot serve, or whose run cannot start, fails, stops at its usage limit, returns a refused or `blocked` result or asks, drops the seat for that pass: no run again, no failed run counted, no stop, and after a usage limit the command's later passes run without it too, a later command trying again; since OWL-404 a later command finds Codex held by the machine's usage state until the limit's end, and drops the seat before any run, told to no one. Each such pass keeps why in `review.json` (its `dropped`), in the runner's words, never Codex's output; a `warning` event says it, the PLAN comment (or the plan's document) and the DELIVERY report list each pass without it. Two rules stay any run's: the ticket's budget is checked before the run, and a run that breaks isolation quarantines the ticket; what every run shares, a credential within the agent's reach or the ticket's worktree, stops the command as for any run. Codex reports no cost: its runs count as runs without a cost in the ticket's spend and stay out of the review chain's sum, and only the ticket's budget and the run's deadline bound one, the personal file's `budget_usd` being Claude Code's. A `continue` that runs a pass cut short again runs its Codex seat again. `owlshift doctor` reports the option and whether Codex can serve it. Rejected: a Codex seat inside the table, which brought back the machine parameter OWL-347 removed for a seat that is not the variant's, and Codex taking a Claude Code seat when usable, the arrangement OWL-347 ended.
- **Design.**
  - *The run.* The `design` role, read-only, with its web tools unless the operator turns them off (section 8, OWL-415), on the ticket's routing (below), in a worktree at the base commit fetched when the ticket is dispatched from Ready: OWL-274's fast-forward of the base is made at the dispatch from Ready, whichever stage follows it, and again at the dispatch to Build. Its brief holds the ticket, its thread, its decider, the rules and, after a review pass, the confirmed findings.
  - *The plan* it returns: the goal, the success criteria taken from the ticket, the approach and the alternatives set aside, the files, ordered steps each with its test, the risks, what is out of scope, and its `forks`.
  - *Forks and questions.* What the work delivers (content, behaviour, interface, wording) and every point the ticket leaves open stays a question, through the resolver, as for Build (section 4, OWL-176): the design decides none of it. A fork is a choice of how, between approaches that would each deliver the ticket, which the design makes and names with the alternatives and its reason; `on-fork` plan approval reads them.
  - *Kept and shown.* The runner keeps the plan in the ticket ref (`plan.json`), with the hash of the ticket's text and the base commit it was written on, and posts it on the ticket as a `[owlshift] PLAN` comment: the approach, the steps, the forks, the findings left and the reviewers who ran. Since OWL-350, on a tracker that holds documents, the plan whole and its review log (each Design review pass of the loop, its reviewers, the findings left and those rejected with their reasons) go in one document attached to the ticket, "Plan · <ticket>", created at the first PLAN comment and updated in place at each one after, its id kept in `plan.json`; the PLAN comment is then a pointer of about 800 characters at most: the plan's version (a new one only when the plan's text changed) and the pass, the link to the document, what changed since the previous version in at most three bullets naming the parts of the plan, the number of forks and of findings open, and the next step. A comment thread of 40,000 to 65,000-character PLAN comments, re-posted at each pass, could not be followed or answered (OWL-160 on 2026-10-18). A tracker without documents gets the whole plan in the comment, as before; a document that cannot be published stops the command as a PLAN comment that cannot be posted does. The approval binds the plan in `plan.json`, never the document, which no run reads back. The Build's brief carries the plan and its base commit, without the plan's `forks` and `alternatives` since OWL-365 (decided with the maintainer on 2026-10-19): they record how the design chose among approaches, while the choice itself is in the approach and the steps, which the design writes so that they stand without them, and the approval round's answers are in the plan Design revised before it was approved. A Build that cannot follow the plan asks its `replan` (below); the resolver that decides it and the Design run that follows read the plan whole, forks, options and reasons included, as do the reviewers, Verify, the arbiter and the answer check. The Build itself starts from the base fetched when it is dispatched, as OWL-274 has it, so a base that moved during a long approval is seen by the Build and does not invalidate the plan by itself.
- **Design review.** Each reviewer of the roster reads the plan, the ticket, its thread, the rules and the repository at the plan's base commit, read-only, with its web tools where its harness has them and the operator leaves them on (section 8, OWL-415; the opt-in Codex reviewer has none, "The Codex harness", below), and returns findings: a severity (`blocker`, `major`, `minor`), the claim, its evidence (a `file:line`, a step of the plan, a line of the ticket), the success criterion it bears on if any, and what would settle it. The findings are confirmed (below); confirmed `blocker` and `major` ones send the ticket back to Design, which revises the plan with them in its brief and answers each ("A finding goes to its author", below), and the revised plan is reviewed again by the same roster, on what changed ("A pass after a revision", below).
  - *Minor findings* (OWL-356, decided 2026-10-19). A confirmed `minor` never sends the plan back to Design, at any pass: it is a note for the Build. The arbiter rules on it as on any finding, a rejected one listed with its reason; the PLAN comment and the plan's document list the notes of every pass of the loop; the Build's brief carries them, findings mode `note`, which the Build weighs while it builds and answers none, its brief showing Owlshift's PLAN comment as its header line only (OWL-357). A pass whose only confirmed findings are `minor` passes, to the approval round or to Build. On the 2026-10-18 drain, fresh reviewers kept finding new minor points on each revised plan, up to 7 passes on OWL-339.
  - *The bound.* At most two review passes, S9's bound (`MAX_REVIEW_PASSES` in the core). A `blocker` or `major` finding still confirmed after the second goes back to Design for one last revision, which answers it as any revision does, and no review pass follows that revision: the plan goes to its approval round or to Build (OWL-377, "A finding goes to its author", below); a `minor` one is a note, as at every pass. Until OWL-377 it went to the decider in a question round, one question per finding, through the resolver first since OWL-342.
  - *After a round.* A round the design run asks, or an approval round whose answer changes the plan, returns to Design: the design role runs with the answers in its thread, and its plan gets one more review pass, not counted against the two. A `blocker` or `major` confirmed in that pass goes to one last revision, as after the second (OWL-377), rounds being unlimited (S2) while each answer buys one pass; a `minor` one is a note.
- **Plan approval.**
  - *When.* Once the review passes are over, by `GatePolicy::plan_approval_required`: always for `risky`; for `standard`, the project's `pipeline.plan_approval` (`on-fork`: the plan names a fork); never for `trivial`, which has no plan.
  - *How.* A question round straight to the decider (since OWL-342, through the resolver first under `on-fork`, which may approve at `all`; under `always` straight to the decider): one question, whether they approve the plan the PLAN comment shows (whole, or through its document's link), with its forks as the options to change; on a tracker with documents, two options, approve or change it, with the forks listed beneath and their options in the document (OWL-352; [build plan](build-plan.md), "The QUESTIONS layout"). Their answer goes through the answer check as any answer. The round returns to Design: the design role runs with the reply in its thread. An approval leaves the plan as it was and records it in `plan.json` (the decider's comment and the plan's hash), and the ticket goes to Build. A change revises the plan, which gets one review pass under "After the round" above and is asked again. Rounds are unlimited, as at any stage (S2).
  - *Held by the core.* When approval is required, a Build is never dispatched on a plan whose hash is not the approved one (OWL-32).
  - *A plan invalidated during Build.* A Build that cannot follow the plan (a step impossible, the approach wrong) asks, its question marked `replan`. On an approved plan it goes to the decider without the resolver, otherwise through the resolver; since OWL-342 always through the resolver. Once a `replan` question is answered or decided, whatever the answer, the runner sends the ticket back to Design, whose new plan is reviewed again, and approved again when approval is required; the new design run judges what the answer changes. On `trivial`, which has no Design, `replan` is read as a plain question and the round returns to Build.
- **Verify.**
  - *Before the pull request* (fork 1). Once the Build's gate has passed under the runner, and after the re-read for late comments (section 4, OWL-139) with the Build run it may add, before the push, the Verify roster reads the diff from the Build's base commit to the head, which the runner writes to a file named in the brief with that base (OWL-302), the plan, the ticket, its thread and the rules, read-only, with its web tools where its harness has them and the operator leaves them on (section 8, OWL-415), and returns findings of the same shape. The complete CI result stays Watch's, through P3's fix runs on a red check: Verify reviews the work, CI tests it. Watch's fix, rebase and correction runs keep P3's rules and get no Verify pass.
  - *The fix loop.* A confirmed `blocker` or `major` before the last pass sends the ticket back to Build: a Verify fix run, on the same branch with the pass's confirmed findings in its brief, its `minor` ones included, which it answers ("A finding goes to its author", below), then the gate, then Verify again. Its questions go as a Build's. At most two review passes.
  - *Minor findings* (OWL-356, decided 2026-10-19). A pass whose only confirmed findings to fix are `minor`, at any pass, a last one included, sends the ticket back to Build for one last fix: a Verify fix run given them, then the runner's gate, then the delivery, with no Verify pass after it (`Event::LastFix` in the core); the DELIVERY report lists them as given to the last Build. A `minor` on a success criterion goes the same way: it is fixed, not deferred, so S13 holds. The exemption covers that Build alone: when it asks a question, decided or answered, takes late comments, or follows a decider's reply to a DECISION comment, the review record drops its mark (`last_fix` in `review.json`) and a Verify pass follows, as after any fix run. So the resolver never sees a `minor` finding as a question: no round asks one, none spends a resolver pass (`policy.resolver_passes`, section 4) or reaches the decider, and the resolver's configuration is unchanged. On OWL-160's run of 2026-10-18, minor findings started two more Build, gate and Verify rounds of about 35 minutes each, the second ones asked and decided "apply the fix". Since OWL-377 the last pass's `blocker` and `major` findings join that last fix ("The residue", below).
  - *The residue* (OWL-377). A `blocker` or `major` finding still confirmed after the second pass goes, with the pass's `minor` ones, to the last fix above: one last Build given them, which answers each, then the runner's gate and the delivery, with no Verify pass after it. The DELIVERY report lists what that Build answered, and every finding a Build of the loop disagreed with or deferred, with its reason; the runner proposes no follow-up from a finding ("A finding goes to its author", below). Until OWL-377 a `blocker`, or a `major` on a success criterion, went to the decider in a question round, through the resolver first since OWL-342, and any other `major` became a proposed follow-up with the reviewer as its source.
  - *Rejected.* Pushing a draft pull request first and reviewing once its CI is complete, which adds a draft state and a CI wait to every pass for what Watch already repairs.
- **A pass after a revision** (OWL-356, decided 2026-10-19, its scope with the maintainer). Pass 2 and every later pass of a loop review what changed since the pass before, not the whole plan or diff again. The review brief's `since` gives, at Design review, the plan's parts that changed, the steps whose text is new, matched by content so that a step an insertion renumbers is not new, and how many steps of the previous plan have a text no longer there; at Verify, the diff from the commit the previous pass reviewed to the head, beside the whole diff. Its `earlier` gives every finding of the loop's earlier passes as the review record keeps it, with its outcome and the arbiter's ruling or the author's response, rejected ones included. A reviewer raises findings only on what changed, what that change breaks elsewhere included, and on whether the findings fixed since are settled; a rejected, noted, disagreed or deferred point is not raised again unless the change brings new evidence (OWL-377); an earlier `blocker` or `major` with no outcome is still open and checked again. For that, each pass keeps what it reviewed: the commit at Verify, a hash of each part and each step of the plan at Design review, `plan.json` holding only the current plan. The pass of Design's last revision (OWL-377) reviewed nothing and keeps no print: the pass after it, after an approval round that changed the plan, reviews what changed since the last pass that kept one. A pass whose previous one kept nothing of the kind (a record written before OWL-356), or whose previous commit is not an ancestor of the head, reviews whole, as before. The arbiter's brief is unchanged.
  - *Rejected.* A `state.json` flag for the last fix, a format change for what the review record holds; the previous plan whole in a later pass's brief, which doubles briefs of 40,000 to 65,000 characters when the steps' hashes name the new ones; `MAX_REVIEW_PASSES` at 1, which drops the second pass a `major` keeps; a `blocker` raised anywhere at a later pass, a safety valve the maintainer declined, fresh reviewers reopening the loop being what OWL-339 showed.
- **A finding goes to its author** (OWL-377, decided 2026-10-19 with the maintainer; designed with OWL-356, "Minor findings" and "A pass after a revision" above, which bound how often a review runs while this sets who owns a finding). In the `/parallel` workflow that built Owlshift's backlog, a reviewer's finding is the worker's business: it revises its plan for the blockers and the important ones, folds the nits into its risks or accepts them, and keeps its own choice with a recorded reason when it disagrees; a finding is never a question for the user. Owlshift asked instead: a finding two passes left was a question round through the resolver, and a decision to apply the fix restarted Design or Build and a new pass, which raised new findings. On 2026-10-18 and 19 that made up to 8 design review passes for one plan (OWL-339) and 3 Verify passes, and 28 of the decider's 29 replies over three days were "ok" or "take every recommendation".
  - *The rule.* A confirmed finding, the arbiter's or one the author did not reject (`pipeline.findings = "author"`), goes to the role that owns the work, with its severity: Design for the plan, Build for the code. No finding becomes a question by itself, and the runner asks none.
  - *What the author answers.* A run given findings to fix answers each one in its `finding_responses`, and the runner refuses a `done` result that leaves one out (`result.json` format 13), its retry told why (`result_refusal`, OWL-430, as for every refused design or review run): `fixed`; `disagreed`, the role keeping its choice, with a reason resting on what shows the finding wrong or not worth its cost (a `file:line`, a criterion, the decider's word), at any severity; or, from the Build and for a `minor` that bears on no success criterion, `deferred`, its reason saying where the point goes, a follow-up the run proposes or a note for the report. A `blocker` or `major` is fixed or disagreed with, and so is a `minor` on a criterion, never deferred (S13). At Design review a `minor` stays a note for the Build (OWL-356), which answers none. In author mode the answers stay `fixed` or `rejected`, the author being the only judge. A disagreement is not a rejection: the arbiter confirmed the finding, so it is listed as a choice the author kept against its reviewers, which the decider may read and overrule.
  - *Seen by the next pass.* The answer is kept with the finding in `review.json`, beside the arbiter's ruling, and the next pass's reviewers read it in their brief's `earlier` (brief format 22): a disagreed or deferred point is not raised again unless the change brings new evidence ("A pass after a revision").
  - *At the bound.* After the last pass (`MAX_REVIEW_PASSES`), the `blocker` and `major` findings left go to their author for one last run with no review pass after it. At Design review it is the last revision: Design runs once more given them, and the runner keeps its plan with one more Design review pass in `review.json` that names the design run (`revision`) and has no reviewer, no finding and no plan's print, so that the approval round after it reads as one after a passed pass; the Design review stage then runs no reviewer, posts the PLAN comment and goes to the approval round or to Build. At Verify it is the last fix (above). What the author answered is listed, never asked: the PLAN comment and the plan's document list the findings the last revision answered, with its reasons, and every finding a revision of the loop disagreed with; the DELIVERY report lists the findings the last Build answered and every finding a Build of the loop disagreed with or deferred, with their reasons. A last run that asks a question drops its mark, as OWL-356's last fix does: the round buys a reviewed pass.
  - *A real choice.* A role may still ask when a finding uncovers a choice that is the decider's: scope, a product trade-off, the floor. That question is the role's, Design's or Build's, routed as any (section 4, "Every question through the resolver"): one filed `scope` at a level that keeps scope goes to the decider without a run, any other to the resolver first, whose decision the level bounds, a resolver labelling it `scope` included. The `residue` event keeps its `asked` list, now empty: what a role asks is in its own run's events.
  - *Records written before.* Decided on 2026-10-20 (OWL-379), the decider answering that refs written before OWL-377 need no more reading: P5 is unreleased, so no period keeps them. A ref is no longer read for a residue decision or a residue question. A residue decision it holds is an ordinary kept decision, its reply followed by the next run as any other's (section 5, "A reversed decision"), and the resolver's brief has no per-question finding. A residue round still waiting when the runner is upgraded takes no special path: its author is run through the last-fix path every ticket takes, given the pass's findings to fix as at the bound (`design_input`, `verify_input`). The `asked` and `proposed` outcomes of `review.json` stay readable, the reports listing them, and this rule does not touch them.
  - *Rejected.* Listing the last pass's findings with no run of their author, which leaves the role neither fixing nor disagreeing with them; the resolver deciding a residue question, still a run, and a round whenever it passes the question on; `rejected` for the author's disagreement in arbiter mode, which hides from the decider that the arbiter confirmed the finding; a third review pass after the last revision, the loop OWL-339 showed.
- **Confirming a finding** (fork 2). `pipeline.findings` in `owlshift.toml`: `"author"`, the default since 2026-10-20 (OWL-391), or `"arbiter"`.
  - *Why the author.* The P5 exit gate's drains (2026-10-16 to 20, 11 tickets) ran the arbiter 90 times for 22 USD, 7% of the 330 USD spent, and it confirmed 498 findings and rejected 18 (3.5%): it almost never filters. Since OWL-377 the author answers every finding it is given, with a reason, so the arbiter's ruling and the author's answer judged the same finding twice; the `/parallel` workflow that built the backlog has no arbiter either. Decided by the maintainer on 2026-10-20; a project that wants the second judge sets `"arbiter"`.
  - *Arbiter (opt-in).* One `arbiter` run per review pass judges all its findings at once: read-only, with its web tools unless the operator turns them off (section 8, OWL-415), on the standard tier of the author's harness, a fresh run that wrote neither the work nor the findings. It merges the duplicates across reviewers and marks each finding `confirmed` or `rejected`, with a reason resting on what it checked in the repository; a reason that does not cite evidence confirms the finding. The rejected ones are listed, with their reasons, in the PLAN comment or the DELIVERY report.
  - *Author (default).* The next Design run or Verify fix run gets every finding and answers each one, fixed or rejected with its reason; the runner refuses a result that leaves one unanswered. A finding the author rejects is not raised again by the next pass's runner. Every one it rejects stays listed, with its reason, in the PLAN comment or the DELIVERY report as the arbiter's rejections are, so the decider can read and overrule it; in arbiter mode, a finding the author disagrees with or defers is listed with its reason as a choice it kept (OWL-377). A ticket whose review record holds the arbiter's rulings keeps them when the runner is upgraded: a finding the arbiter rejected stays rejected whatever the mode now.
  - *Rejected.* Every `blocker` and `major` finding fixed without a judgment, which spends fix passes on a reviewer's false positives, seen in `/parallel` runs.
- **Review comments on the pull request** (fork 3), roadmap P3's deferral.
  - *Whose.* `[forge].reviewers` in `owlshift.toml`, read at the base commit as admitters are, maps each forge login whose reviews are read to its tracker account. A review is an instruction when that account is the ticket's decider, resolved when the review is read, as for an answer (section 4, "Who decides"). A listed reviewer who is not the decider is context: their review starts nothing, reaches the briefs of the next review loop's runs, its Build and its Verify fix runs and, since OWL-396, its Verify reviewers and its arbiter, as quoted data, as a ticket comment of theirs would (section 8), and is recorded as answered with that loop's push or report. Decided by the maintainer on 2026-10-16 (OWL-307), narrowing "the brief of the next run on the ticket": P3's fix, rebase and correction runs read no review, and a context review alone waits for the decider's next review. Anyone else's review is ignored, neither instruction nor context: on a public repository anyone can review. P10's identity map replaces the list.
  - *Which.* A review submitted on a pull request Owlshift follows, after its delivery, with a body or inline comments, whose id the ticket ref has not recorded as answered. Its state does not matter: these pull requests are opened through the maintainer's own GitHub account, on which GitHub lets its owner comment but neither approve nor request changes (checked live on 2026-10-16, build plan check (c)). A review with neither body nor inline comment starts nothing; a body Owlshift wrote never counts. A submitted review is already a batch the reviewer chose to send, so no quiet window applies; a single comment on the pull request's conversation is not read, the ticket being the conversation.
  - *When.* At a pass of watch, for a ticket at Watch and not parked, once the pass found no fix or rebase due: P3's repairs come first, and a review waits for the next pass with none due.
  - *What it does.* It sends the ticket back to Build: in the same pass, as `continue` would, one Build run on the delivered branch for every review due, their ids recorded together, with the reviews' comments in its brief as the decider's instructions on the change, the gate, Verify, a fast-forward push to the same pull request and a DELIVERY report that names the review. A Build that commits nothing ends the loop with its summary in that report, and is no failure. The review's id is recorded as answered once its push lands or its report is posted. What it asks is a Build question, through the resolver as any, returning to Build. While the ticket is at Build or Verify, or waits or is parked there, with a followed delivery, watch still reads its pull request's merge, close and foreign push, which end the following as at Watch; such a Build holds the drain's slot as any Build. The loop is a person's doing: its push counts neither as a fix pass nor as a repair under P3's bounds, and each of its Verify passes is bounded as above. Since OWL-367 the loop starts only in a free turn of the slot, its Build and Verify pass are one stage step, and a Verify fix run is a later pass's ("One step a pass", above).
  - *As built* (OWL-307, 2026-10-16).
    - *The read.* One GraphQL query a pull request, at each pass whose read asks for no repair and runs no correction (a correction first): the newest 100 submitted reviews with their first 100 inline comments, every submitted state named so the token owner's pending review is never read; an inline comment a later push made outdated has no line. `owlshift continue` at Watch reads them too. Watch judges with `[forge].reviewers` as it loaded them and the ticket's assignee, read only once a listed reviewer's review is due; the loop judges again under the lock with the reviewers at the base commit and the decider resolved then, zone owners included. A login listed at the base but not in watch's working tree is ignored until watch restarts.
    - *After its delivery.* `delivery.json` keeps `delivered_at`, set when the first delivery's push is kept under way and kept by its retry and every later push; a delivery made before OWL-307 is dated by the first read of its reviews, and nothing is due at that pass.
