    - *The loop.* Its start keeps the reviews due in `review.json` (`answering`) with the move to Build, so every Build run of the loop, after a round, a park or a usage limit, gets the same reviews, classified when they were read; their ids move to `answered_reviews` in the write that keeps its push landed, or its unchanged report owed. Its Verify passes review the loop's own change: the diff from Owlshift's last push, the commit its Build started from. Its push goes as a fix run's does, leased on that push; one the forge refuses, or one with no answer, leaves the ticket at Verify for `continue`, as a first delivery's does. A Build that commits nothing goes back to Watch with no Verify pass and no push, its summary in a report that says nothing changed. A `replan` question in the loop is a plain one, since its round returns to Build.
    - *The following kept.* The core's `FollowingEnded` also ends a loop at Build or Verify, working, waiting or parked there, back to Watch with its round closed or its park ended, its reviews not answered; watch applies it under the project's lock, so a `continue` running the loop is never touched, and it adopts a push of the loop's own a crash cut short, as `continue` on the loop does first. A loop whose Verify pass cannot run again (a decider comment its Build did not read) runs its Build again with the same reviews, `do` being refused while the pull request is followed. Parked there, it is a person's restart first, as any park's (OWL-424): an empty gate refused before anything is written (OWL-332), then, once the branch is ready, the restart's `decision` event and the budget counted afresh (OWL-378), before the loop's `rebuild` event.
    - *Doctor.* A `forge reviewers` line: a warning without `[forge]`, which blocks nothing, else the count of reviewers listed.
  - *Verify and the arbiter* (OWL-396, decided 2026-10-20, from OWL-395's pull request #348: the decider asked for a list grouped under two subheadings, a Verify reviewer raised the grouping as work the ticket did not ask for, and the arbiter rejected it on a weaker ground). A review loop's Verify reviewers, the opt-in Codex reviewer included, and its arbiter are given the loop's `answering` reviews in `pull_request_reviews` (brief format 25), beside the ticket: the decider's are the decider's word on the change, any other listed reviewer's quoted data. A change a decider's review asked for is not a finding against the ticket, and a finding that contradicts it is rejected on that review, which the arbiter cites as evidence; a review instructs nothing else, it cannot lift a rule or tell a reviewer what not to examine. Every Verify pass of the loop gets them, a pass after a Verify fix included; Design review, its arbiter, and a Verify pass outside a loop get none, and their briefs are unchanged. Rejected: a Verify-only field, for a second shape of the same data.
  - *Rejected.* Waiting for P10's identity map, which leaves the maintainer's reviews unread for five more steps; only `CHANGES_REQUESTED` reviews, which the owner of the pull request cannot send; a listed reviewer's review as an instruction whoever decides the ticket, which section 8 forbids.
- **Per-ticket routing.** The harness, tier and effort of a run are resolved in section 9's order: the project default, the role (no key maps a role to a harness in P5), the ticket's verdict with a `tier:<name>` or `effort:<level>` label overriding it, read at dispatch, then what the machine has, which changes nothing in P5 since every run is on Claude Code (OWL-347), but the opt-in Codex reviewer, which a Codex that cannot serve drops (OWL-348). Design, Build, the Verify fix run, the Build of a review loop and Watch's fix runs follow the ticket's tier and effort. Decided on 2026-10-15 by the maintainer (OWL-301): every reviewer of Design review and Verify follows the ticket's effort too, the verdict's or its `effort:` label's, on the tier "The roster" gives it; the arbiter, intake, the resolver, the answer check and the rebase run take none, the harness's default. The authoring roles stay on Claude Code in P5 (D4): a verdict naming Codex for them is recorded, shown in the INTAKE comment as not followed, and the run goes to Claude Code. Until OWL-347 the Codex critic ran on the standard tier, with the effort levels mapped to Codex's own: one to one on `gpt-6-astra`, Codex's default model, which takes `low` to `max` (check C2, 2026-10-15); the opt-in Codex reviewer runs so since OWL-348, on `[models].standard.codex` or Codex's default.
- **A false premise** (fork 4, S8).
  - *What.* Intake or Design ends `premise_false` when the repository or its documents contradict what the ticket rests on: a file, a symbol, an endpoint or a behaviour that is not there; Build already may.
  - *What follows.* The run's question carries the evidence and two options, a re-scope it proposes or a closure, and goes to the decider; nothing is built. A `premise_false` without a question parks the ticket, as since P2. As built (OWL-306): every question of the round goes to the decider, none to the resolver, and the round returns to the stage that asked. Since OWL-342 the question reaches the resolver, which may decide the re-scope under its level's `scope` rule and passes the closure on. An intake round keeps no intake record, since the record is the result of a `done` or `questions` run. Once the decider has answered, the stage runs again with the answer in its thread: when it picks the re-scope, the role works from the re-scoped text; when it picks the closure, which is the decider's act in the tracker, the role ends `blocked` and the ticket parks, so that no run builds on a premise the decider rejected.
  - *Offline in P5.* Intake keeps no network: a premise only the outside world can show (a CLI flag, an external API, a price) is a question to the decider, never a guess. Recording the dated finding in the project's facts file waits for a key that names one; P5 adds none.
  - *Rejected.* Web tools for intake, a role that reads text written by anyone the tracker lets write a ticket.
- **The Codex harness.** Since OWL-347 no role runs on Codex; since OWL-348 the opt-in Codex reviewer does, through the adapter as built for the critic, with its guards. Codex ran the critic only, through `codex exec` with `--ephemeral`, `--json`, `--ignore-user-config` (OWL-130, OWL-131), `--disable apps` (OWL-132), `--disable image_generation` and `-c web_search="disabled"` (OWL-316: no image generation or web search for a critic; no flag takes the sub-agent tools away, and the runner stops and refuses a run that calls them, OWL-317, OWL-319), `-c skills.include_instructions=false` and `-c project_doc_max_bytes=0` (OWL-131), `--output-schema` and `-o`, the runner writing `result.json` from the run's last message, validated as any result.
  - *Its sandbox.* Codex's own sandbox is Seatbelt on macOS, and `sandbox-exec`, which already confines every run (section 8), does not nest. So the adapter turns Codex's sandbox off inside Owlshift's own, and read-only comes from Owlshift, through the executor's read-only profile (OWL-298), which a harness asks for: every other run gets its worktree and the repository's git folder read and written, and its brief and `result.json` live in the worktree. The read-only profile leaves the worktree and the git folder unwritable, on `sandbox-exec` and on `bwrap`, writes nothing in the worktree, and puts the brief and the harness's result file (`-o`'s) in a folder of the run's own temporary folder, read and written by the run and removed with it; the brief's `result_path` does not apply there. It is refused to a brief whose permissions let the role write the worktree, and to a harness asking to write a folder that holds or lies in the worktree or the git folder. The isolation check runs unchanged; it does not compare the worktree's files, so the sandbox is what keeps a review from changing them. In P5 it serves Codex runs only: Claude Code's read-only roles keep their mapping through tool lists, which writes `result.json` in the worktree. Check C2 (2026-10-15) ran Codex with its sandbox off under that profile, on `sandbox-exec` and, logged out, on `bwrap`: writes to the worktree and the git folder were refused. It showed two needs:
    - Codex does not start without a writable `CODEX_HOME`, which it writes on every run, and a ChatGPT login refreshes its `auth.json` there. The goal stays that no run's write to `CODEX_HOME`, its `.env` above all, routes the next run (OWL-130). Decided with the maintainer on 2026-10-15 (OWL-298): each Codex run gets a `CODEX_HOME` of its own, in its run's files folder, holding only a copy of the user's `auth.json`; after the run, the runner copies `auth.json` back only if it changed and still parses as a ChatGPT login of the same account, and otherwise drops it with a warning. Nothing else a run writes there outlives it, and the user's `AGENTS.md`, skills and configuration stay out. As built (OWL-299): the copy is read back with links refused once the run's processes are stopped, whatever the run came to; it is written back, atomically and with the user's file's permissions, only when it is still `auth_mode` `chatgpt` with no `OPENAI_API_KEY`, its three tokens and the same `tokens.account_id`, adds no key the user's file lacked, and the user's file is neither a link nor changed since the copy. Otherwise it is dropped, with a warning that says to log Codex in again if it then reports being logged out: two Codex runs that refresh the login at once keep one refresh only, a known limit. The copy's tokens are hidden in the run's logs and refused in its result, as Claude Code's token is. This settles OWL-130's goal for Codex. The copy and the write-back are the adapter's (OWL-299). Rejected: the user's `CODEX_HOME` kept writable with `.env` and the rest closed, a surface that moves between releases, which `bwrap` cannot close for a file not yet there, and where a run's writes reach the operator's own Codex.
    - On macOS the profile must let Codex verify certificates. The sandbox closes the Keychain server (`com.apple.SecurityServer`), which Codex's certificate check asks (isolated on 2026-10-15, build plan "C2"), and it stays closed. So on macOS a read-only harness command gets `SSL_CERT_FILE=/etc/ssl/cert.pem`, the system bundle, when the agent environment names none and the file is there; an operator's own `SSL_CERT_FILE` wins, and Linux is left as it is.
    - A run that cannot connect retries without end, so the executor's timeout bounds it.
  - *Its network.* Without Codex's sandbox, Owlshift cannot take the network away from a Codex run, as it cannot from any run (section 8's known limits), and the shell Codex reads the repository with can reach the internet: "without network" does not hold for the critic on Codex. Codex's own web search tool, which searches on the operator's account, is turned off all the same (OWL-316). Accepted, as for every agent: the critic holds no credential, and what it can read it could already send.
  - *Its sub-agents* (OWL-317, decided with the maintainer on 2026-10-16). Codex keeps offering the critic its collaboration tools (`spawn_agent`, `wait_agent` and the rest), which no flag removes (OWL-316). A sub-agent runs outside the review's roster and its routing, may pick its own model and effort, and its tokens are most likely not in the run's usage. So the runner refuses a critic run whose `--json` stream carries a `collab_tool_call` item, whatever its tool: the run fails as a harness failure naming the tools, its last message is not used, and the failure counts as any failed run (`MAX_FAILED_RUNS`: one more run, then a park). It does not wait for the run's end (OWL-319): at the first such item the stream's reader stops the run's whole process tree, the `codex` Node wrapper and the native binary it starts (a process group on Unix, a Job Object on Windows), and the reason adds that Owlshift stopped the run at that call; a run so stopped is never counted as timed out, even when the deadline passed in the same moment. A usage limit Codex reported stays an interruption, and a run Codex reported failed before any such item keeps its reason with the tools added. What the stream shows sets the guard's reach, checked live with codex-cli 0.154.0 (build plan, OWL-317's entry):
    - `spawn_agent` and `list_agents` leave no event; `wait_agent` shows as a `collab_tool_call` item. A sub-agent's own commands and messages leave none in the critic's stream either (OWL-320). A critic that starts a sub-agent and never waits for it is not seen: its sub-agent costs what it costs until the turn ends or the executor's timeout stops the run, and its work reaches the review only through what the critic reads back some other way.
    - In five of the eight checked runs that waited (OWL-317, OWL-320), the run stopped writing events right after its `wait` and never ended its turn. Since OWL-319 that hang never starts: the run is stopped at its `wait` item rather than at the executor's timeout.
    - `-c agents.max_depth=0` does not refuse a spawn: checked live on 2026-10-16 (OWL-320), a sub-agent started and ran a command under it as it did without it. It is not passed.
    - Rejected: a warning that keeps the result, which enforces nothing; asking the critic in its prompt not to start sub-agents, a guardrail requested of a model. Stopping the run at its first `collab_tool_call` item, once rejected for want of a way to stop the run's whole process tree from the stream's reader, is built (OWL-319): the executor hands the harness's driver a stop on the run's tree.
  - *Its rules.* The runner injects the rules itself (section 6, "Harness contract"), so the adapter also passes `--ignore-rules`, which drops execpolicy `.rules` files only (C2). `-c project_doc_max_bytes=0` drops the worktree's `AGENTS.md` and `-c skills.include_instructions=false` its skills, which load whatever the other flags say: the ticket's branch, which agents write, gives the critic no instruction, as the worktree gives Claude Code none (OWL-53, OWL-131). A run's own `CODEX_HOME` holds no `AGENTS.md`. OWL-132's paths are closed before a Codex run reviews anything. A reached usage limit shows as an `error` event and `turn.failed` whose message gives the reset time as local wall-clock text, with exit status 1 (C7's Codex part).

**Parallel.** Decided on 2026-10-20 (OWL-398), roadmap P6, scenarios S5, S10, S16 and S17's fallback part, with the maintainer, and revised the same day after a review of the merged text (the step's envelope, the three counts, widening before delivery, the publication of a ticket's record, the usage episodes, the order of the parts): several tickets of a project at once, and several projects on one machine, without two of them colliding, before the background service of P7. Until now one ticket of a project is worked at a time ("One at a time", "The slot's turn", above) and a command holds the project's lock for its whole length, its runs included. P6 keeps `owlshift watch --drain`, one process per project, and runs each step of a ticket in a child process; the [build plan](build-plan.md), "P6 · Parallel", says who builds what, the live checks and the exit gate.

- **The step, in a child process.**
  - *What runs there.* Every step that may run an agent or the gate, as "One step a pass" defines it: an intake; an answer check and its RESUME, the stage step a counted answer starts being the next step; a dispatch's first stage step; a resumed stage step; a repair at Watch (a fix, a rebase, a correction, a review loop's step). A step that runs no model, such as a RESUME already decided or a move of a stage, stays watch's own. The child is `owlshift continue <TICKET>` with a hidden argument that carries the step's envelope and makes it stop at `Stop::Stepped`: `continue`'s own path (`OnDemand::continue_shown`) and the dispatch's, never a command of its own.
  - *The envelope.* What watch decided the step is, so that a child never applies a stale decision: the kind of step, the ticket ref's commit and the visible stage watch read, an attempt id, and the generation of the ticket's carried state. Under the ticket lock the child reads the ref and the stage again and refuses, spending nothing, when either moved, as `continue_shown` refuses a stale read today.
  - *What it carries* ("What a step carries", above): the resolver's chain and the asking roles that took recommendations at its bound (OWL-375), and the late-comment bound (OWL-139), kept in `<data>/tickets/<id>/carry.json` with the ticket-ref commit it belongs to and a generation. A child reads it when its ref is that commit and starts from zero otherwise, as a person's `continue` does; it writes the new one after the ticket ref it moved, so a child that dies between the two leaves a carry that no longer matches and the next step starts its chain again (an accepted residual: one more chain at most). `do` and a person's `continue` remove it.
  - *Its outcome.* One JSON record per attempt, `<data>/tickets/<id>/outcome-<attempt>.json`, written by the child last: the `Stop` kind, the hold it calls for and its end, and the ticket-ref commit it left. Watch reads it when it reaps the child and removes it; a watch that starts reads those its predecessor left, and applies a hold only when the record's commit is still the ticket's ref. Events go to the event log, whose appends several processes already share; the child's output is piped to watch, which prints each line prefixed by its ticket; the child notifies as any command does.
  - *Stopping.* Each child is a process tree of watch's (`ProcessTree`, its own process group, registered with watch's sentinel), and each of its runs a tree of the child's, so the existing machinery holds at both levels: a stop "now" ends watch through the policy every command shares, which stops every child's tree; a watch killed outright has its sentinel stop them. A child stopped mid-run leaves its run's obligation, so the project is refused until a person looks (section 8, "Isolation among steps"), as a `do` stopped mid-run is today ("How it stops", above). Since P7, a watch can also end after its current steps, or stop them and check their runs before it exits (section 5, "The background service", "Three ways to stop"); only a stop "now" or a hard kill leaves an obligation.
- **Locks.**
  - *The ticket lock.* `<data>/tickets/<id>/lock`, a `flock` taken with try-lock: held by a step, by `do` and by `continue` for their whole length, and by watch for each write it makes of that ticket's ref (an admission, `write_seen` of "Watch after delivery", a stage's move). Busy is answered at once to watch, and to a command once the lock stayed held for 250 ms ("A lock just released", below); watch tries again at a later pass. Two commands never work one ticket.
  - *The project lock.* Until the isolation among steps and the collisions were built (the build plan's parts 1 to 6), every child held it for its whole length, as `continue` did, so the project ran one step at a time while the process boundary, the ticket locks and the slots were put in place. As built (OWL-401), it is held only for short sections: the look at the quarantine, the fetch and the reads of the ticket ref and its run file, an admission, a branch's and a worktree's creation or removal, the delivery's collision check; never across a harness run, a gate or a call to the forge or the tracker but the ticket's read in the command's preparation. A command waits for it, a bounded loop over try-lock, every 10 ms for a minute, through the helper the account lock already shares with it (section 8, "The GitHub App's tokens"), and answers busy past it, as during a first clone. A section never takes another in one process: a second take of the lock waits on the first. Watch's own takes stay single tries. `owlshift forget`, `owlshift unquarantine` and `owlshift gate` hold it for their whole length; `gate`, whose check is a run's alone, also answers busy while any ticket's lock is held, so that no step runs beside it (section 8). "The project lock" above describes the lock before P6.
  - *Their order.* A ticket lock is taken before the project lock and released after it. Watch, which writes a ticket's ref under the project lock at an admission, takes that ticket's lock with try-lock and gives up when busy, so the reverse order never waits and two locks never wait on each other.
  - *Machine run slots.* `concurrent_runs` files `<state>/slots/<n>.lock` in the user's state directory, `n` from 0 to `concurrent_runs` − 1, shared by every project's watch on the machine. Watch counts those held (a probe, never a hold) into the scheduler's `Load.runs`, so a step it cannot start waits with `Wait::RunCap`. The child takes one with try-lock before its first run and keeps it to its step's end, the gate included; when none is free, since another project's watch was faster, it ends with a "no slot" outcome before touching anything, and watch tries again. Lock files are opened close-on-exec, so neither a harness nor a gate inherits them, and the kernel drops a dead holder's lock. Who holds a slot is written beside it (OWL-417): once the child holds `<n>.lock`, it replaces `<n>.holder` (project, ticket, pid, since) with its own, whole, best effort, and removes it before it lets the lock go. A record under a free lock is a dead holder's: it is never read, and a probe that finds the slot free removes it while it holds the slot. Rejected: naming holders from the runs the event log shows open, since a run that died leaves no `run_ended`, a slot is held through its step's gate with no run open, and a person's `do` runs without a slot. Accepted residual: a probe in the moment between a take and its record's write reads no record, or the record of a holder that died, and names that one.
  - *A lock just released* (OWL-414, checked on 2026-10-20; [build plan](build-plan.md), check (e)). Close-on-exec is not instant on macOS: a child the standard library starts through `posix_spawn`, its path for a command that does not set `PATH`, as the runner's git, can keep the parent's descriptors for some milliseconds after the spawn returned, up to about 40 ms under load, even once it ended and was reaped; the fork-and-exec path and Linux showed none. A lock whose holder ran git under it can so look held for a moment after its release, and the next taker, such as a step child watch starts right after an admission, was told busy in 8 of 3,000 tries at a load of about 130. So a command (`do`, `continue`, a step, `gate`, `forget`, `unquarantine`) takes the project lock and the ticket lock through a bounded loop over try-lock, every 10 ms for 250 ms, before it answers busy: a project or ticket that is really busy answers a quarter of a second later. A continue watch runs in its own process takes them as `continue` does. Watch's own takes never wait, in either order: its snapshot, its notes of a quarantine and its writes of a ticket's ref make one try each, and so does `owlshift doctor`'s look at the marker. Accepted residuals: one of watch's tries, or its count of the run slots (a probe), can see a lock or a slot just released as held, which costs that ticket or that dispatch one pass, as a real holder does; a child's take of a slot already tries three times 50 ms apart. The bound is not a requirement of its own: it outlasts the window measured six times over, and part 7's wait for the project lock, a minute (OWL-401), replaces it for that lock.
- **Three counts.** Kept apart, each with its own predicate in the core:
  - *Holding resources* (the collision rule, below): every ticket from its dispatch until its pull request ends, waiting and parked tickets included. Amended on 2026-10-22 (OWL-428): from its Build's admission, a ticket holding nothing before ("Resources admitted at Build", below).
  - *The project's capacity*, `policy.max_in_flight` in `owlshift.toml`, 1 to 10, 1 when absent: the tickets whose ref holds the slot as today (`schedule::holds_slot`: after Ready and before Watch, neither waiting nor parked). It bounds the dispatches from Ready and, as "The slot's turn" does for one, the stage steps at Design, Design review, Build and Verify: when more refs hold it than the cap, which an answered round can make happen, the steps run for the first `max_in_flight` holders by dispatch time and the others wait (`Wait::Slot`). An answer check, a RESUME, an intake and a repair at Watch need no capacity: an answer is always read and resumed, and the work it starts waits for its turn.
  - *The machine's slots*, `concurrent_runs` in the personal file, 1 to 16, 2 when absent: every child, whatever its project and its kind.
  - `policy.max_open_prs` ("Back-pressure") is unchanged. A ticket left at Build by a usage limit holds its capacity and no machine slot.
- **Admission, one transaction.** A ready set computed from a pass's reads is not admission: a person's `do` or another step can take a ticket or a resource between the read and the spawn. So a dispatch from Ready is one section under the ticket's lock and the project lock, inside the dispatch's own step. Amended on 2026-10-21 (OWL-401, approved with the maintainer): the step's child takes its machine slot first, then the ticket's lock, then, in one of the project's sections, the project's ticket refs are read again, the caps (the open pull requests' cap, the project's capacity) and the ticket's resources checked against them and the pass's reads of the forge and the tracker, which watch hands the step (`outside-<attempt>.json` in the ticket's folder; read afresh when it is missing), and the ticket ref written (its stage, its held set, its dispatch time) with its run file (section 8), before the step's first run; then the project lock is released and the step goes on. A child that gets no machine slot touches nothing, and the ticket waits at Ready for a later pass. A dispatch the check holds back runs nothing and records no `dispatch` event, and the next pass, reading the refs again, tells why it waits. `do` and a person's `continue`, before a stage runs, make the same check for their ticket, reading the forge and the tracker themselves, a failed read refusing; a refusal says why the ticket waits as `owlshift why` does. The check weighs what each ticket holds with the operator's project file's named resources, as the pass does. Rejected: watch admitting in its own process before it spawns the child, which duplicates the dispatch's preparation in watch for the same rule. Accepted residuals: what the pass read of the forge and the tracker is as old as the pass, at most the poll interval; a `do` admitted before a dispatch's child takes the capacity or the zone first, and that dispatch runs nothing. Amended on 2026-10-22 (OWL-428): a dispatch from Ready checks the caps alone and writes no held set; the same section, with the ticket's resources checked and written, runs at its Build's admission ("Resources admitted at Build", below).
- **Order.** Within a project, steps of tickets already dispatched first, by dispatch time: repairs at Watch, answer checks, resumes; then one intake, none started while one runs (OWL-401); then dispatches from Ready by the drain's order. Finishing comes before starting. Between projects, the first watch to take a free slot runs: two projects' priorities are not compared before P7's single runner (S16, roadmap's P6 reading). The scheduler's `ReadySet.dispatch` is the list of tickets dispatched in a pass. As built (OWL-401), `owlshift watch --drain` starts each step's child without waiting for it and leaves its ticket alone until it ends; each pass first applies the end of every child that ended, and between passes watch waits for the first to end, or the poll interval. Plain `owlshift watch` and the test bench keep stepping in watch's own process, one step after another.
- **What a ticket holds.**
  - *Its resources.* Zones (folders, written as `[zones]` keys are), named resources (the keys of `stack.resources`, whose values are patterns over repository paths: `*` one part, `**` any number, checked when the file is loaded) and files. `Resource::Browser` exists in the core and nothing takes it in P6: browser QA is a later step of its own (roadmap). Zones and files conflict by containment, part by part and in any ASCII case (a file is the zone of its own path); a named resource conflicts with itself and with any zone or file its pattern can reach.
  - *Declared at intake.* The intake record (`intake.json`) gains `zones` and `resources`: the folders the ticket will change, at the coarsest level the role can honestly name, and the named resources of the project's list, which the brief gives with their patterns. An intake that names no zone holds the whole repository, so a ticket whose reach is unknown collides with everything rather than nothing. A `zone:` label ("Zones a ticket declares, until intake", [runtime and operations](runtime-and-operations.md)) adds a zone; labels never remove one. A declared zone that a named resource's pattern can reach holds that resource too. The INTAKE comment shows them, and the brief's `zones` lists what the ticket holds (since OWL-428, intake's zones with the labels' until its Build's admission, below). Once intake ended, its zones also join the labels' in choosing the decider of a ticket without an assignee (section 4, "Who decides", OWL-412). Since OWL-428 a ticket holds intake's zones only when no plan names its files: a variant without Design, a plan without files, a ticket dispatched straight to Build ("Resources admitted at Build", below).
  - *Kept in the ticket ref.* `held.json`, a new file of the ticket ref (its format bumped): the declared zones and resources, the files the ticket's branch has changed, and the times of its dispatch, of each widening and of its delivery. Not in `intake.json`, which belongs to the ticket's text and is replaced by the next intake. The held set never shrinks while the ticket holds resources. A ticket dispatched before P6, whose ref has no `held.json`, holds the whole repository until its pull request ends: a transient cost, never a guess. Since OWL-428 the dispatch writes `held.json` with its time and nothing admitted, and the Build's admission writes what it admits and the admission's time, its format bumped; a `held.json` of the earlier format keeps holding what its dispatch declared ("Resources admitted at Build", below).
  - *Widened as its branch moves.* After every run that may change the branch (a Build, a fix, a correction, a rebase, a review loop's Build) and before the step goes on, the runner lists the files the branch changed against its merge-base with the base, renames and copies on both sides (`git diff --name-only --no-renames -z`), and adds each to `held.json`, with the named resources whose patterns match it. A file holds itself, never its folder, so a lockfile at the root does not hold the whole repository.
- **The in-flight set.** Read at each pass:
  1. Owlshift's tickets: every ticket ref of the project from its dispatch (its Build's admission since OWL-428) until its following ends at Watch, waiting and parked tickets included, each with its `held.json` and, while its pull request is open, the files that pull request changes, since a person may push to it.
  2. The repository's other open pull requests, a person's, another bot's, a draft: the files each changes, a rename's old and new paths both, read through the forge (on GitHub, the pull request's files, at most 3,000), kept by head commit, base branch and base commit, so a pull request is read again when it moved or was retargeted. A list that cannot be read whole holds the whole repository.
  3. People's tickets: those in the tracker's working state (`[tracker].states.working`) that no Owlshift ticket ref of this project holds (since OWL-428, that no ref of the project owns from its dispatch on, a ticket in Design holding nothing being Owlshift's all the same), a ticket a person moved to working from Ready or from an ended Watch included, with their `zone:` labels. Their pull requests are counted under 2. A person's ticket with neither a pull request nor a label is not counted (decided with the maintainer on 2026-10-20, a deviation from S5 and S12): one `warning` event (`zones_unknown`) per ticket, a line, and a line of `owlshift why`. Rejected: holding the whole repository for it, which stops every ticket of the project while a person works one by hand without a label.
  - A pass whose read of the open pull requests or of the working state fails dispatches nothing from Ready and starts no stage step that the rule below would check: what cannot be read is never taken as free.
- **The collision rule.** In the core, from plain data, against the in-flight set as the pass read it, whichever holder changed since:
  - *At a dispatch from Ready*: only when none of the ticket's resources conflicts with anything held; otherwise it waits, `Wait::Collision` naming the holder and the resource ("waits for LOC-118, zone `backend/billing`"), carried by the `decision` event as the other reasons are ("Why a ticket waits"). Since OWL-428, at a Build's admission instead, with the plan's files ("Resources admitted at Build", below): a ready ticket never waits for a collision.
  - *Before each stage step and before delivery* of a ticket not yet delivered: the same check of its held set, widened, against everything else held. The delivery's check is made inside the step, after the Verify pass and before the push, under the project lock: a conflict stops the step there, the Verify outcome kept for that commit, and the ticket waits at Verify; once free, its next step delivers that commit without another Verify pass if the branch has not moved.
  - *Who goes on.* A ticket with an open pull request never waits, since its repairs are what lets it merge; between two Owlshift tickets not yet delivered, the one dispatched first goes on, so two widenings cannot hold each other; against a person's pull request or ticket, Owlshift's ticket waits. Nothing running is stopped. A ticket that waits for an answer or is parked keeps what it holds, and `owlshift why` names it: a person answers, restarts it or `forget`s it. A ticket that waits for a collision gives up the slot's turn but keeps its capacity, so it and its holder never wait for each other (OWL-403).
  - *Optimistic, with detection* (decided with the maintainer on 2026-10-20, a deviation from S5). Declared zones are a prediction: a run can change a file outside them, which the widening sees once that run ends, so two tickets can change the same file before either is delivered. The exclusion is strict on what was declared and read; a change outside it is found before the next step and before delivery, the later ticket waits, and a conflict between their branches is repaired by a rebase run at Watch (section 5, "Conflicts and the rebase run"). The exit gate checks that this optimism holds on a real backlog. Rejected: a sandbox that denies writes outside the held resources, which would stop runs on every lockfile and cache a tool writes beside its folder.
- **Resources admitted at Build** (OWL-428, decided on 2026-10-22 with the maintainer). Found at P6's exit gate on Locary (OWL-408): from 2026-10-21 15:45 UTC, with `policy.max_in_flight` 10 and `concurrent_runs` 16, 1.5 tickets were in flight on average over 30 hours, and on 2026-10-22 `owlshift why` showed 18 of the 36 admitted tickets waiting for LOC-1416's zones (`frontend/src`, `backend/leases`, `backend/inspections`, `backend/core`), 17 for a blocker and one for LOC-1432's zone. Intake names folders, and a ticket held them from its dispatch until its pull request ended, through a Design and a Design review that write nothing: LOC-1416 changes 22 files under `frontend/src` and held all of it.
  - *The rule.* A ticket holds nothing before its Build. A dispatch from Ready, the drain's, `do`'s or a person's `continue`'s, checks the caps and never the resources ("Admission, one transaction", amended), and writes `held.json` with the dispatch time and nothing admitted (no admission time), so the turn's order by dispatch time and the in-flight set keep reading it; a ref without `held.json` still holds the whole repository and comes first, as one from before P6. A Design, a Design review, their questions, a wait for an answer and a park before the first Build hold nothing. Every step into Build whose `held.json` has no admission time, whatever command runs it (the drain, `do`, a person's `continue`, a Build resumed after a usage limit), first admits the ticket's resources in the admission's one section, under the ticket's lock and the project's lock: the files of the plan `plan.json` keeps ("The quality pipeline", the plan), each a file that holds itself, with the named resources their paths reach and the zones of its `zone:` labels, checked against the in-flight set and written to `held.json` with the admission's time. A variant without Design, a plan without files and a ticket dispatched straight to Build admit intake's zones with the labels', as before. From the admission on nothing changes: the widening after each run that may change the branch, the check before each step and before delivery, the hold until the pull request ends, never shrinking. A ticket that returns to Design once admitted (a `replan`, a reversed decision, OWL-345) keeps what it holds, and its next Build adds the new plan's files to it, checked as an admission checks.
  - *A Build that waits.* A Build whose resources conflict with anything held waits at Build, `Wait::Collision` naming the holder and the resource, whatever the holder's dispatch time: an admitted ticket is never made to wait by one admitted after it. Among Builds waiting in the same pass, the one dispatched first is admitted first; "Who goes on" (the collision rule, above) still settles a widening between two admitted tickets. The waiting Build gives up the slot's turn and keeps the project's capacity (OWL-403). `owlshift why`, `owlshift doctor` and the WAITING comment (OWL-419) name the wait at Build. A ready ticket waits for the caps, a blocker, a limit or the quarantine, never for a collision.
  - *Refs from before OWL-428.* A `held.json` of the earlier format, written by a dispatch that declared intake's zones, keeps holding them; its ticket's next step into Build, when it has not been built, adds the plan's files to them as a return to Design does.
  - *The capacity.* A ticket that finished its Design and waits for its Build counts in `policy.max_in_flight`, as one left at Build by a usage limit does: the cap still bounds the tickets worked at once, the Designs paid ahead of their Build and the age of their plans. Rejected: leaving it out of the count, which the replay below shows delivering a little more early (36 pull requests in the first 30 hours instead of 31) for the same drain length (65 hours instead of 67), against Designs without bound, each a plan that ages while it waits.
  - *Why both halves.* A conflict is decided by containment: a ready ticket whose intake named `frontend/src` conflicts with any file under it. Narrowing only the holder to its plan's files leaves the waiting ticket judged by its folder: on the 13 Locary tickets with a plan, of the 46 ordered pairs whose intake zones conflict, 85 % still conflict with one side's plan files against the other's zones, 39 % with plan files on both sides. Taking resources at Build alone, with intake's zones, fills the capacity with planned tickets that wait for a folder. Both together compare a plan's files with a plan's files.
  - *Replayed on 2026-10-22.* [`live-checks/drain-replay.py`](live-checks/drain-replay.py) replays Locary's drain from its event log and ticket refs, from 2026-10-21 15:45 UTC to 2026-10-22 21:34 UTC: 49 tickets, the 13 that ran with their own phase times, plans, changed files and blockers, the 36 that waited with their intake zones, their blockers and their variant's median phase times; a file-level conflict with a ticket that waited is drawn at the rates measured on the 13 (40 draws); capacity 10. Today's rule replays at 1.6 tickets in flight and 16 pull requests in the first 30 hours, against 1.5 and 13 measured. Agents working, pull requests in 30 hours, drain length to the last merge, mean wait from ready to Build start outside its own Design: today's rule 1.6, 16, 192 h, 42 h; the plan's files after Design alone 1.8, 19, 136 h, 26 h; resources taken at Build alone, with intake's zones, 1.0 (10 counted in flight), 8, 153 h, 50 h; both 2.9, 31, 67 h, 11 h; both, a delivered pull request holding only its changed files, 3.1, 34, 60 h, 10 h; finer intake zones (the folders of the plan's files) 2.0, 20, 106 h, 20 h; no collision rule at all 4.4, 48, 38 h, 1 h, a floor set by Locary's chains of blockers. With every file-level conflict rate raised by 25 points, both take 84 hours against 192 and the plan's files alone gain nothing; with the waiting tickets' times doubled, both take 118 hours against 350. Not replayed: the maintainer's review pace (31 pull requests in 30 hours is one an hour), usage limits on one subscription, the rebases more overlap brings. OWL-429 measures the rule on Locary once built. The merge of the design's pull request (#390), whose branch carried the issue ID, closed OWL-428 before any code; OWL-448 built the rule on 2026-10-25 ([build plan](build-plan.md), "P6 · Parallel", part 13, "As built").
  - *Accepted residuals.* A plan's files are a prediction, as intake's zones were, its misses found by the widening ("Optimistic, with detection"), and a plan made while another ticket holds its files may be stale once that one merges: the Build starts from the base as its step finds it (a branch with no commits of its own fast-forwards, "A broken base"), reads the code again and may ask to replan. Two Designs can plan the same files at once, and the later Build waits. The brief's `zones`, which tells a run where the ticket works, lists intake's zones with the labels' until the Build's admission and what the ticket holds from then on. Two tickets that add migrations to the same chain hold different files, so a project names each migration chain as a named resource (`stack.resources`), which conflicts with itself: Locary's project file named none on 2026-10-24 (`[stack.resources]` empty), and intake's folders were what kept its migrations apart. A ticket that returns to Design once admitted and whose next admission waits for a holder that a widening of its own makes wait for it, by "Who goes on", waits with it until a person acts (found while building it, OWL-448): it takes a replan and a crossing widening.
  - *Rejected.* Finer intake zones alone (106 hours, 184 at the raised rates): a guess before the code is read, and the waiting ticket is judged by the same guess. A delivered pull request holding only its changed files, alone (146 hours): Locary's pull requests merge within minutes, so the wait for a review is not where the time goes; it may be added later where reviews are slow. A dispatch order favouring narrow tickets: it moves the serial stretches without shortening them. Tolerating overlaps that rebase cheaply, as `/parallel` did by hand, as the default: a semantic conflict a rebase does not see. Offered since 2026-10-24 as a project's opt-in, with a refresh and a review where the default makes a ticket wait ("Tolerated overlap", below).
- **Tolerated overlap** (OWL-449, decided on 2026-10-24 with the maintainer, a project's opt-in). The rule above compares a plan's files with a plan's files, but a file two plans share still holds the later Build until the earlier ticket's pull request ends. `/parallel` let such tickets run together and repaired them at merge. A project may choose the same, with the repairs made by the runner and checked as any delivery is.
  - *Measured.* On Locary's 26 tickets that reached a Build (their refs, read on 2026-10-24), 42 of the 325 pairs changed a common file (13 %): 10 pairs shared only code and its tests, 7 only tests, 6 only code, 6 only `settings.py` or `urls.py`, 2 only the message catalogs `frontend/messages/{fr,en}.json`, 2 code and a document, 9 three kinds or more. What plans share is mostly code, not a catalog that rebases mechanically. A Build checked against five admitted Builds finds a shared file about half the time (1 − 0.87⁵). The plans named 89 % of the files their Builds changed (21 tickets with a plan, mean). Owlshift's rebase runs on Locary to that day: 2, both trivial, both on the fast tier, both finished. Of the 56 pairs of pull requests to Locary's `develop` from the maintainer's account (`/parallel`'s and Owlshift's) open at the same time from 2026-10-12, 11 changed a common file, 8 of them the message catalogs; all were merged, the later of each pair taking commits after the first merged.
  - *Replayed on 2026-10-24* ([`live-checks/drain-replay.py`](live-checks/drain-replay.py), from 2026-10-21 15:45 UTC to 2026-10-24 19:14 UTC, 25 tickets that all ran, 20 draws). Agents working, drain length to the last merge, mean wait: today's rule 1.5, 79.6 h, 11.2 h; resources admitted at Build 2.3, 64.5 h, 2.9 h; no collision rule 2.8, 62.8 h, 0.1 h. Locary declares no named resource, so no collision rule is this option's ceiling there. On 2026-10-22's backlog, 36 tickets waiting at once, the same two rules gave 2.9 agents and 67 hours against 4.4 and 38 (above): the gain is large when a backlog waits, as 39 tickets sat in Locary's ready column on 2026-10-24, and small when tickets come one at a time.
  - *The switch.* `policy.overlap` in `owlshift.toml`: `exclusive`, the default, the rule above; or `rebase`. Read at the base branch's head at each pass, as `[forge] merge` is ("Merge on green"), so a ticket's own branch cannot turn it on; the personal file cannot set it. A project file using it raises its `requires`.
  - *What still excludes, under `rebase`.* A named resource (`stack.resources`): two tickets never hold the same one, a migration chain above all, whose conflict git does not see (two migrations on the same parent apply cleanly). A holder of the whole repository: a ref without `held.json`, a ref that cannot be read, an intake that named no zone. People's pull requests and tickets: Owlshift's ticket waits for them as today, since the runner rebases only its own branches. A file already held by two Owlshift tickets: a third Build that would hold it waits at Build (`Wait::Collision`), so each overlap costs one repair and never a chain of rebases; the bound is raised on measures, never by default. A zone counts as a holder of every file it contains.
  - *What overlaps.* Between two Owlshift tickets, a file or a zone. The Build's admission goes on and records the overlap in `held.json` (format bumped): the other ticket, the shared paths, the time. A widening that reaches a sibling's file records it the same way rather than holding the step, within the bound; beyond it, the later ticket waits before its next step and before delivery, as under the exclusive rule. A `decision` event (`overlap`) and `owlshift why` name each overlap. The Build's brief lists them (`overlaps`: the sibling ticket and the shared paths), so the run keeps its edits of a shared file to what its ticket needs, without reordering or reformatting it.
  - *No order of delivery.* Each ticket delivers when its Verify passes, whichever was admitted first; the first to merge sets what the other rebases onto.
  - *The refresh.* At Watch, a pass that reads a new base head changing a path one of the ticket's overlaps names schedules a refresh rebase run ("Conflicts and the rebase run") even when the forge reports no conflict: a merge git makes cleanly does not prove that two changes agree, so the pull request's checks run again on the combined code before anyone merges it. A conflict goes the usual way, a trivial one on the fast tier, any other on the Build routing.
  - *A review after a resolution.* A rebase run that resolves a non-trivial conflict on an overlap runs the Verify pass on the rebased head before its push, with the ticket's reviewers, its findings handled as a Verify pass's are: a resolution that merges two tickets' intents is code no reviewer read. A trivial conflict and a clean refresh get the gate alone, as today.
  - *The merge.* Unchanged. Under merge on green (D14) a pull request merges only once green twice on the base its checks ran on, so a sibling's merge already forces a refresh; under a person's merge, the refreshed checks are what the person sees.
  - *Recorded per day:* refresh and rebase runs by cause and tier, the Verify passes after a resolution, their minutes and cost, and the Builds lost to a park.
  - *Judged on Locary,* after OWL-448: a drain of at least 24 hours under `rebase` beside OWL-429's under `exclusive`: agents working, pull requests, drain length, the runs above, and the base breaks traced to an overlap, whose bar is none. A base break traced to a tolerated overlap turns the option off on Locary until its cause is written here.
  - *Accepted residuals.* A semantic conflict that shares no file (a function one ticket renames and another's new file calls) is found only by the gate and the checks on the combined code, as under the exclusive rule; merge on green's base check is its guard. A Build that rebases onto its sibling's merge may find its plan stale and ask to replan, which the exclusive rule avoided by making it wait. Two Builds that edit the same file at once spend tokens a serial order would not have spent when their rebase parks.
  - *Readings settled while building it* (OWL-449, 2026-10-25; [build plan](build-plan.md), "P6 · Parallel", part 14, "As built"). Watch reads the switch at the default branch's head the forge gives at each pass, as merge on green does, and each step reads it again at the base it fetched. An overlap is recorded once, by the ticket whose admission or widening found it, and read from both sides, so the first to merge refreshes the other whichever recorded it; the widening's record is made in one of the project's sections, as an admission's, so the two never miss each other. A Build that would be a path's third holder is told both holders. The refresh follows the recorded overlaps whatever the switch says now: a project that turns it off still gets its combined code checked. A "new base head" is new since the push: a change the base held when the pull request was pushed gets no refresh, its checks having run on the merge with it. "The Verify pass" after a resolution is one pass, run inside the rebase repair and settled as a last pass, its findings going to one last Build-role run at Watch, as Verify's last fix, before the gate and the push: the ticket never leaves Watch, so the repairs' bound and a rebase's crash recovery hold, which a loop back through Verify would have lost.
  - *Rejected.* A list of shared files exempted from the rule, the overlaps `/parallel` tolerated most: on Locary it frees 8 of the 42 sharing pairs, since code is what plans share. Ordering deliveries, the later ticket waiting for the earlier one's merge before it delivers: it brings back a wait on the review pace, which the refresh makes unneeded. No bound on the holders of a file: a file held by five tickets rebases four times, each onto the previous one's result. Tolerating overlap on named resources: their conflicts are the ones a rebase does not see.
- **Usage limits.** Section 9's "A reached limit is a normal state", as built in P6:
  - *What is known.* Each harness's usage state is a machine-wide file, `<state>/usage/<harness>.json`, written under a lock of its own and replaced whole. It holds the windows the harness reports (Claude Code's `rate_limit_event` names its five-hour and seven-day windows, among others, each with its share used and reset time), each with the share, the reset time and the time the event was read from the stream, not the time the run ended; a report replaces a window's entry only when its reset time is later, or the same and read later. Apart from the windows it holds the harness's limit episodes: a provider's limit (a run ended at its usage limit) with its reset time, or, when the harness gave none, a deadline 30 minutes after it was seen; and a cap's pause, a window at or above `usage_cap_percent`, until that window's reset.
  - *Limited* while an episode stands. When a deadline without a reset time passes, the first child that takes the usage lock marks itself the episode's probe, with its own deadline, and runs; until its run reports, every other step treats the harness as limited. A probe that meets the limit opens a new episode, a probe that runs ends it, a probe that dies lets its deadline pass and the next child probes. Decided with the maintainer on 2026-10-20 (OWL-404): the new episode a probe opens keeps the told mark of the one it replaces, so the operator is told once per outage, not at each deadline.
  - *The effective harness.* One function, shared by watch and the child, gives the harness a run would use: its own when not limited; for a review role, the fallback the personal file names (`[harnesses.<name>].fallback`) when that one is installed, logged in for agent runs and not limited, which the review's line in its comment and a `routing` event say; otherwise none, and the run waits. No other role falls back: the authoring roles, the arbiter, the resolver, intake and the answer check stay on Claude Code (D4, OWL-347), so a fallback named for them is not used and `owlshift doctor` says so. A deviation from S10 and S17, recorded in the roadmap. As built (OWL-406): the whole roster of a Design review or Verify pass runs on the fallback while Claude Code is limited at the pass's start, checked again at each pass, and the opt-in Codex seat is not added beside it.
  - *Where it is checked.* Watch starts no step whose first run has no effective harness; a step whose first action runs no model (a RESUME already decided, a delivery of a verified commit) is never held by a limit. Inside a step, the child checks each run's effective harness before it starts it, a reviewer's, the arbiter's and the resolver's included; none ends the step with an interruption that spends nothing, what earlier runs of the step kept staying kept, and the ticket resumes from its checkpoint after the reset, not counted as a failure (S10). Watch's hold of every continue until a limit's reset ("Pausing", "Watch after delivery") is replaced by this state, per harness.
  - *Told once.* A ticket whose next run has no effective harness waits; the drain dispatches nothing that would run on a limited harness until the earliest end of its episodes; the operator is notified once per episode, the record kept beside the usage file so that no other project's watch notifies it again ([runtime and operations](runtime-and-operations.md), "Notifications"). As built (OWL-404), the record is the episode's own `told` mark in the usage file, under its lock.
- **A broken base** (OWL-418, decided 2026-10-22 with the maintainer). Found at P6's exit gate on Locary (OWL-408) on 2026-10-21: a Dependabot commit on `develop` (pitchopp/locary#1453) bumped `rollup` in `frontend/pnpm-lock.yaml` past what the operator's offline pnpm store held, so `stack.setup` failed for every branch cut from that base before any of the branch's code ran. LOC-1420, LOC-1426 and LOC-1425 each finished their plan, failed the runner's gate on `setup: exit status 1`, spent a Build recovery run on the same cause and parked, 13 and 7 minutes apart; each needed `owlshift continue` once the store was fixed, and about fifteen more tickets were on their way there. A failure that comes from the base is a condition of the project, as a harness at its limit is: it holds the Builds once, is told once, and lifts by itself.
  - *Whose failure.* After a Build-role run at Build (a Verify fix run included, a repair at Watch not) fails on a command of the runner's gate or of its setup before the harness (`Failure::Setup`), the runner checks the run's configuring commit ("Which commit configures a run"): the same setup and gate commands, from that commit's project file, in a fresh worktree, confined as a Build's gate. The failure is the base's when the base fails at the same command of the same step, setup or gate (`owlshift_core::base::whose`); otherwise it is the branch's and takes today's path, a recovery run, then a park on the second failure. A gate fault with no command (uncommitted changes, a moved HEAD, files the gate changed) and a command the runner could not confine, start or watch (`GateReport::runner_fault`) are never a command's failure: the branch's failure then takes today's path, and a base check that ends so is unchecked, a `warning` event (`base_unchecked`), today's path too.
  - *The check, alone.* It is `owlshift gate`'s run (`GateOnce`, its `base` place: `<project>/base/worktree` on the branch `owlshift-gate/base`), under the project's lock and refused while another ticket's step is in flight, since its new branch and worktree entry would read as a breach in a sibling's isolation check (section 8, "Isolation among steps"). A breach quarantines the project, as `owlshift gate`'s does. Its verdict is kept per commit and commands in `base.json` of the project's folder (format 1, at most 16 entries, the oldest without a hold dropped first), with the default branch's head it was checked at; it is read again under the lock before the check runs and written before the lock is let go, so N tickets on one commit and one head cost one check.
  - *The hold.* A hold opens when a ticket's failure matches a failed verdict; a failed verdict that no ticket's failure matched holds nothing. While it stands, every Build-role run at Build configured at that commit is held before it starts: no harness, no setup, nothing counted (the core event `BaseBroken`, valid at Build only, leaves the ticket as it is), the step stopping `BaseHeld` (`StepEnd::BaseHeld`, step outcome format 4, formats 1 to 3 still read). Design, Design review, Verify passes, intake and the drain's dispatch go on; a ticket dispatched straight to Build onto a held commit is held there and keeps the project's capacity, as a usage-limited one does. A branch with no commits of its own leaves a held commit once the default branch moves, at its next step into Build, which fast-forwards it; one with commits of its own is rebased onto a newer head that passes ("Leaving a broken commit", below).
  - *A check that must wait.* A check refused because another ticket's step is in flight marks the entry pending, with the failure: every Build configured there is held before it starts, and the check runs once nothing else runs. Watch gives it priority: once a step ends pending, it starts no other step until those in flight end, then runs that ticket's step at once, its retry hold lifted. The priority is read back from `base.json` too, so a watch started again, or a check a person's command left pending, gets it: the ticket is the one whose `base_wait.json` names the pending commit and commands. It gives the priority up when that step does not start in a pass with nothing else in flight (held back for another reason), when the ticket leaves Build, and after three hours, so a due check never holds the project; a pending retry runs nothing but the check. Locary's three tickets failed with others in flight, so this is a parallel drain's common case. Without watch, the operator's `continue` of the ticket is the retry.
  - *Every check due, one rule* (OWL-431, decided 2026-10-24 with the maintainer). A held commit's re-check ("Lifting") and the newer head's check ("Leaving a broken commit") wait as a first check does: a step that finds one due and cannot run it beside the other steps ends `BaseHeld` with its check pending, and gets the same priority, given up the same way; its texts stay the hold's. In the drain, each dispatch it starts lifts the retry hold of the tickets held for their base, so their steps come at its next pass, not after the retry delay. The bound: a held ticket's check runs before the drain's second dispatch after a fix lands, the first giving it its step, whose check due then takes the priority, after which nothing starts, a dispatch included, until it has run. Its price: while a hold stands, each move of the default branch's head makes a re-check due, which in a busy drain holds new steps back until those in flight end, once per move and at most three hours, as a first check's priority does.
  - *A finished Build.* A done Build whose gate failure is held keeps its result, its notes and follow-ups, the newest decider edit its brief held and whether it was the run given late comments, in `base_wait.json` of the ticket's folder (format 1; machine-local, as its commits are), valid while the ticket is at Build in the same round, on the same branch head and configuring commit; a pristine Build's held failure keeps the file without a result. Once the hold lifts, the ticket's next step reruns only the gate on that commit (`Executor::regate`: the setup and the commands, the isolation checked as after a run, no harness, no usage admission, no cost), then goes on as after a passing gate: the late comments read against the kept mark (OWL-139), Verify, the delivery. A red gate then is a failed run, classified again. `owlshift forget` drops the file. A rebase onto a newer base keeps it at the rebased head and the commit the ticket is then configured at (OWL-427, "Leaving a broken commit").
  - *Lifting.* A held commit is checked again once per move of the default branch's head, by the first held ticket's step, and whenever the operator runs `owlshift gate --at <commit>`, or `owlshift gate` while the default branch is that commit: run on a held or pending commit, its verdict replaces the entry's, a pass lifting the hold, a failure keeping it, its command updated. That is the gesture the ticket's "`owlshift continue <project>`" became: `continue` takes a ticket, not a project, and the gate is the check an operator runs after a fix anyway, so no command is added. No timer: a fix outside the repository, Locary's store, is checked by the operator's gate or by the next commit on the default branch. Watch lifts its retry hold on the held tickets as soon as `base.json` no longer holds their commit. A re-check that still fails lets a held branch with commits of its own leave that commit instead (OWL-427, below).
  - *Told.* The operator is notified once per hold (its told mark in `base.json`): the commit, the step and command, and the re-check command. Each verdict recorded is a project `decision` event (`base` = `checked`, its `change` `opened`, `still_held`, `lifted` or `cached`, the command, its reason and the last 2 KiB of its output); each held stop a ticket `decision` event (`base` = `waits`, `reason` = `base_broken`), which `owlshift why` reads. A held ticket shows working, so it gets OWL-419's WAITING comment, its reason the commit, folded once it goes on. `owlshift why` lists each held commit with its command, the last lines of its output and the re-check command. A rebase off a held commit is a ticket `decision` event (`base` = `rebase`), and its failure a PARKED comment ("Leaving a broken commit").
  - *Leaving a broken commit* (OWL-427, decided 2026-10-23 with the maintainer). A branch with commits of its own stays on the commit it was made from (OWL-331), so when the fix is a new commit on the default branch, such as a revert, rather than the environment, its Build would stay held: each re-check of that commit finds it failing still. Such a branch is rebased onto the newer head once that head passes, and its Build goes on.
    - *When.* At a Build step held at commit C whose re-check, made first ("Lifting"), still fails, against the default branch's head H the step fetched: H is not C and contains it, the ticket's branch has commits of its own (its head is no ancestor of H), its worktree lists no uncommitted path, and the ticket has no followed delivery. The core decides (`owlshift_core::base::leave`). An environment fix lifts the hold through C's re-check before any of this, so no branch is rewritten for it.
    - *The head's check.* H is checked as a base is, alone, with the setup and gate of its own project file (`GateOnce`, its base place, no ticket's failure behind it), its verdict kept in `base.json` per commit and commands with a `decision` event (`base` = `checked`) as for any check; the other held tickets read it there at the same head. A failed verdict holds nothing, since no ticket's failure matched it. A check that cannot run, another ticket's step being in flight, leaves the ticket held with that check pending, which gets a first check's priority (OWL-431, "Every check due, one rule").
    - *The run.* One `rebase` role run, its brief naming C as `broken` and no pull request: on the fast tier (`[models].fast`) when `git merge-tree` finds the merge of the branch into H clean or trivial, on the Build routing otherwise, as at Watch, with a `decision` event (`base` = `rebase`: `from`, `onto`, `trivial`, `tier`, `conflicting`). It runs in the Build's own worktree, kept as it is, so the Build's uncommitted plan and ledger stay. It rebases onto H, resolves what conflicts and commits, and runs nothing else: its brief's gate is empty and it gets no setup, so the runner's gate after its `done` only checks that everything is committed on the branch. The Build's gate judges the result, which an unfinished Build fails by design. Its cost counts in the ticket's spend and budget, as any run's. Nothing is pushed: the branch is pushed at its delivery, as before.
    - *After it.* A `done` whose commit descends from H and still changes every path the branch changed (`rebase_keeps_delivery`, Watch's push guard) is the branch now. The step dispatches again, configured at H, the branch's merge-base now, with a second `dispatch` event, and keeps `base_wait.json` at the new head and configuring commit. A done Build then reruns only its gate (`Executor::regate`) and goes on to Verify and the delivery; a Build held before its run starts that run. A red gate is classified as any is: H failing at the same command holds the ticket at H, any other failure is the branch's and gets a recovery run.
    - *A failed rebase.* Any other end (`failed`, `blocked` or questions, a refused or unread result, a `done` the guard refuses, a usage limit) first moves the branch back to its head before the run when the run moved it: `git reset --hard` in its worktree, recorded through its run file, the untracked plan and ledger kept. A usage limit then stops the step as any does, no failed run counted. Every other end parks the ticket (the core event `RebaseFailed`, valid at Build only, `ParkReason::Rebase`); the PARKED comment names C and its failing command, H, what the run said or why it was refused, and that `owlshift continue` runs the rebase again. One attempt, no retry on the Build routing: the Build's worktree, which holds its plan and ledger, is never made anew between attempts as Watch's is. A decider's comment on the ticket reaches the next run's brief as at Watch, so a conflict only the decider can settle is settled there, then `continue`.
    - *Exceptions.* A run that breaks isolation leaves everything for the person who looks, as at Watch, the branch included. A run that ends in the middle of its rebase, its worktree on no branch, is such a breach (the role is told to abort first): the person aborts that rebase in the ticket's worktree before `owlshift unquarantine`, which takes the worktree as it then is. A crash between the run's end and the new keeping of the wait drops the wait, whose head is no longer the branch's: the Build then runs again from its commits instead of its gate alone. A worktree with uncommitted paths is not rebased, since moving the branch back would drop them: the ticket stays held, with a `warning` event (`base_not_rebased`) naming them.
    - *An open pull request* (decided by the maintainer on 2026-10-23). A ticket whose delivery is followed, a review loop's Build, is not rebased: its push would replace the history of an open pull request, which only Watch's leased and guarded rebase push does. It is held only when its delivered branch sits on a commit that broke after the delivery, so it stays held as before, and its notice still says a person rebases it.
    - *Accepted residuals.* Watch's usage check before a step counts a held Build with a kept result as a gate run alone, though the step may start a rebase run first: the child's own check of that run's harness holds it. A rebase whose branch changed a path the fix now carries identically is refused by the guard and parks: a person decides.
    - *Rejected.* The rebase run as at Watch, its gate included, which an unfinished Build fails by design and a done one runs twice; the runner's own `git rebase` without a model, which rewrites an agent's commits outside any sandbox (identity, signing, hooks) and parks on a trivial conflict; merging H into the branch, a commit the runner writes with no sign-off; configuring the branch at H without moving it, whose code keeps C's breakage; rebasing every resumed Build (OWL-331), a run per resume where this is one per held branch and fix.
  - *Accepted residuals.* The check holds the project's lock for its length, so watch's passes and the other steps' sections wait for it, as for `owlshift gate`. A red gate on a passing base costs one base check per commit and head. Watch's fix, correction and rebase runs are not classified: a red check on their pull request is already told apart by its state on the base head ("What a read decides", above). A gate that checks what the base cannot have yet, a deliverable, fails on the base too and holds the ticket: real gates pass on their base. With steps in children (OWL-401), a dispatch the drain starts in the seconds a held ticket's own step takes to find its check due comes before that check too (OWL-431). A re-check's or head check's priority, unlike a first check's, is not read back from `base.json`: a watch started again gives the ticket its step at the next dispatch or after the retry delay.
  - *Rejected.* Judging from the failure alone (a `setup:` reason taken as the base's), which a branch that edits its lockfile also gives; checking the base before every Build, a gate run per dispatch; holding the whole project, whose Design, review and Verify passes never reach the gate; running the check beside sibling runs; a timer; rerunning a finished Build from its ledger once the hold lifts, a model run per ticket for what the gate alone decides; a new command for the re-check. For OWL-431: the priority alone with the retry delay, a bound in time and not in dispatches; watch reading the default branch's head at each pass, a fetch per pass; the priority on a hold's first head move only, which leaves a later fix unbounded; no dispatch while a held ticket's step runs, which holds them for a whole Build once the hold lifts.
- **`owlshift why`.** A read-only command, without the lock, that works while watch runs. In a project, every admitted ticket that is not running with its current reason, read from the `decision` events (`drain` = `waits`) and the ticket refs: its blockers, a collision with the ticket, pull request or person's ticket and the resource, a cap, a harness limited until when, the quarantine, a broken base (OWL-418); and the people's tickets left out for want of zones. `owlshift why TICKET` gives that ticket's reasons over time. Both end with the machine's slots in use, by project and ticket, the harnesses limited, until when, and the commits whose broken gate holds Builds. The slots' holders are read from their holder records ("Locks", machine run slots), never from the events; a held slot without a readable record is shown as held, holder unknown.
- **What P7 keeps.** P7 keeps the child per step and its envelope, the ticket locks, the run files and the obligations of section 8, the slot files and the usage file, all unchanged: its daemon supervises the watches that use them rather than replacing them (section 5, "The background service"). The priorities of two projects are not compared in P7, and claims with leases on git refs move to P10 (section 7).
- **Rejected.** Threads in one watch, where a crash ends every ticket's step and `OnDemand` assumes one command owns the lock; P7's single runner brought forward, which compares projects' priorities natively but doubles P6 and overlaps P7; a hidden command of its own for the step, a second entry beside `continue`; one shared registry of the runs in flight, a single file every step writes, whose loss would leave every check without its exemptions; children that outlive watch, which the sentinel of OWL-86 stops by design and whose output would have nowhere to go.


**The background service.** Decided on 2026-10-23 (OWL-432), roadmap P7, scenario S11, with the maintainer, after a review of the plan by two reviewers. P7 runs the drain without a terminal: started at login, kept alive, stopped without leaving a run unchecked when the machine logs out or shuts down, and silent unless a person is the blocker. The commands and their files are [runtime and operations](runtime-and-operations.md), "How it runs" and "Install, lifecycle & uninstall"; this section holds the rules.

- **A supervisor.** `owlshift daemon`, started by the operating system's service manager (a user LaunchAgent on macOS, a systemd user unit on Linux and WSL2), holds what one process per machine must hold: one instance, the services file it reads, the digest. It runs one `owlshift watch --drain` per served project as its child, a process tree of its own registered with the daemon's sentinel, so the watches and their steps die with the daemon however it ends. Each watch is P6's, unchanged: its child per step, its locks, its run files and obligations, the slot and usage files.
  - *One instance.* `<data>/service/daemon.lock`, a `flock` taken with try-lock for the daemon's whole life, so a second daemon on the same data directory exits at once, saying which one holds it. The unit names `OWLSHIFT_DATA_DIR` and `OWLSHIFT_CONFIG_DIR` explicitly, so the service and a person's terminal never use two data directories without knowing it.
  - *Served projects.* `<data>/service/projects.json` (format 1): each served project's repository (`owner/repo`) and the checkout it was registered from. `owlshift start`, inside a checkout, adds its project; `owlshift stop --forget` removes it; the file is replaced whole, written to a temporary name then renamed, under `<data>/service/projects.lock`. The daemon reads it at each of its own passes (every 60 seconds), starting a watch for a project added and draining the watch of one removed.
  - *The served lock.* `<project data>/served.lock`, taken by the daemon, not by the watch child, exclusively and for as long as it serves the project, across the child's restarts. A watch typed in a terminal on a served project refuses at once, naming `owlshift stop`; `continue --to-watch` reads a served project as watched. The child keeps `watch.lock` shared, as in P6.
  - *Its configuration.* A served watch reads the project file from the default branch of the project's dedicated checkout (`refs/remotes/origin/HEAD`, as its gate, rules, zone owners and admitters are read), after a fetch it makes itself between two passes, at most once a minute and only when no other command holds the project's lock (built in OWL-434: a watch's pass did not fetch; only its steps did). The registered checkout only locates the project: `owlshift watch --drain --served owner/repo`, the daemon's own and hidden, finds it in `projects.json`, never from its working directory. The file in force is kept in `<project data>/served.json` (format 1), which the watch's step children read too, so a watch and its steps run on one text. A newer file accepted there takes effect once the steps under way end: the watch starts no step and runs no pass meanwhile, unlike a pause (below), then starts again on it in the same process. A project file Owlshift refuses there (it does not parse, asks for a variable or cache folder the operator does not allow, does not suit the drain, or is not a text file of at most 64 KiB) keeps the last one accepted, with one `warning` event and one notification until one is accepted again, recorded in `served.json` so a restarted watch does not tell it twice; with none ever accepted, the project is not served and `served.json` holds why, which `owlshift status` shows. The steps' own reads at their base commit (gate, rules, zone owners, admitters, reviewers) are unchanged, since a run's instructions come from the commit it works on: such a merge also stops them with their own refusal, as in a terminal. A watch typed in a terminal keeps reading the working tree it runs in. `owlshift doctor` and `owlshift status` say which file a project runs on.
  - *As built* (OWL-435, 2026-10-25). `owlshift daemon` takes `daemon.lock` for itself alone, trying for a quarter of a second so that a probe's instant hold is outlasted, and writes `daemon.json` (format 1: pid, version, binary, since), which it removes at its end; a second daemon exits 1, naming the first. It reads `projects.json` at each pass, every 60 seconds, and looks at its watches, the stop asked and the starts due every half second. Each watch is a process tree of its own, sent SIGTERM at the daemon's checked stop, started from the binary resolved at the daemon's start: on Linux `/proc/self/exe`, which runs that very file even once it is replaced on disk (checked on 2026-10-25 in debian:12, build plan, "P7 · Background service", part 4), and so are a watch's steps; elsewhere the path `current_exe` gave. Each line a watch writes is printed after its project's name, its last line of error kept as the reason it ended. A watch typed in a terminal shares `served.lock` for its life, so a daemon started after it says so once and serves the project once it ends. Restarts are those of "Restarts" below (`owlshift_core::restart`). A served watch that refuses before its loop ends with exit code 78, unless a restart may mend the refusal: a tracker or forge that did not answer, a default branch that could not be read while no project file was ever kept, and the project's lock held by another command end 1 and restart on backoff. So a keychain that refuses, a secret missing, a credential rejected, no project file accepted, a checkout gone or a host that cannot confine pause the project, a refusal at a start onto a newer project file included. The daemon's pause is `<project data>/pause` (`by` `owlshift daemon`, with the reason), written unless a pause stands, told once, and not again across the daemon's restarts while it stands; the project is tried again at each pass, and the pause removed once a watch so started still runs a pass later; a person's pause is left as it is. An exit 78 leaves the run of ends as it was. A change to the first text ("Drain", below, and the build plan's part 2): `owlshift stop` writes `<data>/service/stop` (one JSON line: who, when, why) and returns; the daemon writes that request as the stop file of each watch it runs, starts and restarts nothing more, and exits 0 once they have ended, removing the request and any stop file of its own that still stands, so no stop file is left for a watch waiting to start again.
