A daemon that finds the request at its start serves nothing and exits 0, as a watch does with its stop file; `start` removes one left while no daemon runs, and `stop` its own when the daemon ended meanwhile. A project no longer listed has its watch drained the same way, its served lock and the daemon's pause let go. A Ctrl-C of a daemon in a terminal is a drain, a second one or SIGTERM a checked stop, which the platform passes to each watch; the daemon has no bound of its own, each watch carrying its own. Plain `owlshift start`, when no daemon runs, starts `owlshift daemon` for the session as a process of its own, in a process group of its own out of the terminal's reach, its output appended to `<data>/service/daemon.log` (readable by the user alone), `OWLSHIFT_DATA_DIR` and `OWLSHIFT_CONFIG_DIR` named; nothing starts it again after a crash before the next `start`. `daemon` and `start` are refused on native Windows (D9).
- **Restarts.** A watch child that ends without being asked is started again after a delay that doubles from 10 seconds up to an hour, reset once a child has run for 10 minutes; after three ends in a row the operator is notified once, naming the project and the end. A child that ends on a failure no restart can mend, a keychain that refuses its secrets or a credential the tracker or forge rejects, ends with an exit code of its own: the daemon pauses that project (below), notifies once, and tries it again at its next pass, so a keychain locked while the screen was locked serves again once it opens. The service manager restarts the daemon itself after a crash or a kill, never after a stop it was asked for (`KeepAlive` restarting an unsuccessful exit only, `SuccessfulExit` false, with a `ThrottleInterval` of 30 seconds; `Restart=on-failure`, `RestartSec=30` and no start limit), so neither gives up for good. The daemon's exit code says which: 0 once a stop asked of it is done, by `owlshift stop` or by SIGTERM's checked stop, and 0 from the launcher that finds its binary gone, so nothing starts it again before the next login or `start`; 75 once an upgrade's drain is done, so the manager starts it again, on the new binary. Decided on 2026-10-24 from OWL-433's live checks, a change to the first text: under its `KeepAlive` true launchd started a job again 30 s after every exit 0, so `owlshift stop` would have lasted 30 s and a launcher without its binary would have run every 30 s until the session ended, while `Restart=on-failure` starts nothing again after an exit 0, so an upgrade ending 0 would have left the daemon stopped under systemd ([build plan](build-plan.md), "P7 · Background service", L1 and L2).
- **Three ways to stop.** Every watch, served or typed in a terminal, knows three stops, the strongest one asked winning:
  - *Drain:* no new dispatch and no new step of any ticket; the steps under way finish; then the watch exits. Asked by `<project data>/stop` (written by `owlshift stop` or by the upgrade below), or by the first Ctrl-C of a watch in a terminal, which prints that a second one stops checked and a third now. OWL-161's graceful stop. Decided on 2026-10-24 in OWL-161's alignment, a change to the first text, which had the first Ctrl-C write the stop file: the first Ctrl-C asks the drain of its own watch alone, held in memory, and the stop file stays a drain asked from outside the process, removed by the watch that drains on it. Written by a Ctrl-C, the file would outlive a watch stopped "now" after it, so the next watch would drain at once, and it would drain every other watch of the project typed in another terminal.
  - *Checked stop:* each step's runs are stopped at once, as a usage limit cuts a run, and each one's isolation is checked with the snapshot the step still holds, before the step and then the watch exit. A clean check retires the run's obligation and leaves the ticket at its stage with an outcome `stopped`, resumed at the next start as a run a usage limit cut is resumed (section 5, "Parallel", "Usage limits"), from its checkpoint, or from the step's start when the checkpoint does not parse; a breach is the quarantine, as at any check. Asked by SIGTERM, which is what the service manager sends at a logout, a shutdown, `launchctl bootout` or `systemctl --user stop`, and by the second Ctrl-C in a terminal. The watch forwards it to each step child as SIGTERM to the child's own process, not its group, so the step stops its runs itself; it waits for its children up to 45 seconds (`CHECKED_STOP_BOUND`), then stops their trees. The units give the daemon 90 seconds on Linux (`TimeoutStopSec`, held at `systemctl --user stop`, at the end of a session and at a power-off, checked on 2026-10-24 in a container) and 60 on macOS (`ExitTimeOut` 60: checked on 2026-10-24, launchd kills the job 60 s after SIGTERM at a bootout whatever larger value the plist names, 90 or 120). The rule they set: the watch's bound on its children ends before the smallest grace measured, less a margin for the daemon's own exit, so a checked stop finishes before the service manager's SIGKILL. A logout and a shutdown on macOS also gave 60 s (checked on 2026-10-24, build plan, "P7 · Background service", L1), so 60 s is the smallest grace. Decided on 2026-10-25 (OWL-450): the bound is 45 seconds, which leaves 15 for what is not under the bound: the time before it starts (the signal's forwarding, and the trees a plain watch stops at once), then the watch stopping the step trees, which it does by freeze-and-search ("Which processes"), then exiting, then the daemon exiting; the daemon adds no wait of its own on its watches beyond this one. The daemon is not built when this is written, so the margin is not measured; a test pins the bound at 15 seconds or more below the smallest grace. The units' graces were decided on 2026-10-24 from OWL-433's live checks, a change to the first text, which gave both units 90 seconds.
  - *Now:* every tree stopped at once, P6's policy: a third Ctrl-C in a terminal, SIGQUIT, or a checked stop past the bound above.
  - *As built* (OWL-161, 2026-10-24). Each Ctrl-C asks one degree more than the one asked (drain, checked, now); SIGTERM, and SIGHUP of a closed terminal, ask a checked stop unless one is asked; SIGQUIT asks now. Plain `owlshift watch`, whose steps run in its own process, stops its own runs at a checked stop as a step's child does. Every other command keeps the policy of "now" for every signal; native Windows, where `watch` is refused before any step (D9), keeps it too.
    - *Which processes.* Each process tree says what a checked stop does to it: a run's harness and the gate's and setup's commands are stopped at once; a step's child is sent SIGTERM; the runner's own git, its probes and its other commands are left to end, since the check needs them. A tree started after the checked stop is acted on as it starts, and no step's child starts once any stop is asked, so a drain lets no new child start; a step of plain `watch` that passed its check before the drain was asked runs to its end. A child told before it put its own policy in place, in the first moments of its life, ends at once, having written nothing, its step unreported, run again at the next start. What left a tree's group (OWL-451): Claude Code runs each Bash call of its run in a session and process group of its own (`setsid`, observed on 2026-10-24 and 2026-10-25, build plan, "P7 · Background service", L7), which a signal to the harness's group does not reach. So on macOS and Linux, stopping any process tree, at once or at a checked stop, freezes the tree's group (SIGSTOP), then searches the process table for every process descending by parent pid from one of the tree's, and every member of a process group one of those leads; it freezes each one found and searches again until a search made once every frozen process has stopped finds none new, so that a process started in the meantime is found too (eight searches at most, nothing waited on); it checks each one again, kills those whose parent, group and start time are unchanged with the group, and lets go of any other, never killing a process it cannot tell is the one it found (the start time compared since OWL-452, for every tree, so that a pid reused meanwhile is never killed). The search is the runner's own. The ticket's first path, SIGTERM to Claude Code then the group killed after a bound, was considered, since the probe of 2026-10-25 showed Claude Code ending a running call's group within half a second of SIGTERM, and set aside: that is Claude Code's own handler, which a later version may drop and which Codex was never checked for, and an isolation guardrail is enforced by the runner, never asked of a harness or a model; it would also make every stop wait, and it ends nothing the search misses. What a run left (OWL-452): a background job a Bash call left once that call returned has neither its parent nor its group's leader any more, so the search above does not find it, and it outlives SIGTERM to Claude Code and a run that ends by itself (checked on 2026-10-25). So a run's harness tree is tied to the run's worktree, and every stop of it, at once, at a checked stop, at its deadline, and once the run ended by itself, also searches from each process the run left: one whose working directory is the worktree or inside it, that started no earlier than the tree's root (read right after its spawn; on macOS, which describes no zombie, a root already gone by then is bounded by the clock read just before its spawn, less 10 ms), whose parent is where an orphan is handed (pid 1, or on Linux one of the stopping process's own ancestors, the nearest subreaper above it, such as `systemd --user` under the unit), and whose process group's leader has ended; never the stopping process nor one of its ancestors. What descends from those is found as above, and all are frozen, checked and killed with the group; once the run ended by itself, its group empty, only those are searched from. Checked on 2026-10-25 and 2026-10-26 ([owl-452.sh](live-checks/owl-452.sh), build plan, "P7 · Background service", L7): the working directory of another process of the same user is read by `proc_pidinfo` on macOS, a confined one's and an Apple binary's included, and from `/proc/<pid>/cwd` on Linux; a real Claude Code run's job, once the run ended, had parent 1, its group's leader gone, a start after Claude Code's and its working directory in the run's own; under bwrap's pid namespace such a job descends from the tree and ends with it, so the tie matters on macOS and for an unconfined run on Linux. The rules tie a process to the run against what a run leaves by accident, not against a process that evades them; the group leader's end spares an operator's editor or pager in a worktree, which leads its own group. The gate's and setup's commands are tied the same way (OWL-455, decided on 2026-10-28): each command's tree to the worktree it runs in, its search bounded by that command's own root, so a job a command leaves there, such as a nested shell's in a group of its own once that shell ended, is stopped with it; a plain `cmd &` stays in the command's group, which was stopped already. A command's leftovers are stopped at the end of that command, once its root exits and before the next command starts, and at the gate's or the setup's deadline and at a checked stop; never later, at the step's end, which would let a setup's leftovers run through the checks between the setup and the gate, and on into the Build's harness, whose own tie would not reach them, started before its root. What the tie reaches is frozen before it is killed, so none of it runs once its command is done, and what it wrote before is read by the checks that follow (the guard, the clean worktree and HEAD); the limits are the ones above, eight searches at most, each best effort, and what the tie does not reach. So no setup command leaves a server or a daemon running for the gate or the harness: `stack.setup` installs the gate's dependencies (OWL-330), and a server the Build needs is started by the harness inside its own tied run; a build daemon a command leaves without a session of its own starts again in the next command, as it already does inside bwrap's pid namespace on Linux. Rejected: the commands left untied, where such a job outlives the checks on macOS and for an unconfined command on Linux. Rejected: a harness setting that ends what each Bash call leaves (`CLAUDE_CODE_SHELL_PREFIX`), which asks the harness to enforce a guardrail, covers no other harness and ends a server an agent starts in one call to test it in the next; a subreaper or a control group per run, Linux only, where bwrap's pid namespace already holds a confined run; following process creation during a run, which races a call that lasts milliseconds. Not reached: what a run left that leads its own process group (`setsid cmd &`, a daemon) or works outside the worktree, even one writing in it by a path; a process whose working directory the system does not show the runner, such as one running as another user, or on Linux a non-dumpable one; and, at a hard kill of the daemon, whatever left its group, since the sentinels kill groups only ("A cut run", below), which on Linux the service unit's control group ends with the daemon.
    - *A stopped run.* A run that failed while a checked stop was asked, cut or not, ends `stopped`; one that finished, broke isolation or met its usage limit ends as it would have. A step asked to stop between two runs starts no other: its next run ends `stopped` before it writes its obligation, what the earlier runs kept staying kept, as at a usage limit. A base check (section 5, "A broken base") cut so keeps no verdict, and a Build's result kept for its gate alone stays kept for the next start. `stopped` changes no stage and counts no failure (the core's interruption), notifies no one, holds nothing in watch, so the ticket resumes at the next start, and drops `carry.json` as a usage limit does. The step's outcome kind is `stopped` (format 5) and the run's `run_ended` outcome `stopped`. A Build's checkpoint is its plan and ledger, given to the next run only when both are plain files of the worktree's run folder, read through no link, the ledger parsing as JSON and the plan as UTF-8: otherwise the run starts from the step's start.
    - *Ending.* Once any stop is asked, the watch runs no further pass: it waits for its children, applies the end of each, then exits 0, its tracker dropped as at any end, so the Linear app's token is revoked (and each step's). It prints one line and records one project `decision` event (`watch` `stop`, `stop` `drain` or `checked`, `asked` `signal` or `stop_file`) per degree it reaches. A stop file found when a watch starts is a drain asked: it starts nothing, removes the file and says so.
- **A cut run.** A run stopped "now", or cut by a crash, a hard kill of the daemon or a power loss, leaves its obligation, which stays the quarantine of section 8, notified. Checking it afterwards would need the run's snapshot and the sibling generations it read, persisted beside the obligation; the snapshot holds the shared git state and does not fit a record, and a crash cuts every step at once, so each cut run would read its cut siblings' unrecorded work as a breach. Left for a later ticket if the exit gate shows crashes are frequent: the gate records each cut and what it cost. What a hard kill of the daemon leaves, checked on 2026-10-24 (L7): through the chain of sentinels, no process of the watches and their steps, except a descendant that left its group with `setsid` or `setpgid`, which every Bash call of a Claude Code run does; inside the Linux unit, nothing, its control group killed with the daemon.
- **Pause.** `<data>/service/pause` pauses every project of the machine, `<project data>/pause` one project: while it stands a watch dispatches nothing and starts no step, the steps under way finish, and its passes go on reading answers, merges and stages, so `owlshift status` stays current; removed, the next pass goes on. `owlshift pause [owner/repo]` and `owlshift resume [owner/repo]` write and remove them; the daemon's own pause of a project (above) is the same file, with its reason in it. S12's pause.
- **An upgrade.** The daemon and its children run the binary resolved at the daemon's start (`current_exe`, kept as an open handle where the system allows, so a binary replaced on disk is not the one a later spawn runs). A newer `owlshift`, at `start` or at `doctor`, finds the daemon of another version from `<data>/service/daemon.json` (its pid, version and binary path) and writes the drain to every served project and to `<data>/service/drain`; once the last watch has exited, the daemon exits with 75 ("Restarts", above), and the service manager starts it again on the new binary (checked on 2026-10-24: a launcher executing a binary replaced on disk ran the new one at the restart). A run file, obligation or ticket ref of a newer format than the running binary reads is refused as today, so a downgrade stops rather than misreads.
- **Keep awake.** A step child that holds a slot keeps the machine from idle sleep for its own life: on macOS `caffeinate -i -w <pid>` started for the child's pid; on Linux a `systemd-inhibit` held beside the child, not around it, ended when the child ends, asking `--what=idle:sleep`, then `--what=idle` when that is refused, then nothing, with one warning for each refusal; a refusal never holds the step back. So the assertion ends with the child however it ends. Decided on 2026-10-24 from OWL-433's live checks, a change to the first text, which put the child inside `systemd-inhibit`: from a user unit, and from a session with no seat, logind refused `idle:sleep` and `sleep` (its polkit policy gives `inhibit-block-sleep` to sessions only) and `systemd-inhibit` then exited 1 without running its command, while `idle` alone was held (build plan, L5); a desktop's active session was not checked. Part 8 (OWL-439) builds it. *As built* (OWL-439, 2026-10-25): a step's child, once its slot is held, takes the assertion just before its step, after the keychain and the adapters, unless the personal file says `keep_awake = false`. On Linux the inhibitor's command says `held`, then reads its standard input, a pipe only the child writes, to its end, so the child's end, however it comes, ends the inhibitor; a refusal is told apart by the missing `held`, and an inhibitor silent for 5 s counts as a refusal and is stopped; a `systemd-inhibit` that cannot start is one warning, and nothing more is asked. Each refusal is a `warning` event (`keep_awake_refused`, with what was asked and the reason), so a host without logind warns twice per step until `keep_awake = false`. That `caffeinate` holds once started is not checked. Plain `owlshift watch`, whose steps run in its own process, and a person's `do` or `continue` hold none. A laptop's lid closed on battery still sleeps; a sleep freezes the daemon and its steps, which go on at wake, and the runner's deadlines, measured with `Instant`, do not count the time asleep (checked on 2026-10-24 on macOS, build plan, L6).
- **The digest.** Once a day, at `[service] digest_hour` of the personal file (8 by default, local time), one notification by the existing notifier: tickets delivered, waiting for a person, parked, paused projects, usage, ending with `owlshift status`. `<data>/service/digest.json` keeps when it was last sent, so a machine asleep at the hour sends it once at wake, never twice. Off with `digest = false`. *As built* (OWL-440, 2026-10-25): the daemon looks at each of its passes, never once a stop is asked, with the settings it read at its start (`daemon::digest`). A digest is due once a digest hour, in the machine's time zone at the pass, has passed since the record's `at` (format 1: `at`, and the latest digest's `line` for `owlshift status`); a day whose hour falls in a daylight-saving gap takes the first time after it. With no record, the first pass writes one counting from then and sends nothing, so the first digest comes at the first hour after the daemon first ran; a record that does not parse is replaced so, and one of a newer format sends nothing while it stands. The record is written before the notification is shown and kept in memory, so neither a crash after it nor a record that cannot be written sends one twice; a lost one is accepted. It counts the tickets of the served projects whose delivery opened or pushed a pull request since `at` (the forge's `pull_request` events), the tickets waiting for an answer and the parked ones from their refs, the paused projects (`<data>/service/pause`, each `<project data>/pause`), and each harness at its limit or cap, or its highest share of a window not yet reset; up to five tickets are named per list. It is made and recorded with no notifier too, shown by none; a `decision` event (`service` `digest`) records each, and a notification not shown is a `warning` event, both of the machine (`project` `owlshift daemon`). Its live check is L4 (build plan, "P7 · Background service").
- **`owlshift status`.** Read-only, without a lock: the daemon (running, version, since), each served project and its watch (running, restarting and why, paused and why, draining), the steps under way from the slot holders, the tickets waiting for a person, each harness's usage and reset, the latest digest. It reads the files above, the slot files and the event log; no channel to the daemon.
- **Dispatch and resume.** A dispatch is a ticket taken from the ready column, a `dispatch` event with the command `drain`; a resume is a later step of a ticket already dispatched, its run's `run_started` naming the stage it resumes. No double dispatch means no two runs of one ticket overlapping in time (a `run_started` before the previous run's `run_ended`) and no second `drain` dispatch of a ticket that has not ended (delivered, parked, canceled or moved out by a person); the exit gate reads both from the events.
- **Rejected.** A single runner process running every project's passes, which compares priorities but rewrites watch and `OnDemand` around several projects at once and stops every project with one crash; one service unit per project, which leaves nothing to hold one instance, the served lock or the digest; a control socket in P7, for which pause, stop and status need nothing files cannot carry, with a local API's peer checks and stale sockets to keep; children that outlive the daemon, which a new daemon would have to tell from a watch a person typed; the run's snapshot persisted to check a cut run afterwards ("A cut run", above).

## 6. Adapters & contracts

Five adapter kinds, each declaring its capabilities; `init` refuses a project whose adapters miss a required capability and applies a documented fallback for each optional one.

| Kind | What the core needs | First (P1 to P5) | Kept in mind |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Tracker | Tickets, comments, visible stage (table below) | Linear; a test tracker in the repo | GitHub Issues and the public Markdown tracker in P9; GitLab, Jira, Plane |
| Forge | Open a PR, read the complete check set and review comments, push branches and custom refs, read merge state; optionally, merge a PR at an expected head after reading its review decision (`MergePullRequest`, OWL-446), needed only when `[forge] merge` is not `never`, a person merging without it | GitHub, through the user token of Owlshift's GitHub App (D13) | GitLab, Gitea, Bitbucket |
| Harness | Run one role headless in a directory through the vendor's own CLI and the login that CLI's agent runs use (Codex's own, the stored `claude setup-token` token for Claude Code), with a model, an effort and a permission level; honour the result-file contract; report usage and usage limits | Claude Code, Codex | OpenCode, Gemini CLI; a third one on paper before the contract freezes |
| Notifier | Send one short line to one person | Tracker mention; a generic webhook | Chat apps, e-mail, push services |
| Stack profile | Declarative: install, full gate (lint, formatter, tests), dev servers and ports, resource patterns such as migration paths, path-scoped rules to inject | Hand-written for the first adopter | Detection presets per ecosystem in `init` |

The forge's merge capability is declared by the GitHub adapter; refusing a `[forge] merge` other than `never` on a forge without it is not built yet, GitHub being the only forge (the forge trait comes with the second adapter).

The Markdown tracker is not a toy: it is the core's test double, the zero-account demo, and the mode for projects without a tracker.

**Tracker capabilities.**

| Capability | Required | Fallback when missing |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Read a ticket: title, description, priority, assignee, labels, author | yes | none |
| List admitted tickets | yes | none |
| Read and write comments with author and last-edit time | yes | none |
| Show the visible stage (ready, in progress, needs input, in review) | yes | `agent:*` labels when states are fixed (GitHub Issues is open/closed only) |
| Blocked-by relations | no | a parsed text convention (`Blocked by #123`) |
| A proposals inbox for follow-ups | no | an `agent:proposed` label and a saved view |
| Documents attached to a ticket, created and updated in place (OWL-350) | no | the whole text in a comment |
| Resolve a comment, to fold the runner's superseded ones (OWL-353; on Linear `commentResolve(id)` without `resolvingCommentId`, which Linear answered with an internal error on 2026-10-19) | no | every comment stays expanded |
| Read a comment's reactions, so a thumbs-up answers a question asked alone (OWL-371) | no | only a typed answer answers |
| A distinct agent identity | no | an `[agent]` marker on comments and an external notifier |
| Webhooks | no | polling, always supported |

Jira is the known hard case: its workflows forbid some transitions, so its adapter must find a path between two states or fail at `init`, never at run time.

**GitHub credentials** (OWL-336, decided on 2026-10-17 with the maintainer). The forge adapter calls GitHub with the user access token of Owlshift's own GitHub App (D13), which `owlshift init` gets through the device flow: the user enters a code on GitHub, and no token is made, scoped or pasted by hand. The app is the maintainer's, public so any account can install it, and asks Pull requests (write), Contents (read and write, since D14) and Checks, Commit statuses, Actions and Metadata (read); its client ID and slug ship in the binary as two constants, each with a personal-file override, `github_app_client_id` and `github_app_slug`. A user token acts as the user, within the permissions the app asks and on the repositories it is installed on; it made every call of the adapter, checked live on 2026-10-19 ([build plan](build-plan.md#results), C9 to C12 and C15), with two points unverified there: a status check rollup holding check runs, since the head read had none, and the author of the pull request it opens. GA6 settles both on a delivered pull request ([build plan](build-plan.md#github-app-sign-in-owl-336)). The adapter's capabilities thus depend on the credential. A personal token, stored with `owlshift init --github-token`, is the expert option: it can do what it was given, and a fine-grained token has no Checks permission, so watch cannot read a pull request's status check rollup with one (OWL-334). The push stays on the `git` CLI and the user's own git credentials; the app asked Contents (read) only until D14, whose merge needs Contents (write) (section 5, "Merge on green"). That permission also lets the token push to any branch of the repositories the app is installed on, which is why it never leaves the runner. Agents hold neither token (section 8, "The GitHub App's tokens").

**Harness contract.** A harness is always the vendor's own CLI, run on the user's own subscription: Owlshift never calls a model API itself. A confined Claude Code run logs in with a token of that subscription the operator made with `claude setup-token` (OWL-94). A run gets a brief file in and must leave a `result.json` out (status, numbered questions, decisions taken, follow-ups, PR data). A read-only run that cannot write it gives it as its last message, which the runner reads (OWL-323): Codex always so (section 5, "The Codex harness"); on Claude Code only when a run its harness reports completed left nothing at the result's place, a file left there always winning, and always for the resolver's run with Bash (OWL-344), whose harness applies the rule that follows. The runner takes the whole last message when it parses as JSON, else the body of its last fenced code block (fences of at least three backticks at the start of a line, the closing one at least as long as the opening one and followed by nothing but whitespace), else nothing, and the run left no result. What it takes is written at the result's place, through no link, and judged as any result: validated against the brief, refused when it holds a login secret; a `run_warning` event says so, naming the tools the harness refused. The read-only prompts ask for the JSON alone; the fenced block is what a model still gives when told so (Locary's first run, build plan C1). A write-in-worktree run (Build, rebase) gets no such reading. Permission levels map onto each harness's own mechanism: permission modes and tool lists for Claude Code, sandbox modes for Codex. A harness adapter builds the command and reads the run's output; the executor spawns the command in a process group of its own, so stopping a run, timeouts and the run log stay with the executor whatever the harness ([build plan](build-plan.md#cli-surface-for-p0-and-p1), OWL-14). The runner injects project conventions itself, including path-scoped rules for the ticket's declared zones, so a harness's own instruction-loading mechanism never decides what an agent knows. Since P1 it injects the files the project names in `stack.rules`, by default the root `AGENTS.md`, as they stand at the base commit, each a rule for the whole repository; path-scoped rules arrive with the zones intake declares ([build plan](build-plan.md#cli-surface-for-p0-and-p1), OWL-61, OWL-67). Checked on 2026-09-27 on local installs: `claude -p` accepts `--model`, `--effort`, `--json-schema`, `--max-budget-usd` and `--permission-mode`; `codex exec` accepts `-m`, `-s` (sandbox) and `-C`, while `codex review` rejects `-m`.

**Extensibility.** Built-in adapters come first. Once the contracts are stable, an out-of-process adapter protocol (JSON over stdio, in the spirit of LSP and MCP) lets anyone write an adapter in any language.

## 7. State, coordination & multiple developers

Shared state lives where every participant already has access, the tracker and the git remote, so several developers and machines coordinate without any server.

| State | Where | Why there |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Visible stage, questions, answers, decisions, delivery reports | Tracker | People read and answer there |
| Claim on a ticket, with a lease | A git ref per ticket under a product namespace | An atomic update on the remote: exactly one runner wins |
| Pipeline position, round counters, checkpoint, plan, ledger, review findings | Git refs per ticket | Any runner on any machine can resume |
| Code | The ticket's branch | As usual |
| Run history, measured cost | Local SQLite; a cost summary copied to the ticket | Per-operator accounting |

**Claims.** Built with several runners (P10, moved from P7 on 2026-10-23, OWL-432): on one machine, P6 keeps tickets apart with a lock per ticket (section 5, "Parallel"), the background service included (section 5, "The background service"). A runner claims a ticket by creating its claim ref with "absent" as the expected old value; the git server applies ref updates as compare-and-swap, so a second runner's push is rejected. The holder renews a lease at every run; a lease older than a configured limit can be taken over, which recovers the tickets of a laptop that went to sleep. Whether each forge accepts pushes to a custom ref namespace is checked per forge adapter.

**Privacy.** A public repository paired with a private tracker would publish plans and questions through git refs. The artifact store is therefore configurable: git refs (default), a local directory (solo use), or tracker attachments.

**People.** Four roles, which one person holds alone in solo use:

- **Decider**: answers the gates of a ticket; its assignee, or for a ticket without one, the single owner of the code zones it touches (section 4).
- **Delegator**: admits a ticket for an agent to work.
- **Reviewer**: reviews and merges the PR.
- **Operator**: runs a runner; its runs consume the operator's own subscriptions.

**Which runner takes which ticket.** A developer's runner takes only the tickets its operator delegated, so one person's machine never spends another person's budget. A team server takes every admitted ticket.

**In flight** means everything claimed by any runner, every ticket a human has in progress, and every open PR; resources are computed across all of them, so an agent never starts on a zone a colleague is editing by hand. From P6 (section 5, "Parallel", "The in-flight set"): Owlshift's tickets from their dispatch until their pull request ends, the repository's other open pull requests by the files they change, and people's tickets in the working state by their `zone:` labels, a person's ticket with neither a pull request nor a label left out and warned of (decided with the maintainer on 2026-10-20).

**Identities.** A person maps to a tracker account and a forge account in the project config, so a PR review comment and a ticket comment count as the same decider. The runner posts under a distinct agent identity wherever the tracker offers one: Linear app users can be delegated issues while the human assignee stays the owner, and are not billed as seats ([Linear docs](https://linear.app/docs/agents-in-linear)); Owlshift's own polls, so it takes neither delegation nor mentions (D8); on GitHub, a GitHub App acting as itself, a P10 step ([roadmap](roadmap.md)). The GitHub App of D13 is not that identity: its user token acts as the user who signed in, so the pull requests it opens are that person's, an assumption still unverified (the author was not read, [build plan](build-plan.md#results) C11, 2026-10-19). Elsewhere, the `[agent]` marker applies.

## 8. Policy, security & trust

The floor is code a project cannot configure away: no agent merges, deploys, changes infrastructure or holds tracker credentials, and nothing written by an unauthorised person is ever treated as an instruction. The one merge a project can grant is the runner's own, under merge on green (D14), and the floor checks that grant.

**The floor.**

- No agent merges, approval or not. The Writer merges only under merge on green (D14, section 5, "Merge on green"): `floor::check_action` refuses `Action::Merge` unless the call carries a merge grant, the project's `[forge] merge` read at the base branch's head allowing this ticket, and the expected head, the commit Owlshift last pushed; it never merges a pull request Owlshift does not follow and never asks GitHub to bypass a protection, and it keeps a required review itself, from a last read made right before the call, whatever its token may bypass (OWL-446). Without the grant a human merges, as before D14. Owlshift force-pushes only its ticket's own branch, after a rebase run, leased on its own last push (OWL-208, section 5, "Watch after delivery"); every other push of a branch is a fast-forward, and an agent never pushes. No deploy or infrastructure change (environment variables, feature flags, DNS, CI settings) without an explicit human approval recorded on the ticket.
- Owlshift never handles model API keys: Codex authentication stays in Codex's own configuration. The one model login it holds is the token confined Claude Code runs log in with, made by the operator with `claude setup-token`, kept in the system keychain, read by the runner and handed to the harness command alone, on a pipe it inherits (OWL-96): never in any environment, the agent's included, a gate command, a probe, a file Owlshift writes, an event or a message, and replaced by `<redacted>` in the run's logs (OWL-94).
- Agents hold no tracker, forge or cloud credentials; the Writer is their only holder. An agent starts from an empty environment: what a program needs to run, where a harness other than Claude Code finds its login, the operator's proxy unless it holds a login, which refuses the run (OWL-76), and the variables the project declares for its own gate (`stack.gate_env`) that the operator allows in the personal configuration (`allow_gate_env`): a project's declaration never widens that list, as with the home caches it declares (`stack.caches`, `allow_caches`, OWL-330), and a known credential, a variable of Claude Code's own (`ANTHROPIC_*`, `CLAUDE_*`) or of Codex's (`CODEX_*`, `OPENAI_*`), which chooses where a run sends its requests and with which login, or one that makes a program load code as it starts (`LD_*`, `DYLD_*`, `BASH_ENV`, `NODE_OPTIONS`), is refused even when listed (OWL-63, OWL-120, OWL-125). Git and gh are left without a credential, a harness loads no settings file, the user's or the repository's, and no MCP server, and the Executor checks this before each run ([build plan](build-plan.md#results), OWL-22); a Claude Code run that reports an API key or an MCP server fails.
- **The GitHub App's tokens** (OWL-336, D13). The user access token and its refresh token, with the access token's expiry, are kept as one value under the project's keychain account `github.com/<owner>/<repo>/github-app` (the build plan's "P5 · Quality pipeline", part K), so a rotation is one keychain write. They are read by the runner alone and never put in an agent's environment, a brief, an event, a file Owlshift writes or a log. User-token expiration is on: the access token lives 8 hours and the refresh token 182 days, and the refresh grant takes the client ID alone, so no secret of the app sits on a user's machine: branch R of [check C13](build-plan.md#results), 2026-10-19.
  - *The refresh.* It is the Writer's: one function of the runner, which the Writer's own calls, watch's forge reads and `doctor`'s token line all go through; no agent process holds or reaches the tokens. It runs on an access token past its stored expiry, or on a 401 while a refresh token is stored, and the call is then made once more. A refresh rotates both tokens and revokes the access token it replaces (C13), so the new value is written to the keychain before the new access token is used.
  - *The account lock.* Two refreshes with one refresh token lose one of them (C13), and forge reads are made without the project lock ("Polling and the forge's budget", section 5), whose `Project::lock` answers busy within a quarter of a second rather than wait for its holder (section 5, "A lock just released"). So the refresh is serialised by a lock of its own, the account lock: a file `github-app.lock` beside the project's `lock`, taken with the same `flock` through one helper the two share, for the refresh and its keychain write alone, with a bounded wait. It is never the project lock, never held around a command, and never held while the project lock is waited for. Under it the runner reads the keychain again and refreshes only while the stored access token is still the one it found expired or was refused with; otherwise it uses the stored one. The kernel drops a crashed holder's `flock`; a child spawned while the lock was held can keep the file open a moment after its release (`Project::lock`'s note), which the bounded wait outlasts.
  - *The refresh-failure rule.* A refresh GitHub refuses (a revoked authorization, a refresh token past its life or already used), or a rotation lost to a crash between GitHub's answer and the keychain write, is a typed forge error that stops the command and names `owlshift init`, worded "sign in again": `init` takes a stored token refused this way for none and signs in again, the new value replacing it, and stores a token only once the app's installation lists the repository ([build plan](build-plan.md#cli-surface-for-p0-and-p1), "`init`"). It never falls back to a personal token. That a revoked authorization refuses both tokens is unverified (C17 not observed, 2026-10-19).
- A process that goes looking is stopped by the operating system (OWL-41): the Executor runs every agent, the harness and everything it starts, every gate command and every setup command (`stack.setup`: what a project runs to install its gate's dependencies, before a Build's harness and before each gate, OWL-330) inside a sandbox it builds itself, `sandbox-exec` on macOS and `bwrap` on Linux. The home is unreadable but for the folders a run needs, among them the tool chains its declared variables name and the caches the project declares (`stack.caches`) that the operator allows in the personal configuration (`allow_caches`), read-only and never a folder that is, lies in or holds a credential path (OWL-68, OWL-330); nothing is written outside the worktree, the repository's git folder (not its hooks, its configuration or the entries of other worktrees in `worktrees/`, the run's own entry being written, OWL-339) and the run's own temporary folder, one folder for every command of the run on macOS and Linux alike (OWL-330), the temporary folders the user's other processes share are closed, and the system credential store (the Keychain, the Secret Service) is closed. Claude Code therefore runs on the token above, in an empty configuration folder inside the run's temporary folder, gone with the run (OWL-94), and keeps its temporary files in a folder of that one too, clear of the user's own `/tmp/claude-<uid>` (OWL-100). A machine that cannot confine an agent runs none: native Windows is refused and pointed to WSL2 (decision D9), and a Linux machine without a usable `bwrap` is refused, with `owlshift doctor` saying how to fix it. Known limits, recorded with their live checks in the [build plan](build-plan.md#results): the network stays open, so an agent can reach anything it may read, the agent login included, which its shell commands inherit and the operator can revoke; secrets outside the home on shared mounts are not hidden; on macOS a process that leaves the run's process group with `setsid` is not stopped with it, and `sandbox-exec` is deprecated and does not nest.
- Two gate categories are always human, whatever the project's settings: security and irreversible external actions never reach the resolver (OWL-328). Data loss, money, legal wording and scope changes go to a human too unless the project's delegation level hands them to the resolver (section 4, "Delegation level"); a project may add categories, which stay human at every level. A question names its category with a token: `security`, `data_loss`, `money`, `legal`, `irreversible` or `scope`. A category that contains a token as whole words, such as `scope_change` or `risk of data loss`, counts as that token, and a question with no category goes to a human. The resolver's own label on a question it decides is held to the level's always-human set and must be a token: an always-human or malformed label sends the question to a human (OWL-144).
- After every run, and after the gate, the Executor checks isolation: main checkout untouched, the shared git files and the other worktrees' entries in the common git folder untouched (the sandbox closes them to a run, the check finds a write that got past it, OWL-339), diff inside the worktree, expected branch. A violation quarantines the run and parks the ticket. Decided on 2026-10-19 (OWL-369): a run other than a rebase may only add to its branch, and the Build role says so; it never rewrites a commit the branch held when the run started (no `rebase`, `commit --amend`, `reset` or `cherry-pick` over it), and only the `rebase` role moves a branch onto a new base. OWL-160's Build had rebased its branch onto the same base, which its prompt did not forbid, and the breach quarantined the project for about 8.5 hours. A rewrite stays a breach. When the branch no longer descends from the run's start but still shares a commit with it, the history breach's message adds "the run rewrote its branch's history (rebase, amend or reset)", so a person reads in one line where that breach came from; it says nothing of the run's other violations, and a rewrite with no shared commit left, such as an amended root commit, keeps the plain message. Rejected: accepting a rewrite that keeps the start's tree reachable and touches only the ticket's branch, reported as an event, which loosens the check for a gain the prompt gives for free.
- **The quarantine** (OWL-368, decided 2026-10-19). A violation also writes the project's `unverified` marker (since OWL-400 the quarantine, `<data>/quarantine`: "Isolation among steps", below), and while it stands every command refuses the whole project on that machine: no ticket of it is run, continued, taken in or built, since the dedicated checkout may hold what the run changed outside its worktree. On 2026-10-18 a breach of OWL-160's Build froze this project for about 8.5 hours with one line in watch's terminal as its only sign, so the quarantine now tells, once each, everyone it stops:
  - *The operator*, by the notifier, once per marker: the runner sends it as it writes a breach marker, naming the project, the run, the ticket, the kind of breach in fixed words and the command that clears it, and the park that follows sends nothing more. A marker no command notified, one a crash, a Ctrl-C or `owlshift gate` left, or one a command wrote as it refused a kept worktree changed between runs, is notified by the first watch pass that finds it. A record beside the marker, written under the project's lock, says it was notified, so no later pass and no other process notifies it again ([runtime and operations](runtime-and-operations.md), "Notifications"). The notification is the runner's, not the command's outcome's as for every other moment, because the marker is written there, by every command that runs an agent, and found there by watch.
  - *The ticket that broke isolation*: its PARKED comment says that the whole project is refused until the quarantine is cleared, and how.
  - *Each ticket it holds up*: a ticket whose decider's answer counts, the ticket the drain would dispatch, and, since OWL-456 (decided 2026-10-28), a ticket left at a stage watch would resume while the tracker still shows that stage and its step does not run, such as a Build a hard kill of the daemon cut or a step the quarantine stopped at its run's boundary, get one `decision` event and one short WAITING comment per quarantine, saying that it waits for the quarantine and goes on by itself once it is cleared. Such a ticket shows In Progress with nothing moving, as one a stage step's wait holds back does ("Told on the ticket", section 5); one whose step still runs is told once it has stopped. A followed ticket at Watch is not told: it shows In Review, with its pull request. To know them, watch reads the ticket refs of the quarantined checkout, with the runner's read-only plumbing alone (no hook, no file-system monitor, as `owlshift doctor` and `owlshift forget` already read them), and writes nothing there. Since OWL-423 (decided 2026-10-22) the comment is folded once the quarantine is cleared, as a wait's is ("Told on the ticket", section 5), and nothing is posted: watch keeps each comment it posted, by ticket, in a file of its own beside the quarantine, which the clear leaves; the first pass and the first drain snapshot that read the project and find no quarantine remove that record, then resolve each comment it named, so none is resolved twice. A new quarantine text that tells a ticket again folds the comment it had. The clear itself folds nothing: `owlshift unquarantine` needs no tracker and no valid `owlshift.toml`. Rejected: the clear folding them, which would need both; the quarantine's own record keeping the comments, which is written under the project's lock that watch only tries, and goes with the quarantine.
  - *`owlshift doctor`* fails on the marker, shows what it says and how to clear it.
  - *The clear*: `owlshift unquarantine`, in the project's repository on that machine, shows the marker and clears it once a person types the project's name (`owner/repo`) at a terminal. It removes the marker only: the checkout and the worktrees stay as they are, and a person who wants a fresh clone deletes them first. A terminal and a typed name are a speed bump against scripts and a coding agent's non-interactive shell, not a proof that a person typed it; an agent Owlshift launches cannot reach the marker at all, since its sandbox writes nothing outside its worktree, its git folder and its temporary folder (above). There is no `--yes`, and the marker is still a file a person may delete.
- **Isolation among steps** (OWL-398, decided 2026-10-20 and revised the same day after a review of the merged text, roadmap P6; section 5, "Parallel"). Until P6 the project lock kept runs apart, so the `unverified` marker, written as a run starts and removed when its check passes, meant one thing, and a check could take every ref but its run's branch as frozen. With steps of several tickets at once, a sibling's marker would read as a breach, a run that passes would remove the marker of one that broke out, and a sibling writing its own ticket ref would quarantine everyone. Built before the project lock is narrowed (section 5, "Locks"), so:
  - *Obligations and the quarantine.* A run writes its own obligation, `<data>/unverified/<run-id>`, naming its ticket, before it starts, and removes only that one when its check passes. A breach writes the project's quarantine, `<data>/quarantine`: the notification, the WAITING comments and `doctor`'s line of "The quarantine" above move to it, and every command refuses the project while it stands. An obligation whose ticket lock is free belongs to a run that ended without its check, a crash or a stop, and the first command or watch pass that finds one writes the quarantine, naming it: as a crash does today, work nobody checked is never resumed. `owlshift unquarantine` shows the quarantine and the obligations left behind, and once the person types the project's name removes the quarantine and those obligations, never one whose ticket lock is held; a step still running keeps its own and is checked as usual. `owlshift doctor` lists the obligations, running and left behind. As decided on 2026-10-21 (OWL-400): Since P7, a checked stop retires a stopped run's obligation through the same check, so only a stop "now", a crash or a hard kill leaves one behind (section 5, "The background service", "Three ways to stop").
    - An obligation is named `<ticket>-<run>`, since a run's id is unique within its ticket only. `owlshift gate`'s names no ticket: a holder of the project lock that finds it takes it as left behind, `gate` holding that lock for its whole length. Since OWL-401, when steps hold the project lock for short sections only, `gate` also answers busy while any ticket's lock is held: every step holds its ticket's lock for its whole length and starts with one of the project's sections, so a gate and a step never run at once, and the gate keeps the check of a run alone.
    - A breaching run's obligation stays until `owlshift unquarantine`. A breach while the quarantine stands is appended to it: a new text, which is notified again, the record of what was told (`quarantine-told`) being keyed by the quarantine's text.
    - The `unverified` file an Owlshift before P6 left is read as the quarantine, and `unquarantine` removes it.
    - *An obligation is never seen half written (OWL-425, decided 2026-10-22).* A step writes its obligation without the project lock, while a watch pass that holds it reads the folder. Created in place and filled afterwards, an obligation read in between was empty, so it did not parse and quarantined the project: once in 48 runs of `child_steps.rs`'s three-ticket test, six at a time, on 2026-10-22, and once in OWL-422's 29 loaded runs, where the cause was not kept. It is now written and synced as `unverified-<name>.partial` in the project's folder, which nothing lists, then hard-linked into `unverified/` and the partial removed. A hard link fails on a name already taken, where a rename would replace it. A partial left by a crash takes its name, as a written obligation does, and a run's id never comes again. Likewise, an obligation removed between the listing and its read is gone, not unreadable. Its run's check passed, and on the same bench the first fix alone still found 80 of 341 reads failing that way. So is one Windows refused while it was being deleted (next item). Pinned by `ticket_run::tests::an_obligation_is_never_seen_partly_written`, whose writer writes and removes 300 obligations while a reader lists the folder: on the code before, with 1000 obligations, 1902 of 17186 reads were empty. Not chosen: taking an unreadable obligation as in flight when its ticket's lock is held, since a lost record never widens what a check leaves out; reading it again after a pause, a wait on time.
    - *Windows refuses a read while its obligation is deleted (OWL-426, established 2026-10-23).* While OWL-425 was built, CI's Windows leg found reads of obligations refused as access denied: 5 of 163 on 2026-10-22 (PR #388, run 39755650884), then 3 of 178 (run 39760215551). Fixes were stacked before the cause was known: asking the file system whether a refused entry was being deleted (`STATUS_DELETE_PENDING`), put down to a virus scanner holding the new files; then, with 3 refusals left, skipping an entry gone when asked again and a move instead of the hard link on Windows. A probe on windows-latest settled it (Windows Server 2025 build 26100, NTFS, Defender's real-time protection off), with a reader that skips nothing and reads the NTSTATUS of every refused call:
      - Run 39899383970: four writers, publishing by hard link or by a move that replaces nothing, and retiring by deletion or by a rename out of the folder, 40 runs each. Retired by a rename, no read was refused, 52,374 reads whichever the publish. Retired by deletion, 1,918 were, every one during that obligation's own deletion (one could not be told from its partial's removal, which by itself refused none), and every open refused was `STATUS_DELETE_PENDING` (883 of 883).
      - Run 39900818487: the two deleting writers again, 40 runs each, each refused entry asked whether it was gone until it was. 2,129 refusals, all `STATUS_DELETE_PENDING`; a first ask found 57 still there, a second found every one gone; at most 4.3 ms after the refused read.
      - Run 39902389694: a rule without the delete-pending question, a refused entry skipped when gone within 10 asks in a row. The test failed 1 of 40 runs (an entry still there, "gone now: false"), and the probe saw one entry need 18 asks (1.4 ms).
      - Run 39903150539: the production writer, 100 runs on four runners, each refusal judged by OWL-425's rule. It caught 263 of 263, all `STATUS_DELETE_PENDING`: the file system said "being deleted" once, for the one entry still listed when asked, which "gone" alone would have missed, and "gone" caught the other 262. The 10-ask rule failed the test 1 of 100 runs again.
      - So the cause is the run deleting its own obligation: Windows keeps the name listed for a moment, refusing every open of it meanwhile, and that moment has no bound a count of asks can hold. No other process is involved, and publication plays no part.
      - What stays: the hard link on every system (above) and OWL-425's rule. A read refused as access denied is skipped when the file system says the entry is being deleted (`owlshift_platform::files::delete_pending`) or, if it says no, when the entry is gone: the deletion has then ended, since a run's id never comes again. A refusal that is neither is reported unreadable.
      - What went, never supported: the move on Windows and its half-second retry, since no read was refused without a deletion and no retry was ever needed. The 10-ask rule replaced the delete-pending question for one run and failed 2 of 140 runs (above), so the question came back on the maintainer's answer on OWL-426 (2026-10-23).
      - Why "it answered yes 0 times in 1,975 asks" (runs 39899383970 and 39900818487) misled: those runs asked it only once the deletion was ending, after a read or a metadata call had taken a moment, and never while an entry stayed listed. Run 39902389694 saw entries stay listed for over a millisecond, and in run 39903150539 the question, asked while one was, answered yes.
      - The test's own diagnostic could not tell any of this, since the skips it checks hide every refusal. The probe and its workflow were temporary, approved by the maintainer on 2026-10-23 in a comment on OWL-426 as the CI-settings floor above requires (that ticket's branch only, its pushes only, read-only), and deleted before the pull request. On the final code, run 39903969102 passed the test 100 of 100 times on four runners, while the probe counted 237 refusals, all `STATUS_DELETE_PENDING` and all caught by the rule.
    - Once the name is typed, `unquarantine` takes the run file of every ticket whose lock it can take afresh from the refs as they are, the dedicated checkout being kept as it is, or removes it when no checkout is left. It then removes the obligations left behind, and the quarantine last, so that a clear cut short leaves the quarantine for a second one. Without that, the next step would find its branch or ref where a run's check never verified it, and quarantine again.
  - *The run file.* Each ticket has `<data>/tickets/<id>/run.json`, written only under its ticket lock, replaced whole (written, synced, renamed) and carrying a format version: a generation, raised at every write of the file, so a run's start, the record of its check and each step of a ref write raise it (decided 2026-10-21, OWL-400: raised only as a run starts, a write of watch's between a sibling's snapshot and its check, the ticket's lock held only for that moment, would read as a breach); the tip of the ticket's branch its last check verified; the ticket-ref value the runner last wrote; and the worktree entry its worktree owns. While a write of the ticket's ref is under way it also holds that write's expected value, set before the ref is updated and cleared once it is: so a ref found at the old value or at the expected one is the runner's, and a write cut off between the two is finished by the next holder of the ticket lock, whichever value the ref then shows. Every write of a ticket's ref goes through it: an admission, a step's, `write_seen` of "Watch after delivery", a person's command. Decided on 2026-10-21 (OWL-400):
    - A move of the ticket's branch that the runner makes outside a run is recorded the same way, with the tip it moves to: the fast-forward of a branch with no commits of its own to the base, and the branch made or moved at its last push before a repair.
    - Each write settles the file first and then checks the ref, so a ref moved by anyone else is found at the next write, not only at the next step's start.
    - A ticket ref is moved and deleted without following a symbolic ref, so a ref a run made an alias is replaced, never its target moved. The check's own listing of the refs carries each ref's target, so a ref that turns symbolic during a run is a change even at the same commit. One found symbolic between runs is refused as unreadable, and `owlshift forget` deletes it. A ticket's branch found symbolic is a breach at the step's start, even at the same commit: its worktree would commit through it into the ref it names. Taking the run files afresh does not accept it.
  - *The check among steps.* At its snapshot a run records each other ticket's generation. `Snapshot::check_among` (build plan, "Rebase runs and parallel runs") reads the run files and the ticket locks twice, before and after it reads the refs and the worktree entries, and takes a ticket as running when its lock is held in either read, and as ended since the snapshot when its generation moved: its `Concurrent` sets, built from the run files. A running ticket's branch, ticket ref and worktree entry are left out; an ended one's branch and ticket ref must be at the values its run file records in both reads (a run file that changed between the two reads is left out, as a branch that ran again is today), and its worktree entry is left out as today's ended runs' are; a ticket that did not run since the snapshot is checked whole against the snapshot, its worktree entry included, as for a run alone. A run file or obligation that cannot be read or does not parse quarantines the project: a lost record never widens what a check leaves out. A sibling's run file found unreadable as a run takes its snapshot is that run's check failing, so the run's outcome is the quarantine.
  - *Starting a step.* Before its first run, under its ticket lock, a step checks that its branch and its ticket ref are at the values its run file records (an expected value counting, as above), and that its kept worktree's entry is the one the run file names (OWL-400); a difference is a breach. This replaces the build plan's earlier rule that nothing touches a run's branch until every run that overlapped it was checked: a sibling that changed an ended ticket's branch or ref is found when that ticket next starts, where the earlier rule held each ticket until the longest sibling run had ended. Its worktree's contents are the run's own to compare, as today's kept worktree is.
  - *Tickets from before.* A ticket ref with no run file, made before P6, gets one the first time a command holds its ticket lock while no obligation of the project stands, taken from the refs as they are: the same baseline today's single-run snapshot takes. A ticket of which nothing exists yet, no ref, no branch and no kept worktree, gets its empty baseline at once, whatever runs (OWL-401): what a run in flight would make of it is found at its next start.
  - *The shared checkout.* While an obligation stands, the runner's fetch is `fetch --no-tags --prune origin`, so no tag is written; `remote set-url` runs only when the address differs; `worktree prune` waits until no obligation stands, since it removes entries a check reads. Live check (f), on 2026-10-21, settled these three rules ([build plan](build-plan.md), "P6 · Parallel"). The fetch's prune is skipped while an obligation stands. A step whose rebase run needs its own entry pruned first answers busy instead, and is tried again.
  - *With a quarantine.* Once it stands, no step pushes or posts a delivery; a push already sent is not taken back (an accepted residual). Decided on 2026-10-21 (OWL-400), with the maintainer:
    - A step that finds it at its next run's boundary, or just before a push, stops as a command refuses a quarantined project. Nothing more runs or is pushed, so neither the pull request nor a delivery comment is posted. The ticket waits where it is and goes on by itself once the quarantine is cleared, as its WAITING comment says (OWL-456).
    - The ticket of the run that broke isolation still parks, as today.
    - Rejected: parking every ticket a sibling's quarantine stops. Each would then need a person's restart after the clear, for no gain in safety.
  - *Its limit*, unchanged: a run that adds a commit to a running sibling's branch, without breaking that branch's history rule, goes unseen.
- A write-in-worktree agent may change any file the sandbox lets it write, the repository's agent configuration included (`.claude/` rules, settings and hooks): on Claude Code no permission mode holds it back, the sandbox and the isolation check bound it ([build plan](build-plan.md#results), OWL-346). A run reads no settings file of the worktree, so a planted setting or hook acts on no later run. Such a change reaches the pull request and acts on whoever checks it out: the review roles and the decider's review are the check there, as for any code an agent writes.
- A run without a valid `result.json` is a failure, whatever its exit code.

**Untrusted input.** On a public repository anyone can open an issue, so admission is what stands between a stranger and a runner:

- A ticket enters the queue only through an admission gesture (delegation, a label or a state) by a person listed in the project config: `[tracker].admitters`, read at the base commit, from P4 (section 5, "The drain"; delegation is not built yet).
- Text from anyone who is not the ticket's decider reaches a brief as quoted data inside a marked block, never as instructions, and cannot answer a gate.
- Follow-ups proposed by agents never reach the ready queue without a human.
- The web tools of the read-only roles. Decided by the maintainer on 2026-10-21 (OWL-415): Design, Design review, Verify, the arbiter and the resolver get their web tools, WebSearch and WebFetch, so that a plan, a review or a decision checks an external fact (a CLI flag, an API's behaviour, a library's version) rather than guessing it or asking the decider, which cost resolver runs and rounds. The runner sets `permissions.network` in their briefs (`Role::takes_web_tools` in the core), and the Claude Code adapter allows the two tools by name, since `dontAsk` denies an offered tool that is not allowed (checked live on 2026-10-22, [build plan](build-plan.md), the Claude Code harness adapter's "Permissions"). Each prompt says that a fetched page is data, never instructions, and that a fact checked on the web is cited with its source and date. Intake keeps none (section 5, "Rejected"), nor does the answer check; Build and rebase keep the briefs they had. The opt-in Codex reviewer keeps Codex's web search off (OWL-316): its brief's `network` changes nothing on Codex.
  - *The accepted risk.* A fetched page can carry instructions into a run. Closing the web tools never closed the network: every run's shell reaches it (the known limits above), so they add no reach for exfiltration, and the runs hold no tracker or forge credential. What such a page could steer is bounded where the roles' output goes: Design's plan is reviewed, the resolver's decisions are reversible and the decider's word overrides them, and findings are confirmed by their author or the arbiter.
  - *The operator's key.* `web_tools = false` in the personal configuration, beside `allow_caches` ([runtime and operations](runtime-and-operations.md#configuration)), turns them off for these five roles on that machine: their briefs then say `network: false`, the adapter removes the two tools, and their prompts say they have no web tools. Absent, they are on.
  - Checking an external premise live stays a declared capability of Intake that P5 does not build (section 5, "Offline in P5"). A Codex run's shell keeps the network, with Codex's own sandbox off inside Owlshift's (section 5, "The Codex harness"); since OWL-347 no role runs on Codex, but the opt-in Codex reviewer (OWL-348). The resolver's Bash reaches the network too (OWL-344, section 4).

**Per-project policy.** Additional always-human categories, the resolver's delegation level and pass limit, plan approval mode, caps, budgets, allowed harnesses per role, the quiet window before resuming.

**Audit.** Every decision the resolver takes is a comment on the ticket; every run records harness, model, effort, cost and result status.

## 9. Usage & routing

Usage is bounded by construction: zero tokens at rest, short contexts for every role except Build, one routing verdict per ticket, and limits expressed in each subscription's own terms.

- **Tiers, not model names.** Roles ask for `deep`, `standard` or `fast`; the config maps each tier to a model per harness. Model generations change every few months, so the product never hardcodes one.
- **One routing verdict per ticket.** Intake decides harness, tier, effort and pipeline variant for that ticket alone, never while looking at a batch: a single view over several tickets tends to spread them across models regardless of their content. The verdict is written on the ticket and a label overrides it.
- **Mixing stacks in four layers:** project default, then per role, then the ticket's verdict, then what the machine has (a developer with only Codex installed).
- **The subscription is the budget.** On a subscription, the binding limit is the provider's usage window. When a CLI reports its limit, the runner records the reset time it gives, pauses that harness until then, and the interrupted run resumes from its checkpoint afterwards. `budget_usd` is a dollar cap on each run of a harness, not on a day's total, whatever the billing: where the harness's CLI takes one (Claude Code does, Codex does not), it stops a single runaway run, while bounding total use is the usage cap's job ([runtime and operations](runtime-and-operations.md#configuration)). What one ticket's runs cost in all, across its commands, is bounded by `policy.ticket_budget_usd` (section 4, "Ticket budget", OWL-378).
- **A reached limit is a normal state.** Each role has a fallback (a Codex reviewer falls back to a Claude reviewer on another model), and the switch is logged on the ticket; since P6 the review roles alone fall back, the authoring roles waiting for the reset (D4; section 5, "Parallel", "Usage limits"); with no fallback left, dispatch waits for the earliest reset and the operator is notified once.
- **Low concurrency by default.** Parallel runs share one subscription, so the default cap on concurrent runs is small and raised deliberately: 2 steps at once per machine (`concurrent_runs`) and 1 ticket in flight per project (`policy.max_in_flight`) from P6 (section 5, "Parallel").
- **Measured, then tuned.** Usage per ticket, stage and harness (tokens, runs, time to limit) goes to the local store and to the delivery report; it is the evidence for future routing changes.

| Role | Default tier | Constraint |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Intake | standard | Reads intent, not only the spec |
| Resolver | deep at `all`, standard below (OWL-342) | The questions, the asking run's output, the ticket; at `all` it weighs trade-offs |
| Answer check | standard | Small context: the questions, the answers, the ticket |
| Design, Build, fix runs | from the routing verdict | Same harness for all three unless the verdict says otherwise, Claude Code in P5 (D4); a fix run repairs a red check after delivery (section 5) |
| Design review, Verify | another model than the author's: `deep`, or `standard` when the author ran on `deep` | Every reviewer on Claude Code since OWL-347, the author's own model when no other is configured, which the report says (section 5, "The quality pipeline"); with `pipeline.codex_reviewer` on, one more reviewer on Codex's `standard`, dropped when Codex cannot serve (OWL-348); every reviewer takes the ticket's effort (OWL-301, 2026-10-15) |
| Arbiter | standard | The author's harness, a fresh run that wrote neither the work nor the findings; run only when `pipeline.findings` is `"arbiter"` (opt-in; `"author"` is the default) |
| Rebase | fast | Escalates to the Build routing on a non-trivial conflict, or after a failed fast run (section 5) |

## 10. Technology choices

A Rust core (runner, adapters, CLI) now, and later a Tauri 2 desktop app for macOS and Windows (Linux for free) that reuses the same core. Decision D2.

**Why Rust for the core.**

- The runner is a long-lived supervisor of child processes (harness CLIs): timeouts, signals, locks, restarts. Rust's type system and the absence of garbage-collector pauses fit that job.
- One static binary per platform, with no Node or Python runtime to install: it matters for an open-source tool installed by strangers.
- The desktop app's backend is Rust too, so the core is shared rather than wrapped.
- Agents will write much of the code; a strict compiler gives them a fast and exact feedback loop.

The costs: slower first iterations than TypeScript, and fewer casual contributors for adapters. The out-of-process adapter protocol (section 6) answers the second: an adapter can be written in any language.

**Why Tauri 2 for the desktop app.** It pairs a Rust backend with a web front end rendered by the system webview: WebView2 on Windows, WKWebView on macOS, WebKitGTK on Linux ([Tauri docs](https://github.com/tauri-apps/tauri-docs/blob/v2/src/content/docs/concept/process-model.md)). It supports a system tray icon and bundling external binaries as sidecars, so the app can ship the runner itself. Installers stay small because no browser engine is bundled.

| Option | For | Against |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Rust core + Tauri 2 app (chosen) | One core for CLI, daemon and app; small installers; tray support | App UI in web technology, not platform widgets |
| Go core + Wails app (Go is Sortie's choice) | Simplest cross-compilation, quick to write | Less expressive types for a policy-heavy core; smaller desktop ecosystem |
| TypeScript core + Electron app | Fastest to write, largest contributor pool | A Node runtime to ship, a heavy app, weaker as a long-running supervisor |
| SwiftUI app + WinUI app over a shared core | Truly native widgets on each platform | Two UI codebases for one maintainer |

**What the app is for.** A tray companion, not a second tracker: how many questions wait for me, answering a gate inline (posted to the ticket through the Writer, so the tracker stays the record), approving a plan next to its diff, runner health and spend. The steps before P11 do not need it: the tracker and notifications are enough.

**Distribution.** Binaries on GitHub Releases, Homebrew, winget and `cargo install`; signed installers once the app exists (Apple notarisation and Windows code signing carry a yearly cost to plan).

**Platforms and Docker.** A native binary on every developer machine; Windows through WSL2 first; Docker only as the server distribution and as an optional per-run sandbox. Reasons, service lifecycle, updates, configuration, observability and tests are in [runtime & operations](runtime-and-operations.md).

## 11. Scope & milestones

Twelve small steps, P0 to P11, each shippable and usable on its own; the full list, exit gates and scenario coverage are in the [roadmap](roadmap.md). P2, the question loop on the ticket, is the bet; parallelism waits until P6 because a serial drain cannot collide.

Owlshift works on its own backlog, in a dedicated Linear workspace, from P1; the first external adopter is Locary (Linear, GitHub, a Django and Next.js stack) from P5, whose Linear team already has a Needs Input state and Triage enabled since 2026-09-21. The second project for P9 is still to be chosen (decision D6); without it, neutrality stays a claim.

**Out of scope for now:** merging automatically, a hosted service, a board or UI competing with the tracker, version control other than git, running third-party adapters in-process, telemetry of any kind.

## 12. Decisions

D1, D2, D3, D4, D5, D7, D8, D9, D10, D11, D13 and D14 are agreed; two decisions remain open, neither of them blocking P0. Also settled: open source under Apache-2.0, repository content in English, tracker, forge and harness as adapters.

| # | Decision | Outcome or recommendation | Status |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D1 | Build from scratch or extend Sortie | Build Owlshift from scratch (decided 2026-09-27): Sortie is too small a base to be worth extending, and the three bets would rewrite its core anyway. Its `WORKFLOW.md` format and adapter pitfalls stay worth reading | Agreed |
| D2 | Core language and desktop stack | A native Rust binary on every machine; a Tauri 2 app in P11; Docker only for servers and sandboxing | Agreed |
| D3 | Product name | Owlshift, hosted on the maintainer's personal GitHub account; `owlshift.dev` not registered according to the `.dev` registry (2026-09-28, [check C6](build-plan.md#results)) | Agreed |
| D4 | First adapter set (P1 to P5) | Tracker: Linear. Forge: GitHub. Harnesses: Claude Code for building and reviewing (agreed 2026-10-15, OWL-296: in P5 the authoring roles stay on Claude Code whatever the routing verdict. The review roles ran on Codex, on Claude Code where Codex was not usable, until the maintainer decided on 2026-10-18, OWL-347, that they run on Claude Code only, so that no second subscription is required; the Codex adapter stays for a reviewer of another family as an opt-in complement, OWL-348). From P6, a declared fallback harness takes over the review roles only, never the authoring roles (OWL-398, section 5, "Parallel") | Agreed |
| D5 | Config format | TOML (decided 2026-09-27): a product-owned `owlshift.toml` for pipeline, routing and policy; importing a Symphony-style `WORKFLOW.md` stays an optional later addition | Agreed |
| D6 | Second validation project for P9 | A project on another stack, ideally on GitHub Issues; to name | Open |
| D7 | Default runner location | The developer's machine; a team server is a P10 mode | Agreed |
| D8 | Agent identity on Linear from P2 | A Linear app user (decided 2026-10-04, OWL-141, once [check C4](build-plan.md#results) confirmed it works with polling and no public endpoint): an OAuth app the workspace creates with client credentials, no webhook and no mention or delegation scope, whose token the runner requests for each run. In the check, Linear notified the decider of its comment and of its mention of them, and not of a comment posted through their own key; a decider not subscribed to the issue is reached by a mention. The desktop notification stays the operator's channel on their machine. Built in OWL-157, and optional: with the app stored, every comment and stage move comes from its user, the questions, re-asks and replies mention the decider, and an app that cannot be used refuses the run rather than fall back; without it, Owlshift writes through the personal API key and `owlshift doctor` warns | Agreed |
| D9 | Windows scope | WSL2 from P1; native Windows only when a user needs it. Decided 2026-09-29: native Windows confinement is set aside; a confined run is refused there and WSL2 is the supported path. The live checks (OWL-72: a plain AppContainer cannot run git, a restricted token was compared, [build plan](build-plan.md#results)) are the starting point if native Windows comes back; the `owlshift-launch` launcher that OWL-71 built for it was removed (2026-09-30, OWL-89) | Agreed |
| D10 | Contribution terms | Developer Certificate of Origin sign-off on every commit, no CLA (decided 2026-09-28): light for contributors, authorship still traced; see `CONTRIBUTING.md` | Agreed |
| D11 | Where the code lives | `~/Projects/owlshift`, on the personal GitHub account (`pitchopp/owlshift`), no dedicated organisation | Agreed |
| D12 | Minimum supported systems | Set at the first release: the two latest macOS versions, the current Ubuntu LTS, Windows 11 with WSL2 | Open |
| D13 | GitHub identity from P5 | Owlshift's own GitHub App, whose user token `owlshift init` gets through the device flow (decided on 2026-10-17 with the maintainer, OWL-336; checked live on 2026-10-19, [checks C9 to C15](build-plan.md#results)). Owned by the maintainer's account and public, so any account can install it: the app `owl-shift`, client ID `Iv23li5EhBqiuljIPbiK`, registered by the maintainer on 2026-10-19, the app the checks ran on. A move to an organisation keeps its client ID: assumed, unverified, since no check of C9 to C17 transferred the app. Permissions: Pull requests (write); Contents (read, then read and write from 2026-10-23 for D14's merge, [check C19](build-plan.md#results)); Checks, Commit statuses, Actions and Metadata (read); no Issues, no webhook. The push stays on the user's own git credentials. Two constants in the binary, the client ID and the slug, each with a personal-file override for a fork or a private app (`github_app_client_id`, `github_app_slug`); the slug is the source of the install link `https://github.com/apps/<slug>/installations/new`, since it cannot be derived from the client ID without the app's private key. Neither a client secret nor a private key is generated, and none sits on a user's machine. User-token expiration is on, branch R of C13 (2026-10-19): the Writer refreshes under the account lock, not the project lock (section 8, "The GitHub App's tokens"). This app is the production one and GA1's named input: it keeps the settings the checks proved, user-token expiration turned on again after C14 ran with it off, and GA6 only verifies it. A personal token through `init --github-token` stays the expert option, and the app's token wins when both are stored ([build plan](build-plan.md#github-app-sign-in-owl-336)) | Agreed |
| D14 | Merge on green | A project may let the runner merge the pull request it follows once green (decided on 2026-10-23 with the maintainer, [check C19](build-plan.md#results)): `[forge] merge` = `never` (default), `labeled` (tracker label `owlshift:merge`) or `all`, read at the base branch's head; `merge_method` `squash` by default. The Writer merges through the REST merge endpoint with the head Owlshift last pushed as the expected `sha`, only after two green reads on that head and base, nothing due, the stage still review and no question under a human category, then a last read right before the call (open, same head and base, not a draft, a review decision that clears; OWL-446); a refusal that will not pass is notified once per head. No agent merges; the floor checks the grant. GitHub's native auto-merge rejected: not available on every repository. The App asks Contents (read and write) for it. Section 5, "Merge on green" | Agreed |

**Name.** Owlshift, chosen on 2026-09-27: an owl working the night shift on the backlog, with the questions waiting at breakfast. On that date `owlshift` was free on crates.io and npm; the GitHub handle `owlshift` is taken. Its look and voice, the logo, colours, typefaces and tone, are set in [visual identity](visual-identity.md) (OWL-162).
